Prometheus — Complete PCA Guide

From Zero to Prometheus Certified Associate (PCA)

A comprehensive, highly-illustrated guide covering Prometheus monitoring, PromQL, instrumentation, alerting, Grafana dashboards, and PCA certification preparation — using anihpj/jobpost Django project as your hands-on learning lab.

🔥 Prometheus PromQL Grafana Alertmanager Observability Monitoring Time-Series 🟢 Beginner-Friendly

🔥 Welcome to the Prometheus PCA Complete Learning Guide!
Aligned with the official Prometheus Certified Associate (PCA) exam syllabus from the Linux Foundation. From observability fundamentals to advanced PromQL — everything you need using anihpj/jobpost as your real-world Django example.

🔥 What Is Prometheus? — One Sentence

Prometheus is an open-source monitoring and alerting toolkit that collects numeric metrics from your applications, stores them as time-series data, lets you query and analyze them with PromQL, and fires alerts when things go wrong. It was created at SoundCloud in 2012, inspired by Google's Borgmon, and is now a CNCF graduated project — the second project ever to graduate (after Kubernetes).

How Prometheus Works — The Big Picture

🔄 PROMETHEUS DATA FLOW — From App to Alert
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │                         HOW PROMETHEUS WORKS                              │  │                                                                           │  │  1. Your app exposes metrics at /metrics (Prometheus format)             │  │     ┌──────────┐                                                         │  │     │ anihpj   │  :8000/metrics → "http_requests_total{status="200"} 1234"│  │     │ Django   │                                                         │  │     └──────────┘                                                         │  │           │                                                               │  │  2. Prometheus SCRAPES (pulls) /metrics every 15 seconds                 │  │           ▼                                                               │  │     ┌──────────┐                                                         │  │     │Prometheus│  Stores in TSDB → 2-hour blocks → 15-day retention      │  │     │  :9090   │                                                         │  │     └────┬─────┘                                                         │  │          │                                                                │  │  3. You QUERY with PromQL                                                │  │          ▼                                                                │  │     rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj"}[5m])  ← per-second rate       │  │          │                                                                │  │  4. ALERT when thresholds are crossed                                    │  │          ▼                                                                │  │     "Error rate > 5% for 5 minutes → page on-call"                      │  │          │                                                                │  │  5. VISUALIZE in Grafana                                                 │  │          ▼                                                                │  │     📈 RED Dashboard: Rate, Errors, Duration panels                      │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key differentiator: Unlike most monitoring tools that PUSH data to a central server, Prometheus PULLS — it visits each application's /metrics endpoint on a schedule. This means your app doesn't need to know about Prometheus; it just exposes metrics. Prometheus handles discovery, scraping, storing, and querying.

❓ Why Monitor anihpj with Prometheus?

❌ Without Prometheus
  • Users report errors before you know about them
  • "Is it slow?" → guesswork, no data
  • Debugging = SSH into servers, tail logs manually
  • No historical data to compare "normal vs now"
  • Scaling decisions based on feelings, not metrics
  • Outages discovered when customers complain on Twitter
✅ With Prometheus + Grafana
  • Detect errors in real-time before users notice (alerting)
  • P99 latency graphs show EXACTLY when/why it's slow
  • Query 30 days of metrics to compare week-over-week
  • Scale based on actual CPU/memory/request trends
  • Dashboards show health at a glance — no SSH needed
  • Alertmanager pages on-call within 2 minutes of outage

For PCA exam: Understand WHY monitoring matters. The exam's Observability domain (18%) tests concepts like: why pull beats push, what SLIs/SLOs/SLAs are, and how metrics differ from logs and traces.

📋 What You Need Before Starting

🌐
Basic Web / DevOps Knowledge

You should understand what a web server does, what an API endpoint is, and basic networking concepts (ports, HTTP). Docker familiarity is a plus but not required.

💻
Terminal + Docker Access

A machine with Docker installed (4GB+ RAM recommended). All anihpj examples run locally via Docker Compose. No cloud account needed.

🐍
Python/Django Basics (Optional)

The anihpj Django project is used for examples, but you don't need to be a Django expert. All code is explained. If you know Python basics, you're golden.

🧠
Curiosity + Persistence

Monitoring concepts take time to click. The Novice's Corners explain everything in plain English. If something doesn't make sense, re-read the Novice's Corner for that chapter.

🗺️ Master Summary & Roadmap

Your Journey to PCA Certification

This guide maps directly to the PCA (Prometheus Certified Associate) exam syllabus. Every chapter, diagram, and practice question builds your understanding systematically — from observability fundamentals to advanced PromQL. We use anihpj/jobpost throughout as our hands-on Django example.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~40 hours📊 16 Chapters🎯 PCA Ready

🧠 How Prometheus Thinks — Pull Model vs Push Model

Unlike most monitoring tools (Graphite, InfluxDB, Datadog agent) where applications push metrics to a central server, Prometheus uses a pull model: it visits each target's /metrics endpoint on a schedule (default: every 15 seconds).

PUSH Model (Graphite, Datadog)
  • App must know WHERE to send metrics
  • If server is down, metrics are lost
  • Hard to know if app is actually healthy ("it's pushing but is it working?")
  • Firewall: app needs egress to monitoring server
PULL Model (Prometheus)
  • App just exposes /metrics — no config needed
  • If Prometheus is down, metrics are still generated (just not collected temporarily)
  • UP/DOWN detection built-in: if /metrics doesn't respond, target is DOWN
  • Firewall-friendly: Prometheus initiates connection

PCA Exam Note: The exception is the PushGateway — used ONLY for batch/cron jobs that end before Prometheus can scrape them. Never use PushGateway for long-running services!

🏗️ Architecture Overview — The 5 Components

Prometheus Server has 5 internal components that work together. Know these for the PCA exam:

🔍
① Service Discovery

Finds WHAT to monitor. On K8s, auto-discovers pods. Types: kubernetes, file, consul, ec2, dns, static.

📥
② Retrieval (Scraper)

Pulls /metrics from discovered targets every scrape_interval. Handles HTTP requests + parsing.

💾
③ TSDB (Storage)

Stores time-series data in 2-hour blocks on disk. Default retention: 15 days. Compresses efficiently.

🌐
④ HTTP Server

Serves PromQL queries via REST API (:9090). Also serves the Expression Browser UI at /graph.

⚙️
⑤ Rules Engine

Evaluates recording rules (pre-compute) and alerting rules (fire alerts) every evaluation_interval.

External components: Alertmanager (routes alerts), Grafana (visualizes), Exporters (translate app metrics), PushGateway (batch jobs).

📊 Time-Series Data Explained

A time series = metric name + labels + sequence of (timestamp, value) pairs. Each unique label combination is a separate time series. Prometheus stores data in compressed 2-hour TSDB blocks on disk with 15-day default retention.

Anatomy of a Time Series
# HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests
# TYPE http_requests_total counter
http_requests_total{method="GET", endpoint="/jobs/", status="200"} 1234 1717800000
http_requests_total{method="GET", endpoint="/jobs/", status="500"} 5 1717800000
http_requests_total{method="POST", endpoint="/jobs/", status="201"} 89 1717800000
#                ↑                    ↑                                      ↑
#           metric name            labels                              [timestamp]
# Each line = one data point in a time series. Each unique {labels} = separate series.

🔄 The Monitoring Loop — Collect → Store → Query → Alert → Visualize

Prometheus operates in a continuous 5-stage loop. Understanding this flow is essential for the PCA exam:

📥
Collect

Prometheus scrapes /metrics from targets every 15s. Pull model. Service Discovery finds targets automatically.

💾
Store

TSDB compresses data into 2-hour blocks. 15-day default retention. Remote Write for long-term.

🔍
Query

PromQL at :9090. Instant + range queries. Expression browser for ad-hoc exploration.

🚨
Alert

Rules engine evaluates conditions. Alerts → Alertmanager → PagerDuty/Slack/Email.

📊
Visualize

Grafana dashboards with RED panels, variables, and alert annotations.

🎯 PCA Exam Strategy & Preparation

Master the PCA Exam — Complete Strategy Guide

The PCA exam is 60 multiple-choice questions in 2 hours. Passing score: 75% (45/60). This section gives you the complete strategy to maximize your score — from exam format to time management, key topics, lab setup, and scoring tips.

📋 PCA Exam Format

AttributeDetail
Format60 multiple-choice questions (single answer)
Duration2 hours (120 minutes)
Passing Score75% (45 out of 60 correct)
DeliveryOnline, proctored (PSI Exams)
Hands-on?NO — no terminal, no live systems. All theory + query reading.
Allowed ResourcesNone — closed book. No browser tabs. No notes.
Retake PolicyOne free retake included with registration
Certification Validity3 years from passing date

⏱️ Time Management — The 3-Pass Strategy

PassTimeWhat You Do
Pass 1: Easy WinsFirst 60 minAnswer questions you know IMMEDIATELY. Skip PromQL-heavy Qs. Mark uncertain ones. Aim for 35-40 done.
Pass 2: ThinkersNext 40 minReturn to marked questions. This is where you tackle PromQL (Q24-40). Read carefully. Eliminate wrong answers.
Pass 3: ReviewFinal 20 minReview ALL answers once. Check for silly mistakes (= vs =~, forgetting [5m]). Answer every question — no penalty for guessing!
💡 Golden Rule: Never spend more than 2 minutes on a single question. If stuck, mark it and move on. An unanswered question = 0 points. A guessed question = 25% chance. Guess, mark, return later.

🔑 Key Topics to Master — Highest ROI for PCA Exam

PriorityTopicWhy CriticalStudy
🥇rate() and irate()MOST tested function. Know exactly when to use each.Ch 6-7
🥇histogram_quantile()Full pipeline: bucket→rate→sum by(le)→quantile. Exam favorite.Ch 7-8
🥇4 metric typesCounter, Gauge, Histogram, Summary. Know use cases + code.Ch 2,9
🥈Alerting rule anatomy5 fields: alert, expr, for, labels, annotations.Ch 11
🥈prometheus.yml sectionsglobal, scrape_configs, rule_files, alerting.Ch 3
🥈Alertmanager 5 functionsDedup → Group → Route → Inhibit → Silence. Guaranteed question.Ch 12
🥉Relabeling (before vs after)relabel_configs vs metric_relabel_configs.Ch 4
🥉Recording vs Alerting rulesrecord: vs alert:. Different purposes.Ch 13
🥉CardinalityWhat it is, why it's dangerous, how to avoid.Ch 2,9

⚠️ Top 10 PCA Exam Mistakes to Avoid

  • ❌ #1: Using rate() on a Gauge — returns garbage. rate() is ONLY for Counters!
  • ❌ #2: Applying histogram_quantile() to raw buckets without rate() first.
  • ❌ #3: Forgetting the [5m] in rate(metric[5m]) — causes syntax error.
  • ❌ #4: Confusing = (exact) with =~ (regex). NOT ==~!
  • ❌ #5: Using sum() on histograms without by(le) — destroys bucket boundaries.
  • ❌ #6: Confusing sum by(X) (keep X) with sum without(X) (drop X).
  • ❌ #7: Forgetting for: in alerting rules — fires on every transient spike.
  • ❌ #8: Using user IDs/emails as labels — explodes cardinality.
  • ❌ #9: Confusing recording rules (record:) with alerting rules (alert:).
  • ❌ #10: Not knowing that Grafana queries Prometheus — it does NOT store metrics.

📚 Exam Day Setup & Checklist

WhenAction
Night BeforeReview PromQL Cheat Sheet (Appendix A), Gotcha blocks, sleep 8 hours. No late-night cramming.
Morning OfLight breakfast, hydrate. Quick skim of Key Topics table (es-key-topics above). Mental warm-up: write rate(metric[5m]) from memory.
30 min BeforeSet up exam space: quiet room, cleared desk, webcam ready. Run PSI system test. Have government ID ready.
Start of ExamRead ALL 60 questions once (5 min scan). Mark easy ones. Note domain distribution mentally.
During Exam3-pass strategy. DO NOT open browser tabs — proctor will flag you. No phone, no notes on desk.
After ExamResults shown immediately. Save/print your score report. If you pass, certification appears in Linux Foundation portal within 24-48 hours.

🖥️ Hands-on Lab Setup — Practice PromQL in 5 Minutes

Quick PromQL Practice Environment
# Start Prometheus with self-monitoring (no external targets needed)
docker run -d --name prom-test -p 9090:9090 prom/prometheus

# Open http://localhost:9090/graph
# Try these queries against Prometheus' own metrics:
#   prometheus_tsdb_head_samples_appended_total
#   rate(prometheus_http_requests_total[5m])
#   rate(prometheus_http_requests_total{code="200"}[5m]) / rate(prometheus_http_requests_total[5m])

# For full anihpj stack (Django + all exporters):
cd anihpj && docker compose up -d   # From Appendix E
# Then explore: http://localhost:9090/targets

# Stop when done:
docker stop prom-test && docker rm prom-test
💡 PCA exam has NO hands-on terminal, but practicing PromQL in a real Prometheus instance will make the concepts stick much faster than just reading. Even 30 minutes of hands-on querying is worth 3 hours of reading.

✅ Scoring & Passing Tips

✅
Passing: 45/60 (75%)

No penalty for wrong answers — answer every question. If you eliminate 2 wrong answers, your guess is 50/50. Always guess if unsure.

⚠️
Watch the Clock

60 questions ÷ 120 minutes = 2 min each. But PromQL questions (Q24-40) need more time. Save time on easy Qs to budget for PromQL.

🎯 Target Strategy: Aim for 50+/60 (83%+) on practice tests. The real exam has some unfamiliar questions — a buffer above 75% gives you safety margin. If you consistently score 80%+ on this guide's 190 questions, you're ready.

Question Pattern Recognition — 6 PCA Question Types

PatternExampleHow to Answer
"Which PromQL returns...""Which query shows p99 latency per endpoint?"Look for histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m]))
"How to configure...""Where do you specify scrape targets?"Answer: scrape_configs in prometheus.yml
"What does this alert do..."Given a YAML alert rule, what does it detect?Read the expr — it tells you the condition
"Choose the metric type...""You need to count HTTP requests. Use?"Counter for cumulative, Gauge for up/down, Histogram for distribution
"What is the purpose of...""What is Alertmanager's inhibition feature?"Recall the 5 functions: Dedup→Group→Route→Inhibit→Silence
"Identify the error...""Why won't this alert rule work?"Check: missing [5m] in rate(), = vs =~, missing for:, cardinality bombs

Chapter 1: What is Observability? 🟢 Beginner

Seeing Your System Clearly — Beyond "Is It Up?" on Kubernetes

Observability is the ability to understand what's happening inside your system by looking at its external outputs. anihpj/jobpost runs as Django pods on a Kubernetes cluster — pods come and go, services auto-scale, and nodes can fail. In this dynamic K8s world, you can't SSH into a pod (it might be gone tomorrow). You need metrics, logs, and traces to understand your system. This chapter builds the observability foundation for everything else in Prometheus on Kubernetes.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📊 PCA 18% ☸️ K8s-Native 🎯 Foundation

🎯 What You'll Learn in This Chapter

  • ✅ The Three Pillars of Observability — Metrics, Logs, and Traces — and which tool solves each
  • ✅ The critical difference between Monitoring (known unknowns) and Observability (unknown unknowns)
  • ✅ How anihpj/jobpost benefits from each pillar with real production scenarios
  • ✅ RED Method (Rate, Errors, Duration) for application health monitoring
  • ✅ USE Method (Utilization, Saturation, Errors) for infrastructure health
  • ✅ The Four Golden Signals (Latency, Traffic, Errors, Saturation) from Google SRE
  • ✅ The SLI → SLO → SLA reliability stack and how to apply it
  • ✅ When Prometheus is the RIGHT tool — and when it absolutely is NOT
🎓 PCA Relevance
Observability Concepts 18% Foundation

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Observability in Plain English

If you're completely new to observability, here's the simplest way to understand it:

1. Monitoring = Checking known problems. Like a car dashboard — you have a fuel gauge, speedometer, and check-engine light. You know exactly what to watch. If the fuel is low, the gauge shows it. But what if your car has a problem you never anticipated? The dashboard can't help.

2. Observability = Asking NEW questions. Like having a full telemetry system on a race car — you can ask "Why did the engine temperature spike at Turn 3?" even though nobody predicted that exact scenario. You have enough data (metrics + logs + traces) to investigate ANY question, not just the ones you thought of in advance.

3. Prometheus handles the "Metrics" pillar. It's AMAZING at collecting numbers over time (CPU at 78%, 245 requests/second, p99 latency = 340ms). It does NOT handle logs (use Loki) or traces (use Tempo/Jaeger). The PCA exam will test you on knowing which tool solves which problem!

4. For anihpj on Kubernetes: Your Django pods are ephemeral — kubelet can reschedule them to any node at any time. Monitoring tells you "the jobpost API pod is returning 5% errors." Observability lets you ask "WHY did the error rate jump from 0.1% to 5% at 2:14 PM?" — then trace through metrics (Prometheus), logs (Loki, kubectl logs), AND traces (Tempo) to find the exact database query. On K8s, you also need to monitor the platform itself: node health, pod restarts, etcd latency, API server request rates — all exposed as Prometheus metrics via kube-state-metrics and kubelet.

PCA Exam Focus — The 3 Pillars Question

The PCA exam WILL ask you to match observability pillars to their tools and use cases. Memorize this: Metrics → Prometheus (WHAT/aggregates), Logs → Loki/ELK (WHY/details), Traces → Tempo/Jaeger (WHERE/time). A classic exam trap: suggesting Prometheus for log analysis. Don't fall for it!

🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 1 Must-Knows
📊 Three PillarsMetrics→Prometheus, Logs→Loki, Traces→Tempo. Exam asks: "Which tool for [scenario]?"
🔴 RED MethodRate, Errors, Duration — application endpoints. Exam asks: "Which method for API monitoring?"
🔵 USE MethodUtil, Satur, Errors — infrastructure. Exam asks: "Which method for CPU monitoring?"
📏 SLI/SLO/SLAIndicator (measure) → Objective (target) → Agreement (contract). SLO must be STRICTER than SLA.
🧮 Error Budget= 1 − SLO. 99.9% = 43 min/month. Exam asks: "Calculate error budget for 99.95% SLO."
❌ NOT PrometheusLogs, traces, billing, SQL JOINs, push model (except PushGateway). Most common exam trap!

1.1 The Three Pillars of Observability

Modern systems like anihpj produce massive amounts of data. That data falls into three categories — the Three Pillars of Observability. Each pillar answers a different question about your system's health:

OBSERVABILITY — THREE PILLARS COMPARISON
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        OBSERVABILITY — THREE PILLARS                          │
  ├───────────────────┬───────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                   │                   │                                        │
  │   📊 METRICS      │   📝 LOGS         │   🔍 TRACES                            │
  │   (Numbers)       │   (Text)          │   (Journey)                            │
  │                   │                   │                                        │
  │ CPU:     78%      │ [ERROR] DB        │ Request Timeline:                      │
  │ RAM:     4.2GB    │ connection        │                                        │
  │ Req/s:   245      │ refused at        │ Client ──10ms──▶ Nginx                 │
  │ Errors:  3/min    │ 14:32:01 UTC      │         ──50ms──▶ Auth Service         │
  │ p99:     340ms    │                   │         ──200ms─▶ DB Query             │
  │                   │ [WARN] Cache      │         ──90ms──▶ Render               │
  │ Stored in:        │ miss for key      │         ──10ms──▶ Response             │
  │ Time-Series DB    │ "jobpost:12"      │                                        │
  │                   │                   │ Stored in:                             │
  │ ❓ Answers:        │ Stored in:        │ Span trees with duration               │
  │ "HOW MUCH?"       │ Text files /      │                                        │
  │ "HOW FAST?"       │ Loki / ELK        │ ❓ Answers:                             │
  │                   │                   │ "WHERE did time go?"                   │
  │ 🔧 Prometheus     │ ❓ Answers:        │ "WHICH service is slow?"               │
  │                   │ "WHAT happened?"  │                                        │
  │                   │ "WHY did it fail?"│ 🔧 Tempo / Jaeger                      │
  │                   │ 🔧 Loki / ELK     │                                        │
  └───────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘
📊
Metrics — The "WHAT"

Numeric measurements collected over time. CPU at 78%, 245 requests/second, p99 latency = 340ms. Stored in time-series databases like Prometheus. Metrics are aggregated — you see the big picture, not individual events. Perfect for dashboards and alerting.

📝
Logs — The "WHY"

Timestamped text records of discrete events. "[ERROR] DB connection refused at 14:32:01 UTC." Each log line is one event. Logs provide detail — you see the exact error message, stack trace, and context. Stored in Loki, Elasticsearch, or flat files.

🔍
Traces — The "WHERE"

Follow a single request through multiple services. Shows each step: Nginx (10ms) → Auth (50ms) → DB Query (200ms) → Render (90ms) = 350ms total. Traces reveal which service is the bottleneck. Stored in Tempo or Jaeger as span trees.

🧩
How They Work TOGETHER

Metrics alert you to a problem (error rate spiked at 2:14 PM). Traces show you WHERE the time went (DB query took 800ms instead of 50ms). Logs tell you WHY (connection pool exhausted). Together = full observability.

1.2 Monitoring vs. Observability — What's the Difference?

This distinction is tested on the PCA exam. Many engineers use these terms interchangeably — but they mean fundamentally different things:

Dimension🔍 Monitoring🔭 Observability
DefinitionWatching KNOWN failure modesAbility to ask NEW questions
Scope"I know these 5 things can fail""I can investigate ANY behavior"
SetupPre-configured dashboards & alertsRich telemetry enables ad-hoc exploration
Questions"Is CPU > 80%? Is disk full?""Why did latency spike at 2:14 PM?"
DataPre-defined metrics onlyMetrics + Logs + Traces combined
MindsetReactive — respond to alertsProactive — explore & understand
anihpj ExampleAlert: "jobpost error rate > 5%"Investigate: "Which endpoint? Which DB query? Since when?"
💡 The Best Analogy

Monitoring is like a car's dashboard — fuel gauge, speedometer, check-engine light. You know exactly what to watch. Observability is like a flight data recorder (black box) — after any incident, you can replay EVERYTHING and ask questions nobody anticipated. Prometheus gives you the dashboard. Metrics + Logs + Traces together give you the black box.

1.3 Why anihpj Needs Observability

Let's make this concrete. anihpj is a Django job board with a jobpost app. Employers post jobs, candidates search and apply. Here are 4 real production scenarios where observability saves the day:

🐌
Scenario 1: The Slow API

Problem: Users complain the /api/jobs/ endpoint is slow. Without observability: Guess — restart the server, add more workers, hope it helps. With observability: Metrics show p99 latency jumped from 50ms to 800ms at 2:14 PM. Traces show the PostgreSQL query now takes 780ms (was 20ms). Logs show a missing index warning. Fix: Add the missing database index. Problem solved in minutes, not hours.

💥
Scenario 2: The Midnight Crash

Problem: anihpj goes down at 3 AM. Without observability: A user tweets about it. You wake up 2 hours later, no idea what happened. With observability: Alertmanager pages you at 3:01 AM. Metrics show memory climbing steadily for 6 hours (memory leak). Logs show the OOM killer fired at 3:00:14 AM. Traces point to a specific jobpost search query that allocates but never frees memory.

📉
Scenario 3: The Silent Drop

Problem: New job applications dropped 40% this week. Without observability: Nobody notices for days. Revenue impact unknown. With observability: Business metrics dashboard shows the drop immediately. Traces reveal the "Apply" button POST request fails with a 500 error. Logs show a validation error from last week's code deployment. Fix: Rollback the deployment. Applications recover within the hour.

🔐
Scenario 4: The Cache Eviction Storm

Problem: Redis cache hit rate drops from 95% to 15%. Without observability: Database load spikes, everything slows down, nobody knows why. With observability: Redis exporter metrics show eviction rate jumped 100x. Node metrics show memory pressure. Logs show Redis maxmemory limit reached. Fix: Increase Redis memory allocation or tune eviction policy.

😰 anihpj WITHOUT Observability
  • 🔴 "The site is down!" — you find out from a user tweet
  • 🔴 "Is it slow?" — guess based on "feeling" or manual curl tests
  • 🔴 "When did this start?" — no history, no way to compare
  • 🔴 "Should we add more servers?" — guesswork, often too late
  • 🔴 "Which part is broken?" — SSH into every server, grep logs manually
  • 🔴 Mean Time To Resolution (MTTR): hours to days
🎉 anihpj WITH Full Observability (Prometheus + Loki + Tempo)
  • ✅ Prometheus detects error rate spike BEFORE users notice
  • ✅ PromQL query shows p99 latency jumped from 50ms → 800ms at exactly 2:14 PM
  • ✅ Prometheus stores every data point — compare now vs yesterday vs last week
  • ✅ Metrics-driven auto-scaling adds capacity BEFORE users feel pain
  • ✅ Traces pinpoint the exact slow database query in seconds
  • ✅ Mean Time To Resolution (MTTR): minutes
🏭 The Factory Floor Analogy — How Prometheus Monitoring Works

Think of your Kubernetes cluster running anihpj as a factory floor. Each machine (pod, database, cache) has sensors. Prometheus is the central monitoring room that sends inspectors to read every sensor on a schedule.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🏭 THE FACTORY FLOOR — PROMETHEUS MONITORING ANALOGY                │
  │                                                                                        │
  │  📊 WITHOUT PROMETHEUS (No Monitoring)                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Machines run blind. "Is the stamping press working?" — Nobody knows.            │  │
  │  │  "When did the conveyor belt slow down?" — No record.                            │  │
  │  │  "Which machine caused the production halt?" — Guesswork, hours of inspection.   │  │
  │  │  Result: Production stops → Customer tweets → You find out HOURS later.          │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                                        │
  │  🔥 WITH PROMETHEUS (Full Factory Monitoring)                                           │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                                                                                 │  │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │  │
  │  │  │              🏢 CENTRAL MONITORING ROOM (Prometheus Server)               │    │  │
  │  │  │                                                                         │    │  │
  │  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                │    │  │
  │  │  │  │ SCRAPER  │  │   TSDB   │  │  RULES   │  │   HTTP   │                │    │  │
  │  │  │  │  📋       │  │  🗄️      │  │  ⚙️       │  │  🌐      │                │    │  │
  │  │  │  │ Inspector │  │ Archive  │  │  Alarm   │  │ Dashboard│                │    │  │
  │  │  │  │ visits    │  │ 2-hr     │  │  Rules   │  │  Server  │                │    │  │
  │  │  │  │ every 15s │  │ cabinets │  │          │  │  :9090   │                │    │  │
  │  │  │  └─────┬─────┘  └─────┬────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘                │    │  │
  │  │  └────────┼──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────┘    │  │
  │  │           │              │            │              │                            │  │
  │  │           ▼              │            ▼              ▼                            │  │
  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │  │
  │  │  │                        FACTORY FLOOR MACHINES                           │     │  │
  │  │  │                                                                        │     │  │
  │  │  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐         │     │  │
  │  │  │  │  🖥️ anihpj Django │  │  🗄️ PostgreSQL   │  │  ⚡ Redis Cache  │         │     │  │
  │  │  │  │  :8000/metrics  │  │  :9187/metrics  │  │  :9121/metrics  │         │     │  │
  │  │  │  │                 │  │                 │  │                 │         │     │  │
  │  │  │  │ 📏 Gauge:       │  │ 📈 Counter:     │  │ 📈 Counter:     │         │     │  │

  │  │  │  │ 📈 Counter:     │  │ 📏 Gauge:       │  │ 📏 Gauge:       │         │     │  │
  │  │  │  │  HTTP Requests  │  │  Connections    │  │  Memory Used    │         │     │  │

  │  │  │                                                                        │     │  │

  │  │  │  │  🖥️ Node Exporter │  │  🌐 Blackbox    │                              │     │  │
  │  │  │  │  :9100/metrics  │  │  :9115/metrics  │                              │     │  │
  │  │  │  │                 │  │                 │                              │     │  │
  │  │  │  │ 📏 Gauge:       │  │ 📏 Gauge:       │                              │     │  │
  │  │  │  │  CPU, Memory,   │  │  Is anihpj.com  │                              │     │  │
  │  │  │  │  Disk, Network  │  │  reachable?     │                              │     │  │
  │  │  │  └─────────────────┘  └─────────────────┘                              │     │  │
  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │  │
  │  │                                                                                 │  │
  │  │  🔄 THE LOOP (repeats every 15 seconds = scrape_interval):                       │  │
  │  │  1️⃣ Scraper visits each machine → reads /metrics → stores in TSDB               │  │
  │  │  2️⃣ Rules Engine checks: "error rate > 5%?" → if yes, fires alert                │  │
  │  │  3️⃣ Alert → Alertmanager → dedup → group → route → Slack/PagerDuty              │  │
  │  │  4️⃣ You open Grafana → queries TSDB via PromQL → sees dashboard                  │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                                        │
  │  🎯 KEY INSIGHT: Prometheus doesn't wait for machines to report problems — it          │
  │     ACTIVELY visits every machine every 15 seconds. This is the PULL model:            │
  │     "If a machine doesn't respond, Prometheus KNOWS it's down."                        │
  │     Push model (machines send data): "If data stops arriving, is machine down          │
  │     or network broken? Hard to tell."                                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🧑‍🏫 Novice's Takeaway: Think of Prometheus as the factory monitoring center — it doesn't trust machines to self-report. It sends an inspector (scraper) to check every machine every 15 seconds. If a machine is broken, Prometheus KNOWS because the inspector couldn't read its sensors. This pull model is why Prometheus is so reliable during outages — it doesn't depend on broken machines to call for help.

1.4 Beyond Metrics — Logs, Events, and Traces (PCA Exam Topic!)

The PCA exam explicitly tests your understanding that Prometheus is NOT the whole observability picture. Here's what Prometheus does NOT handle — and what you should use instead:

Metrics + Logs + Traces — The Full Observability Stack

THE FULL OBSERVABILITY STACK — WHICH TOOL FOR WHICH JOB
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │   📊 METRICS (Prometheus)    📝 LOGS (Loki/ELK)      🔍 TRACES (Tempo)       │
  │   ──────────────────────    ────────────────────    ─────────────────────    │
  │   "error rate = 5%"         [ERROR] DB timeout      nginx ──10ms──▶          │
  │   "p99 = 1.2s"              at 14:32:01 UTC         auth ──50ms──▶           │
  │   "CPU = 78%"               trace_id=abc123         DB query ──800ms──▶      │
  │                                                      render ──90ms──▶         │
  │   Aggregated NUMBERS        Individual EVENTS       Request JOURNEY          │
  │   Answers: WHAT?            Answers: WHY?           Answers: WHERE?           │
  │                              Answers: WHO?           Answers: WHICH SERVICE?  │
  │                                                                              │
  │   🔧 Prometheus              🔧 Loki / ELK            🔧 Tempo / Jaeger       │
  │      (Metrics only!)            (Log aggregation)        (Distributed tracing)│
  │                                                                              │
  │   PCA EXAM NOTE: The exam expects you to know which tool solves which        │
  │   observability problem. Don't use Prometheus for logs or traces!            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

How They Work TOGETHER — The Incident Response Flow

📊
Metrics ALERTPrometheus detects error spike
→
🔍
Traces SHOWTempo reveals slow DB query
→
📝
Logs EXPLAINLoki shows connection errors
→
🔧
YOU FIXAdd index, restart pool
📖 PCA Exam Memory Hook

What → Where → Why: Metrics tell you WHAT happened (error rate = 5%). Traces show you WHERE the time went (DB query = 800ms). Logs tell you WHY it failed (connection pool exhausted). Together = full observability. Prometheus handles only the WHAT.

Logs are timestamped text records of discrete events. Events are state changes (pod started, deployment rolled back). Traces/Spans follow a single request through multiple services. Each requires a different tool — Prometheus is ONLY for metrics.

1.5 RED Method, USE Method & The Four Golden Signals

How do you decide WHAT to monitor? Three proven frameworks answer this question. The PCA exam expects you to know when to apply each one:

RED Method — Application Health

The RED Method (coined by Tom Wilkie) focuses on application-level monitoring. For every service endpoint, monitor:

🔴
RATE — Requests per Second

How much traffic is this service handling? Measure with rate(http_requests_total[5m]). Answers: "Is traffic normal, spiking, or dropping?" For anihpj: track /api/jobs/, /api/skills/, /api/apply/ separately.

🔴
ERRORS — Failed Requests per Second

How many requests are failing? Measure with rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]). Answers: "Is the service healthy?" For anihpj: alert when error rate exceeds 5% for any endpoint.

🔴
DURATION — How Long Requests Take

What's the latency distribution? Measure with histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])). Answers: "Are users waiting too long?" For anihpj: track p50, p95, p99 latency per endpoint.

USE Method — Infrastructure Health

The USE Method (coined by Brendan Gregg) focuses on infrastructure-level monitoring. For every resource (CPU, memory, disk, network), monitor:

🔵
UTILIZATION

% of resource being used. CPU at 78%, memory at 4.2GB/8GB, disk at 65%. High utilization = resource is busy. Track with Node Exporter metrics.

🔵
SATURATION

Amount of queued/backlogged work. CPU run queue > number of cores, disk I/O queue > 0. Saturation = bottleneck. Often a BETTER indicator than utilization.

🔵
ERRORS

Hardware or OS-level failures. Disk read errors, network packet drops, memory ECC errors. Even at low utilization, errors indicate trouble. Track with Node Exporter.

The Four Golden Signals (Google SRE)

Google's Site Reliability Engineering book defines four signals that cover both application AND infrastructure:

SignalWhat It Measuresanihpj ExamplePromQL Pattern
LatencyTime to serve a requestp99 of /api/jobs/ < 500mshistogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m]))
TrafficDemand on your system245 requests/second to jobpostrate(http_requests_total[5m])
ErrorsRate of failed requests0.5% of requests return 5xxrate(http_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])
SaturationHow "full" your system isCPU at 78%, DB connections 45/50node_cpu_seconds_total + gauges
📖 When to Use Which Framework — Quick Decision Guide

RED = Every microservice endpoint (application layer). USE = Every infrastructure resource — CPU, memory, disk, network. Four Golden Signals = Your SLO dashboard — the four metrics every on-call engineer checks first. For anihpj: RED on /api/* endpoints, USE on Django servers, Golden Signals on the main SRE dashboard.

FrameworkLayerMetricsUse on K8sPromQL Pattern
REDApplicationRate, Errors, DurationEvery Service endpointrate()[5m], histogram_quantile()
USEInfrastructureUtilization, Saturation, ErrorsNode CPU/Mem/Disk/NetRaw gauge, rate() for saturation
Golden SignalsBothLatency, Traffic, Errors, SaturationSLO dashboardCombination of RED + USE patterns

1.6 SLIs, SLOs, and SLAs — The Reliability Stack

This is a guaranteed PCA exam topic. Understanding the reliability stack is essential for configuring meaningful alerts and dashboards:

SLI → SLO → SLA — THE RELIABILITY PYRAMID
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                              │
  │                   ╔══════════════╗                           │
  │                   ║     SLA      ║  💰 CONTRACT              │
  │                   ║   Promise    ║  "99.95% uptime           │
  │                   ║  + penalty   ║   or 25% refund"          │
  │                   ╚══════════════╝                           │
  │                  ╔════════════════╗                          │
  │                  ║      SLO       ║  🎯 TARGET               │
  │                  ║   Objective    ║  "p99 < 300ms            │
  │                  ║   (internal)   ║   99.9% availability"    │
  │                  ╚════════════════╝                          │
  │                ╔═════════════════════╗                       │
  │                ║        SLI          ║  📏 MEASURE           │
  │                ║     Indicator        ║  "Actual p99 = 200ms │
  │                ║    (What we see)     ║   Actual avail =     │
  │                ╚═════════════════════╝   99.97%"              │
  │                                                              │
  │   GOLDEN RULE: SLO should be STRICTER than SLA               │
  │   (Internal target tighter than customer promise =            │
  │    SAFETY BUFFER before you breach the contract)              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📏
SLI — Service Level Indicator

WHAT you measure. The actual metric value. "p99 latency = 200ms." "Availability = 99.97% this month." "Error rate = 0.3%." SLIs are the raw numbers — honest, unfiltered reality. Prometheus collects SLIs via metrics like http_request_duration_seconds.

🎯
SLO — Service Level Objective

Your internal TARGET. "p99 latency MUST be < 300ms." "Availability MUST be >= 99.9%." SLOs define the acceptable range. If an SLI breaches the SLO → alert fires. SLOs should be STRICTER than SLAs (safety buffer).

💰
SLA — Service Level Agreement

Your CONTRACT with customers. "99.95% uptime guaranteed or 25% refund." SLAs have financial/legal consequences. They are typically LOOSER than SLOs — if you breach your SLO internally, you still have time to fix before breaching the SLA.

🔥
anihpj Example

SLI: p99 latency = 200ms, availability = 99.97%. SLO: p99 < 300ms, availability >= 99.9%. SLA: "99.9% of /api/jobs/ requests complete in under 500ms or service credits apply." Error budget = 0.1% of 30 days = 43 minutes/month of allowed downtime.

⚠️ PCA Exam Trap — Error Budgets

The Error Budget = 1 - SLO. If SLO = 99.9% availability, your error budget = 0.1% of the month = ~43 minutes of allowed downtime. If you consume the entire error budget, you should stop deploying new features and focus on reliability improvements. The PCA exam may ask: "Your SLO is 99.9%. How many minutes of downtime are allowed per 30-day month?" Answer: 0.1% × 30 × 24 × 60 = 43.2 minutes.

1.7 When Prometheus Does NOT Fit — Official Guidance

The PCA exam explicitly tests your knowledge of Prometheus's limitations. Knowing when NOT to use Prometheus is as important as knowing how to use it:

WHEN PROMETHEUS IS THE WRONG TOOL
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │  ❌ PROMETHEUS IS NOT THE RIGHT CHOICE WHEN:                                  │
  │                                                                              │
  │  1. You need 100% ACCURACY (e.g., per-request billing)                       │
  │     → Prometheus prioritizes reliability over completeness                   │
  │     → Scrapes can miss data points between intervals                         │
  │     → Use: dedicated billing/audit system + Prometheus for monitoring        │
  │                                                                              │
  │  2. You need to store INDIVIDUAL EVENTS/LOGS                                 │
  │     → Prometheus stores aggregated metrics, not raw events                   │
  │     → Use: Loki, Elasticsearch, or OpenSearch for logs                       │
  │                                                                              │
  │  3. Your data is NOT NUMERIC                                                  │
  │     → Prometheus only handles float64 and histograms                         │
  │     → Strings, text, images don't fit the time-series model                  │
  │                                                                              │
  │  4. You need a PUSH-BASED model (apps pushing to central server)             │
  │     → Prometheus is pull-first (PushGateway is for edge cases only)          │
  │     → Use: InfluxDB, Graphite, or Datadog if push is essential              │
  │                                                                              │
  │  5. You need COMPLEX JOINS or TRANSACTIONS across data                       │
  │     → PromQL is NOT SQL — no JOINs, no transactions                          │
  │     → Each PromQL query operates on time series independently                │
  │                                                                              │
  │  ✅ PROMETHEUS IS THE RIGHT CHOICE FOR:                                       │
  │  • Numeric time-series monitoring (CPU, memory, request rates)               │
  │  • Alerting based on thresholds and patterns                                 │
  │  • Multi-dimensional metric analysis (by service, endpoint, region)          │
  │  • Cloud-native, Kubernetes-native monitoring                                │
  │  • Reliable monitoring during outages (no external dependencies)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
✅
Prometheus EXCELS at

Numeric time-series, pull-based scraping, dimensional labels, PromQL queries, alerting rules, service discovery, Kubernetes monitoring.

❌
Prometheus CANNOT do

Log storage, distributed tracing, 100% accurate billing, complex SQL JOINs, push-based data ingestion (except via PushGateway for batch jobs).

🔧
Complementary Tools

Loki/ELK for logs. Tempo/Jaeger for traces. Thanos/Cortex for long-term storage. Grafana for visualization. Alertmanager for alert routing.

🎯
PCA Exam Tip

If a question says "You need to store and search application logs" — the answer is NEVER Prometheus. Always Loki or Elasticsearch. This is one of the most common exam traps!

🔀 Should I Use Prometheus? — Decision Flowchart

Q1: Is your data NUMERIC (CPU%, request counts, latencies)?
✅ YES ▼ ❌ NO ▶
Q2: Do you need dimensional labels (by endpoint, method, status)?
✋ STOP — Use Loki for logs, Tempo for traces, or a general-purpose DB
✅ YES ▼ ❌ NO ▶
Q3: Can your apps expose a /metrics endpoint (Pull model)?
⚠️ Consider InfluxDB or Graphite (push model). Or use PushGateway for batch jobs.
✅ YES ▼ ❌ NO ▶
🔥 USE PROMETHEUS! It's the right tool for this job.
⚠️ Prometheus CAN work but consider if another tool fits better.
  • 3 Pillars mnemonic: M.L.T. — Metrics (Prometheus), Logs (Loki), Traces (Tempo). Exam WILL ask: "Which tool for [scenario]?"
  • RED vs USE: RED = application (Rate/Errors/Duration). USE = infrastructure (Util/Satur/Errors). RED looks at requests; USE looks at resources.
  • Error budget trick: 99% = 7.2h/month downtime. 99.9% = 43 min. 99.99% = 4.3 min. Subtract from 100% and multiply by 43200 (minutes in 30 days).
  • NOT Prometheus: Logs, traces, billing, SQL JOINs, push model requests. The exam will show you these as distractors.

📊 Chapter 1: What We Learned

📊
Three PillarsMetrics (WHAT), Logs (WHY), Traces (WHERE) — each needs a different tool
🔭
Monitoring vs ObservabilityKnown checks vs exploring ANY question. Prometheus = metrics = monitoring pillar
🔴
RED MethodRate, Errors, Duration — monitor every service endpoint with these three
🔵
USE MethodUtilization, Saturation, Errors — monitor every infrastructure resource
🏆
Golden SignalsLatency, Traffic, Errors, Saturation — Google's 4 essential metrics

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 1 (Observability Concepts)

Q1

What are the Three Pillars of Observability, and which tool handles each pillar in a Prometheus-based stack?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Metrics (Prometheus) — aggregated numeric data. Logs (Loki or Elasticsearch) — timestamped text events. Traces (Tempo or Jaeger) — request journeys through services.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each pillar answers a different question: Metrics = WHAT happened (error rate = 5%), Logs = WHY it happened (connection refused), Traces = WHERE time was spent (DB query took 800ms). Prometheus handles ONLY the metrics pillar. The PCA exam will test that you match the correct tool to each pillar.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              THE THREE PILLARS OF OBSERVABILITY              │
  │                                                              │
  │  📊 METRICS           📝 LOGS              🔍 TRACES        │
  │  ─────────           ──────              ────────        │
  │  WHAT happened?       WHY happened?       WHERE is time     │
  │  Numbers over time    Text events          spent?            │
  │                       with timestamps      Request journeys  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ████████▊ 45%        [ERR] connection     API → DB → Cache │
  │  ██████████▌ 62%        refused at 10:23    ├─ 12ms          │
  │  ███████▎ 38%         [WARN] disk 87%      ├─ 800ms ← SLOW! │
  │                                                              │
  │  Tool: Prometheus     Tool: Loki           Tool: Tempo       │
  │  Query: PromQL         Query: LogQL         Query: TraceQL   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Q2

A developer says: "We should use Prometheus to store and search our application logs." Is this appropriate? Why or why not?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No, this is NOT appropriate. Prometheus is designed for numeric time-series metrics, not log storage and search. Use Loki, Elasticsearch, or OpenSearch for logs.

💡 EXPLANATION

Prometheus stores float64 values and histograms in a time-series database. Logs are text records with arbitrary structure. Attempting to use Prometheus for log storage would result in extremely high cardinality, massive storage consumption, and no search capability. This is one of the most common PCA exam traps — always remember: Prometheus = metrics, NOT logs.

Q3

What does the RED Method stand for, and at what layer of the stack is it applied?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Rate (requests/sec), Errors (failed requests/sec), Duration (request latency). Applied at the application layer — every service endpoint.

💡 EXPLANATION

RED was coined by Tom Wilkie (Grafana Labs). It's designed for microservice endpoints. For every endpoint, you should track: how many requests? how many failed? how long did they take? For anihpj: apply RED to /api/jobs/, /api/skills/, /api/apply/ separately. RED contrasts with USE, which targets infrastructure resources.

Q4

Your anihpj PostgreSQL server is experiencing performance issues. Which method — RED or USE — should you apply to diagnose the database server?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

USE Method — Utilization, Saturation, Errors. Because PostgreSQL is infrastructure, not an application endpoint.

💡 EXPLANATION

USE is for infrastructure resources: CPU, memory, disk, network. For PostgreSQL: check CPU utilization, memory saturation, disk I/O queue, and disk read errors. RED is for application endpoints (HTTP APIs). If you were monitoring the PostgreSQL query API response time, you could use RED — but for the server health itself, USE is correct.

Q5

Define SLI, SLO, and SLA. Which one should be STRICTER — SLO or SLA — and why?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

SLI (Service Level Indicator) = what you measure. SLO (Service Level Objective) = internal target. SLA (Service Level Agreement) = customer contract. SLO must be STRICTER than SLA to provide a safety buffer before breaching the contract.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example: SLI = p99 latency is 200ms. SLO = p99 must be < 300ms. SLA = "99.9% of requests under 500ms or refund." If latency climbs to 350ms → SLO is breached (alert fires, team fixes it). The SLA (500ms) is still safe. Without this buffer, every SLO breach would be an SLA breach → financial penalties. Error budget = 1 - SLO (not 1 - SLA).

Q6

Which of the Four Golden Signals is measured by histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_seconds_bucket[5m]))?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Latency — specifically the 99th percentile (p99) latency over a 5-minute window.

💡 EXPLANATION

The Four Golden Signals are Latency, Traffic, Errors, and Saturation. This PromQL expression calculates p99 latency — "99% of requests complete faster than this value." The histogram_quantile() function computes percentiles from histogram buckets. Always apply rate() to histogram buckets BEFORE histogram_quantile() — this is a classic PCA exam trap!

Q7

Your SLO for anihpj API availability is 99.9%. What is the error budget in minutes per 30-day month?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

43.2 minutes per 30-day month. Calculation: (1 - 0.999) × 30 × 24 × 60 = 0.001 × 43,200 minutes = 43.2 minutes.

💡 EXPLANATION

Error budget = the acceptable amount of downtime/unreliability. For 99.9% SLO: 0.1% of the time can be "bad." 0.1% of a 30-day month = 43.2 minutes. If your service is down for 44 minutes in a month, you've EXCEEDED the error budget → stop feature development, focus on reliability. For 99.99% SLO: only 4.32 minutes/month. The PCA exam may ask you to calculate this!

Q8

A monitoring system sends data from applications TO a central server. Is this the Prometheus model?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No. Prometheus uses a PULL model — the Prometheus server reaches out to scrape /metrics endpoints. This describes a PUSH model (like Graphite or InfluxDB).

💡 EXPLANATION

In the Prometheus pull model, targets expose /metrics endpoints and Prometheus scrapes them on a schedule. This means: (1) Prometheus controls the pace, (2) /metrics endpoint IS a health check, (3) No need to configure apps with server addresses. The PushGateway exists ONLY for short-lived batch jobs — never use it for long-running services. Know this distinction for the PCA exam!

Q9

What is the key difference between Monitoring and Observability?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Monitoring watches known failure modes with pre-configured dashboards and alerts. Observability enables you to ask NEW, unanticipated questions about your system using rich telemetry data (metrics + logs + traces).

💡 EXPLANATION

Monitoring = "I know CPU and memory can fail, so I watch them." Observability = "I have enough data to ask WHY p99 latency jumped at 2:14 PM, even though I never anticipated latency problems on this endpoint." Prometheus is a monitoring tool for the metrics pillar. Full observability requires Prometheus + Loki + Tempo.

Q10

Which of the following is a valid use case for Prometheus? A) Storing application logs, B) Distributed request tracing, C) Numeric time-series monitoring with dimensional labels, D) Per-request billing with 100% accuracy

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

C) Numeric time-series monitoring with dimensional labels. This is exactly what Prometheus was designed for.

💡 EXPLANATION

A = Use Loki/ELK for logs. B = Use Tempo/Jaeger for tracing. D = Prometheus sacrifices completeness for reliability — scrapes can miss data points. It's NOT suitable for billing. C = Prometheus excels at multi-dimensional numeric metrics with labels like {method="GET", endpoint="/api/jobs/", status="200"}. This is the core Prometheus use case.

  • ❌ Gotcha #1: Suggesting Prometheus for log storage. Correct: Loki or Elasticsearch.
  • ❌ Gotcha #2: Confusing Monitoring with Observability. Correct: Monitoring = known checks; Observability = exploring unknowns.
  • ❌ Gotcha #3: Applying RED to infrastructure (CPU). Correct: RED = application endpoints; USE = infrastructure.
  • ❌ Gotcha #4: Making SLA tighter than SLO. Correct: SLO must be tighter — it's your internal safety buffer.
  • ❌ Gotcha #5: Calculating error budget from SLA instead of SLO. Correct: Error budget = 1 - SLO, not 1 - SLA.
  • ❌ Gotcha #6: Thinking Prometheus can do everything. Correct: It's metrics-only. Pair with Loki and Tempo for full observability.
  • ✅ Can you name the Three Pillars and their tools? Metrics→Prometheus, Logs→Loki, Traces→Tempo
  • ✅ Can you explain RED vs USE and when to use each?
  • ✅ Can you define SLI (measure), SLO (target), SLA (contract) and their relationship?
  • ✅ Can you calculate error budget: (1 - SLO) × time period?
  • ✅ Can you list 5 scenarios where Prometheus is NOT the right tool?
  • ✅ Do you know the Four Golden Signals from Google SRE?

Chapter 2: Metrics — The Language of Monitoring 🟢 Beginner

Numbers That Tell Stories — On Kubernetes, Everything IS a Metric

Metrics are numeric measurements collected over time. On a Kubernetes cluster running anihpj, every component exposes metrics: your Django pods, the nodes they run on, kubelet, the API server, etcd — even Kubernetes itself via kube-state-metrics. Understanding Counter, Gauge, Histogram, and Summary is fundamental to Prometheus. This chapter demystifies every metric type with K8s-native examples from your anihpj cluster.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📊 PCA 18% ☸️ K8s-Native

🎯 What You'll Learn in This Chapter

  • ✅ The anatomy of a metric line — metric name, labels, value, timestamp
  • ✅ The 4 Prometheus metric types: Counter, Gauge, Histogram, Summary — with K8s examples
  • ✅ When to use each metric type — a decision table for real-world scenarios
  • ✅ Native vs Classic Histograms — a PCA exam topic!
  • ✅ Labels — how they add dimensions, and why HIGH CARDINALITY kills Prometheus
  • ✅ Time-series data — how Prometheus stores metrics in 2-hour blocks on disk
  • ✅ Naming conventions — the Prometheus way (lowercase_underscores, _total, _seconds, _bytes)
🎓 PCA Relevance
Observability Concepts 18% Fundamentals 20%
PCA Exam Focus — Metric Types ARE the Foundation

You CANNOT pass the PCA exam without mastering metric types. The exam will ask: "Which metric type should you use for tracking request latency?" (Histogram). "Which function should you NEVER apply to a Gauge?" (rate()). "What happens when you apply rate() to a Gauge?" (meaningless garbage data). These are the most common question patterns — master them!

🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 2 Must-Knows
📈 CounterOnly goes UP. Use rate() or increase(). NEVER use raw counter value in graphs!
📉 GaugeUp AND down. Use raw value. NEVER apply rate() to a Gauge — returns garbage!
📊 HistogramDistribution into buckets. histogram_quantile(). ALWAYS rate() buckets FIRST!
📊 SummaryClient-side quantiles. Cannot aggregate across instances — prefer Histogram.
🏷️ LabelsCardinality = product of unique values. < 100 per label, < 10 labels/metric.
📝 Naminglowercase_underscores, _total, _seconds, _bytes. Always base SI units!

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Metrics in Plain English

Metrics are just numbers with labels and timestamps. "CPU usage = 47% at 10:00 AM on web-pod-1" — that's a metric. "247 HTTP requests in the last second on the /api/jobs endpoint" — that's a metric. Prometheus collects millions of these from your K8s cluster every few seconds.

Think of it like a fitness tracker:

Counter = Step counter. It only goes up (10,000 steps today). If you want to know "how many steps per minute?", you can't ask the counter directly — you calculate the rate of change. That's what rate() does: Counter → per-second rate. On K8s: container_cpu_usage_seconds_total is a Counter — total CPU seconds consumed since the container started.

Gauge = Thermometer. It goes up AND down (temperature: 72°F → 74°F → 71°F). You read the current value directly — no calculation needed. On K8s: node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes is a Gauge — how much memory is free RIGHT NOW.

Histogram = Speed camera with buckets. It doesn't just say "average speed is 55 mph." It says: "10 cars at 0-30 mph, 25 cars at 30-60 mph, 15 cars at 60-90 mph." You can calculate ANY percentile from these buckets. On K8s: apiserver_request_duration_seconds_bucket is a Histogram — "how many API requests took ≤0.1s? ≤0.5s? ≤1s?"

Summary = Histogram's cousin (but less useful). Summary calculates percentiles on the CLIENT side and just reports "p50=0.3s, p99=2.1s." The problem: you CANNOT combine Summaries from multiple pods. Prometheus recommends Histograms for almost everything. The PCA exam will test this distinction!

🧠 Decision: Which Metric Type Should I Use?
What kind of measurement am I tracking?
"Total count of something that only increases" — requests, errors, jobs created, bytes sent?
COUNTER — Use .inc(). Name ends in _total. Query with rate() or increase(). Example: jobpost_applications_total.
"Current value that fluctuates" — memory usage, active users, queue depth, temperature?
GAUGE — Use .set(), .inc(), .dec(). Query raw value directly. Example: jobpost_active_listings.
"Distribution of values" — request latency, response size, query duration? Need percentiles?
HISTOGRAM — Define buckets. Use histogram_quantile(). ALWAYS prefer over Summary for aggregatability. Example: jobpost_http_duration_seconds.
"Already have pre-calculated quantiles and can't change the code"?
SUMMARY — Last resort. Cannot aggregate across instances. PCA tip: always recommend Histogram over Summary.

2.1 What Are Metrics? — Numbers That Tell Stories

A metric is a numeric measurement with a name, labels, a value, and a timestamp. Every metric line in Prometheus follows this exact format. Here's a real metric from anihpj running on Kubernetes, scraped from a Django pod:

METRIC ANATOMY — BREAKING DOWN A SINGLE METRIC LINE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │   jobpost_http_requests_total{method="GET",endpoint="/api/jobs/",status="200",│
  │     pod="anihpj-web-7d4f8b-abc",namespace="anihpj-prod"} 24567 1718060400    │
  │   ──────────────────────────── ─────────────────────────────────── ───── ────│
  │            ▲                              ▲                          ▲    ▲  │
  │            │                              │                          │    │  │
  │       METRIC NAME                     LABELS                      VALUE  TIME│
  │   (What is measured)         (Context — where, who, how)      (The number)   │
  │                                                                              │
  │   • Always lowercase          • Key=value pairs in {}          • float64     │
  │   • underscore_separated     • Add dimensions to metric       • Counter: ↗️  │
  │   • Ends with _total,         • MAX 10-20 labels/metric        only goes up  │
  │     _seconds, _bytes         • Keep cardinality LOW!           • Gauge: ↕️    │
  │                                                               goes up/down   │
  │   K8s EXAMPLES:               K8s EXAMPLES:                    • Timestamp:   │
  │   kube_pod_status_phase       pod="anihpj-web-7d4f8b"         Unix epoch ms  │
  │   node_cpu_seconds_total      namespace="anihpj-prod"                         │
  │   container_memory_working_   node="worker-node-3"                            │
  │     set_bytes                 container="anihpj-django"                       │
  │                                                                              │
  │   TOGETHER → ONE TIME SERIES: metric name + unique label combo = one series  │
  │   jobpost_http_requests_total{method="GET",status="200",pod="...-abc"}       │
  │   is ONE time series. Change any label value = a DIFFERENT time series.      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 On Kubernetes, Everything Exposes /metrics

Every component in your K8s cluster exposes metrics at a /metrics endpoint: your Django pods at :8000/metrics, kubelet at :10250/metrics, kube-state-metrics at :8080/metrics, node_exporter at :9100/metrics. Prometheus scrapes ALL of them on a schedule. A single anihpj pod exposes dozens of metrics — request counts, latencies, DB query times, Python GC stats, and more.

2.2 Counter, Gauge, Histogram, Summary — The 4 Metric Types

Prometheus has exactly 4 metric types. Every metric you'll ever use falls into one of these categories. The PCA exam WILL test your ability to choose the right type for a given scenario:

THE 4 METRIC TYPES — VISUAL COMPARISON WITH K8s EXAMPLES
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │  COUNTER (↗️ Only Goes UP)              GAUGE (↕️ Goes UP & DOWN)              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐           ┌─────────────────────────┐           │
  │  │    ████                 │           │  ██                    │           │
  │  │   ██████                │           │ ████    ██             │           │
  │  │  ████████               │           │██████  ████   ███      │           │
  │  │ ██████████              │           │████████████  █████     │           │
  │  │████████████             │           │███████████████████     │           │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘           └─────────────────────────┘           │
  │  📈 Like: car odometer                 📉 Like: thermometer                  │
  │  Use rate() to see speed               Use directly — current value          │
  │                                                                              │
  │  K8s Examples:                         K8s Examples:                         │
  │  kube_pod_restarts_total               node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes        │
  │  container_cpu_seconds_total           kube_deployment_spec_replicas         │
  │  jobpost_applications_total            jobpost_active_listings               │
  │                                                                              │
  │  HISTOGRAM (📊 Distribution)           SUMMARY (📊 Client-Side Percentiles)   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐           ┌─────────────────────────┐           │
  │  │ <10ms:   |||||          │           │ count: 100              │           │
  │  │ <50ms:   ||||||||       │           │ sum: 3450ms             │           │
  │  │ <100ms:  ||||||||||     │           │ p50: 28ms               │           │
  │  │ <500ms:  ||||||||||||   │           │ p90: 85ms               │           │
  │  │  <1s:    |||||||||||||  │           │ p99: 340ms              │           │
  │  │  +Inf:   ||||||||||||||  │           │                         │           │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘           └─────────────────────────┘           │
  │  📊 Server-side quantiles               📊 Client-side quantiles             │
  │  histogram_quantile() in PromQL         Direct p50/p90/p99 values            │
  │                                                                              │
  │  K8s Examples:                         K8s Examples:                         │
  │  jobpost_http_duration_seconds         apiserver_request_duration_seconds    │
  │  (Histogram is preferred on K8s!)      (Summary — used by K8s API server)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Counter — Only Goes Up (Like a Car Odometer)

A Counter is a cumulative metric that only increases — or resets to zero on restart. Think of it like a car's odometer: it only goes forward. NEVER apply rate() to a Gauge, and NEVER use a Counter's raw value directly in graphs (always use rate() or increase()).

📈
Counter Examples on K8s

kube_pod_restarts_total{pod="anihpj-web-abc"} — total pod restarts. container_cpu_seconds_total — total CPU seconds used. jobpost_applications_total — lifetime job applications. kube_deployment_status_observed_generation — deployment updates.

⚡
How to Query Counters

ALWAYS use rate() or increase(): rate(container_cpu_seconds_total[5m]) = CPU usage per second. increase(kube_pod_restarts_total[1h]) = total restarts in 1 hour. Raw counter value is meaningless for graphs!

Gauge — Goes Up AND Down (Like a Thermometer)

A Gauge represents a value that can go up or down. Memory usage, temperature, number of active connections, pod replica count. Use the raw value directly — never apply rate() to a Gauge (it returns garbage).

🌡️
Gauge Examples on K8s

node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes — available memory per node. kube_deployment_spec_replicas — desired replicas. container_memory_working_set_bytes — pod memory usage. jobpost_active_listings — current active job posts.

📉
How to Query Gauges

Use the raw value directly: node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes / (1024*1024*1024) = GB available. kube_deployment_spec_replicas{deployment="anihpj-web"} = desired replica count. For trends: delta() or deriv().

Histogram — Distribution into Buckets (The MOST Important Type)

A Histogram counts observations into configurable buckets. This is how you measure request latency, response sizes, or any value that has a distribution. Histograms are the preferred way to measure latency on Kubernetes — the K8s API server itself uses histograms for request duration.

💡 Why Histograms Are Critical on K8s

In a Kubernetes cluster, you can't just measure "average latency" — that hides the outliers. A Histogram shows you the FULL distribution: "50% of requests complete in under 10ms, 95% in under 100ms, but 1% take over 2 seconds." On K8s, you need this to detect when a specific pod or node is causing tail latency. Use histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m])) for p99 latency.

Summary — Client-Side Percentiles (Less Common)

A Summary is like a Histogram but calculates quantiles (p50, p90, p99) on the client side. The K8s API server uses Summaries for apiserver_request_duration_seconds. Histograms are generally preferred because you can aggregate them across instances — Summaries cannot be aggregated.

Decision Table — When to Use Which Metric Type

You Want to Measure...Use This TypeExample on K8s/anihpjQuery Function
Total requests, errors, restarts (cumulative)Counterkube_pod_restarts_totalrate(), increase()
Current memory, CPU %, active podsGaugenode_memory_MemAvailable_bytesraw value, delta()
Request latency distribution (p50/p95/p99)Histogramjobpost_http_duration_secondshistogram_quantile()
Pre-calculated p50/p90/p99 (client-side)Avoid — use Histogramapiserver_request_duration_secondsDirect quantile values
Pod CPU throttling (cumulative seconds)Countercontainer_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_totalrate()
Number of running pods (current count)Gaugekube_pod_status_phase{phase="Running"}raw value

Native vs Classic Histograms — PCA EXAM TOPIC!

NATIVE vs CLASSIC HISTOGRAMS (Prometheus v3.0+)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │   CLASSIC HISTOGRAM (Legacy)              NATIVE HISTOGRAM (Modern)           │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────┐         │
  │   │ Exposed as MULTIPLE      │           │ Exposed as ONE composite │         │
  │   │ separate time series:    │           │ sample per scrape:       │         │
  │   │                          │           │                          │         │
  │   │ _bucket{le="0.1"} = 5   │           │ Single sample contains:  │         │
  │   │ _bucket{le="0.5"} = 8   │           │ • count: 10              │         │
  │   │ _bucket{le="1.0"} = 9   │           │ • sum: 3.45s            │         │
  │   │ _bucket{le="+Inf"}= 10  │           │ • buckets (dynamic)      │         │
  │   │ _sum = 3.45              │           │                          │         │
  │   │ _count = 10              │           │ ✅ Atomic (all or nothing)│        │
  │   │                          │           │ ✅ Higher resolution      │         │
  │   │ ❌ Can transfer partially│           │ ✅ No bucket config needed│         │
  │   │ ❌ Fixed bucket boundaries│          │ ✅ Always aggregatable     │         │
  │   │ ❌ Hard to aggregate      │           │                          │         │
  │   │   across instances       │           │ 📍 Supported: Go, Java   │         │
  │   └──────────────────────────┘           └──────────────────────────┘         │
  │                                                                              │
  │   PCA EXAM NOTE: "Which histogram type provides atomicity across network?"   │
  │   Answer: NATIVE Histograms (classic can partially transfer because          │
  │   each _bucket series is separate). This IS on the PCA exam!                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Counter Histogram vs Gauge Histogram (Native Only): Counter Histograms use rate() (like classic). Gauge Histograms represent current distribution directly. NHCB (Native Histograms with Custom Buckets) bridges classic → native during migration.

2.3 Labels — Adding Context to Metrics

Labels are key-value pairs that add dimensions to your metrics. They let you filter, group, and aggregate. But labels are a double-edged sword — too many unique label values (high cardinality) will KILL Prometheus performance:

LABEL CARDINALITY — THE SILENT PROMETHEUS KILLER ON KUBERNETES
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │  CARDINALITY = Number of unique label value combinations                      │
  │                                                                              │
  │  ✅ GOOD (Low Cardinality — ~10-100 unique values)                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
  │  │  namespace="anihpj-prod|monitoring|kube-system"   →   3 values   │        │
  │  │  deployment="anihpj-web|anihpj-worker|postgres"   →   3 values   │        │
  │  │  method="GET|POST|PUT|DELETE"                     →   4 values   │        │
  │  │  status="200|301|400|404|500|502"                →  ~6 values   │        │
  │  │  node="worker-1|worker-2|worker-3"               →   3 values   │        │
  │  │                                                                  │        │
  │  │  TOTAL: 3 × 3 × 4 × 6 × 3 = ~648 time series — ✅ Very manageable│        │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
  │                                                                              │
  │  ❌ BAD (High Cardinality — Millions of unique values)                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
  │  │  pod_name="anihpj-web-7d4f8b-abc123"       →  THOUSANDS          │        │
  │  │  container_id="docker://a1b2c3..."          →  THOUSANDS          │        │
  │  │  user_id="user_abc123..."                   →  MILLIONS           │        │
  │  │  request_id="req-abc-def-ghi"               →  BILLIONS           │        │
  │  │  ip="10.244.1.23"                           →  THOUSANDS          │        │
  │  │                                                                  │        │
  │  │  TOTAL: T × T × M × B × T = 💥 PROMETHEUS OOM — MONITORING DIES  │        │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
  │                                                                              │
  │  ⚠️ K8s-SPECIFIC CARDINALITY TRAPS:                                          │
  │  • Pod names include random suffixes (anihpj-web-7d4f8b-abc123) — use       │
  │    deployment labels instead!                                                │
  │  • Container IDs are unique per container — never use as labels!             │
  │  • Pod IPs change on restart — use service names!                            │
  │                                                                              │
  │  RULE OF THUMB: Keep cardinality < 100 per label, < 10 labels per metric.   │
  │  NEVER put user IDs, emails, pod UIDs, or request IDs in labels!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🚨 The #1 Prometheus Performance Killer on Kubernetes

Using raw pod names as labels. Every time a pod restarts on K8s, it gets a new name (anihpj-web-7d4f8b-abc123 → anihpj-web-9g3h2k-def456). If you use pod name as a label, each restart creates NEW time series. After 100 restarts across 10 pods, you've created 1,000 unnecessary time series. Instead, use deployment labels like app="anihpj-web" or K8s-native labels like namespace, deployment, service.

2.4 Time-Series Data — The Heart of Prometheus

A time series = metric name + unique label combination + sequence of (timestamp, value) pairs. Prometheus stores time series in 2-hour blocks on disk. Default retention: 15 days. On Kubernetes, this means you can compare today's pod CPU usage with yesterday's, or last week's.

💾
How Prometheus Stores Data

Append-only, on-disk, 2-hour blocks. Each block is immutable once written. Blocks are compressed (typically 1-2 bytes per sample). The TSDB is write-optimized — queries scan compressed blocks. Default: 15 days retention, configurable via --storage.tsdb.retention.time.

☸️
K8s Storage Considerations

On K8s, use a PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) for Prometheus TSDB data. If the Prometheus pod restarts on a different node without a PVC, it loses ALL historical data. kube-prometheus-stack creates PVCs automatically. For production: use SSD-backed storage classes for better TSDB performance.

📊
Time Series = Label Combinations

jobpost_http_total{method="GET",status="200"} = one series. jobpost_http_total{method="POST",status="200"} = another series. Each unique label combo = new time series = disk + memory cost. This is why cardinality matters!

⏰
Retention & Long-Term Storage

15 days is enough for operational monitoring. For year-over-year comparisons, use Thanos or Cortex with object storage (S3/GCS). Prometheus remote_write sends metrics to long-term storage while keeping recent data locally. See Chapter 15 for details.

2.5 Naming Conventions — The Prometheus Way

Prometheus has strict naming conventions. Following them makes your metrics discoverable and consistent across your entire K8s cluster:

Rule✅ DO❌ DON'T
Lowercase + underscoresjobpost_http_requests_totaljobpostHttpRequestsTotal (camelCase)
Counter suffix: _totalkube_pod_restarts_totalkube_pod_restarts (missing _total)
Duration suffix: _secondsjobpost_request_duration_secondsjobpost_request_duration_ms
Size suffix: _bytesnode_memory_MemTotal_bytesnode_memory_MemTotal_mb
Ratio suffix: _rationode_memory_MemAvailable_rationode_memory_pct
Timestamp suffix: _timestamp_secondsjobpost_last_deploy_timestamp_secondslast_deploy_time
Info suffix: _infokube_pod_infopod_metadata
📖 K8s-Native Naming Examples for anihpj

jobpost_http_requests_total — total HTTP requests (Counter). jobpost_active_listings — current active job posts (Gauge). jobpost_request_duration_seconds — request latency histogram. jobpost_db_connections — active DB connections (Gauge). anihpj_deployment_info — deployment metadata (Info). Notice the pattern: app_component_metric_unit.

  • Counter vs Gauge trick: Does it only go UP? → Counter (use rate()). Can it go UP and DOWN? → Gauge (use raw value). This single question answers 90% of metric-type exam questions.
  • Histogram pipeline mnemonic: B-R-S-H — Bucket counter → Rate() → Sum by(le) → Histogram_quantile(). Forget any step = wrong answer.
  • Cardinality kill switch: user_id, request_id, session_id = NEVER as labels. endpoint, method, status_code = ALWAYS safe. If unique values grow with users → don't use as label.
  • Summary red flag: PCA exam distractor — "use Summary for latency." Correct answer: use Histogram. Summary can't aggregate across instances!
❌ WRONG Metric Type Choices
  • Counter for memory usage → can't go down
  • Gauge for request count → rate() returns garbage
  • Summary for latency → can't aggregate
  • No labels at all → one metric per app
✅ RIGHT Metric Type Choices
  • Counter for requests, errors, jobs → use rate()
  • Gauge for memory, active users, queue depth → use raw
  • Histogram for latency, response size → histogram_quantile()
  • Labels for endpoint, method, status → rich queries

📏

📈
CounterOnly goes UP. Use rate()/increase(). K8s: pod restarts, CPU seconds
🌡️
GaugeUp AND down. Use raw value. K8s: memory, replicas, active pods
