📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 2 (Metrics)

Q1

Which metric type should you use for tracking the total number of HTTP requests to /api/jobs/ since the application started?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Counter — because total requests only increase over time (or reset to zero on restart). Named jobpost_http_requests_total (note the _total suffix).

💡 EXPLANATION

Counters are cumulative. To graph request RATE, use rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[5m]) — never use the raw counter value in a graph. The raw value is always increasing, making graphs look like they're "going up" even when traffic is flat. On Kubernetes, kube_pod_restarts_total is a counter — use increase(kube_pod_restarts_total[1h]) to see restarts per hour.

  COUNTER (total requests)        RATE() → requests per second
  ════════════════════════        ═══════════════════════════
  T=0:   0                        Rate = (new-old)/time
  T=5m:  3000 ██████              = 3000/300s = 10 req/s
  T=10m: 6000 ████████████        = 3000/300s = 10 req/s
  T=15m: 9000 ██████████████████  = 3000/300s = 10 req/s
  
  Raw: always ↗️ (misleading!)   rate(): flat line ✅
  
  GAUGE (free memory)            Read RAW — no rate()!
  ════════════════               ═════════════════
  T=0:  8.0 GB ████████          Shows real fluctuation
  T=5m: 4.2 GB ████▏             DO NOT rate() — garbage!
  T=10m:6.1 GB ██████
  
  🚫 rate(gauge) = nonsense     ✅ gauge as-is = correct
Q2

What happens if you apply rate() to a Gauge metric like node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

It returns meaningless garbage data. rate() is designed ONLY for Counters (monotonically increasing values). Applying it to a Gauge produces mathematically nonsensical results.

💡 EXPLANATION

rate() calculates the per-second rate of increase by looking at how much a value has grown. A Gauge goes up AND down, so rate() might see it go from 8GB → 4GB → 6GB and produce negative or wildly fluctuating values. For Gauges, use the raw value directly, or delta()/deriv() for change over time. This is one of the MOST common PCA exam traps!

  CORRECT: raw Gauge                WRONG: rate(gauge)
  ────────────────────              ──────────────────
  Mem: 8→4→6→5 GB                   "rate" of 8→4→6→5:
  📈  ████▌                         📉  -1.3 +0.7 -0.3
       ██▌  ███▌                          █▌    █▌
            ██▌    ██▌                 █▌  █▌  █▌
  Looks like real usage ✅          Wild oscillations ❌
  
  PCA RULE: rate() = Counter ONLY. Gauge = raw value ONLY.
Q3

On your Kubernetes cluster, 100 pods restart daily. Each pod gets a new name like anihpj-web-7d4f8b-abc123. If you use pod name as a Prometheus label, how many new time series are created per week?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

700 new time series per week (100 restarts/day × 7 days) — and they never get cleaned up automatically! This is the cardinality explosion problem.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each unique pod name is a new label value → new time series. Over a month, that's 3,000 new series. Multiply by all the metrics that pod exposes (CPU, memory, network, app metrics), and you've created 10,000+ useless time series. Solution: Use stable labels like deployment="anihpj-web" or app="anihpj", not ephemeral pod names. Use metric_relabel_configs to drop pod name labels before ingestion.

Q4

Which metric type provides the ability to calculate p50, p95, and p99 percentiles in PromQL, aggregatable across multiple instances?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Histogram — using histogram_quantile(0.95, sum(rate(bucket[5m])) by (le)). Histograms CAN be aggregated across instances; Summaries CANNOT.

💡 EXPLANATION

Summaries calculate quantiles on the client — you can't meaningfully average p99 values from 3 pods. Histograms expose bucket counts that can be summed across pods, THEN quantiles calculated. This is why Histograms are the RECOMMENDED type for latency measurement. The K8s API server uses Summaries (legacy), but modern Prometheus best practice is Histograms.

Q5

What is the difference between Native Histograms and Classic Histograms? Which provides atomicity across the network?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Native Histograms provide atomicity. Classic Histograms expose each bucket as a separate time series (_bucket{le="0.1"}, _bucket{le="0.5"}, etc.) which can partially transfer during scrapes. Native Histograms bundle everything into one atomic sample.

💡 EXPLANATION

With Classic Histograms, a scrape might succeed for le="0.1" but fail for le="1.0" — you'd see inconsistent counts. Native Histograms fix this: the entire histogram (count, sum, all buckets) transfers as one unit — all or nothing. Also: no predefined bucket boundaries needed, higher resolution, and always aggregatable. This IS on the PCA exam!

Q6

Which of these labels would cause HIGH cardinality problems on Kubernetes? A) namespace, B) deployment, C) pod (full pod name), D) method

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

C) pod (full pod name like anihpj-web-7d4f8b-abc123). Pod names contain unique suffixes that change on every restart — creating thousands of unique label values.

💡 EXPLANATION

namespace has ~3-10 values (anihpj-prod, monitoring, kube-system). deployment has ~5-20 values. method has 4-7 values. pod name has THOUSANDS of unique values across restarts. Always use deployment labels or relabel pod names to stable identifiers. In kube-state-metrics, the pod label is auto-generated — use metric_relabel_configs to drop it!

Q7

A metric named jobpost_request_duration measures latency in milliseconds. Is this correct Prometheus naming convention?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No. It should be jobpost_request_duration_seconds — Prometheus ALWAYS uses base units (seconds, bytes). Milliseconds are non-standard.

💡 EXPLANATION

Prometheus convention: always use base SI units. Duration = seconds (not milliseconds). Size = bytes (not megabytes). Ratios = 0.0-1.0 (not percentages). The suffix tells you the unit: _seconds, _bytes, _ratio. When querying, convert to human-readable: rate(...[5m]) * 1000 to get milliseconds. This is tested on the PCA exam!

Q8

How many time series are created by jobpost_http_total{method="GET|POST", status="200|404|500", endpoint="/api/jobs|/api/skills|/api/users"}?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

18 time series — calculated as 2 methods × 3 status codes × 3 endpoints = 18 unique label combinations.

💡 EXPLANATION

Cardinality calculation: multiply unique values per label. 2 × 3 × 3 = 18. This is perfectly manageable. But if you add user_id with 1 million unique values: 18 × 1,000,000 = 18 MILLION time series — Prometheus OOM. Always calculate cardinality before adding a new label. Formula: product of (unique values per label).

Q9

You're monitoring an anihpj Django pod on Kubernetes. Which metric type should you use for tracking the pod's current memory usage?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Gauge — memory usage goes up and down. Example: container_memory_working_set_bytes{pod=~"anihpj-web.*"} (a K8s-native metric from cAdvisor/kubelet).

💡 EXPLANATION

Memory is NOT cumulative (unlike CPU seconds which IS a Counter). It fluctuates as the application allocates and frees memory. Use the raw Gauge value directly. To alert: container_memory_working_set_bytes > 500 * 1024 * 1024 (more than 500MB). K8s provides this via cAdvisor metrics scraped from kubelet at :10250/metrics/cadvisor.

Q10

On Kubernetes, the kubelet exposes container_cpu_usage_seconds_total. What metric type is this and how should you query it to get per-second CPU usage?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Counter (note the _total suffix). Query: rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total{pod=~"anihpj-web.*"}[5m]) — returns CPU cores used per second.

💡 EXPLANATION

CPU seconds are cumulative — a pod that uses 1 CPU core for 10 seconds accumulates 10 CPU seconds. rate() converts this to "CPU cores per second." If rate() returns 1.5, the pod is using 1.5 CPU cores. If your pod has a 2-core limit, that's 75% utilization. Multiply by 100 to get percentage: rate(...[5m]) * 100 / 2.

  • ❌ Gotcha #1: Applying rate() to a Gauge. Correct: rate() is ONLY for Counters.
  • ❌ Gotcha #2: Using pod name as a label. Correct: Use deployment/service labels instead.
  • ❌ Gotcha #3: Using milliseconds for duration metrics. Correct: Always base units — seconds, bytes.
  • ❌ Gotcha #4: Aggregating Summary quantiles across instances. Correct: Use Histograms for cross-instance aggregation.
  • ❌ Gotcha #5: Forgetting rate() before histogram_quantile(). Correct: Always rate() histogram buckets first!
  • ❌ Gotcha #6: Adding user_id or request_id as labels. Correct: Cardinality explosion — use log-based systems for per-request data.
  • ✅ Can you name all 4 metric types and give a K8s-native example of each?
  • ✅ Do you know which function to use for Counters (rate/irate/increase) vs Gauges (raw value)?
  • ✅ Can you calculate cardinality: product of unique values per label?
  • ✅ Can you explain why Native Histograms are better than Classic Histograms?
  • ✅ Do you know the Prometheus naming convention: lowercase_underscores, _total, _seconds, _bytes?
  • ✅ Can you identify which K8s metrics are Counters vs Gauges (pod_restarts_total = Counter, memory_working_set = Gauge)?

This chapter is the engine room — EVERYTHING else in Prometheus depends on understanding this architecture.

Pull ModelPrometheus visits /metrics every scrape_interval. PushGateway ONLY for batch jobs.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Prometheus Architecture in 5 Minutes

If you're new to Prometheus, think of it as a security guard making rounds:

1. Service Discovery: The guard has a list of "rooms to check" (targets). On K8s, this list auto-updates — new pod? Added. Pod dies? Removed.

2. Retrieval: The guard visits each room every 15 seconds, reads the sensors on the wall (/metrics), and writes down the numbers.

3. TSDB: The guard's notebook. Organized in 2-hour sections. Keeps notes for 15 days, then recycles old pages.

4. Rules Engine: While making rounds, the guard also checks: "Is room temperature > 80°F?" (alerting rule). And pre-calculates: "What's the average temperature today?" (recording rule).

5. HTTP Server: A window where you can ask the guard questions: "What's the temperature in room 3?" (PromQL query). Grafana looks through this same window.

Why Pull, not Push? If rooms had to CALL the guard when something's wrong (Push), a broken phone line looks the same as "everything is fine." Pull means the guard KNOWS when a room is unreachable — silence = problem.

3.1 The Prometheus Server — 5 Core Components

The Prometheus server is NOT a monolith. It's composed of 5 distinct components that work together. Understanding each one is critical for the PCA exam and for debugging production issues:

PROMETHEUS SERVER — 5 COMPONENT ARCHITECTURE (with anihpj K8s targets)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        PROMETHEUS SERVER (Single Binary!)                     │
  │                                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ① RETRIEVAL      │   │ ② TSDB           │   │ ⑤ SERVICE DISCOVERY      │  │
  │  │   (Scraper)      │   │   (Storage)       │   │   (K8s, Consul, DNS,    │  │
  │  │                  │──▶│                   │   │    EC2, Azure, File)     │  │
  │  │  Scrapes /metrics │   │  2-hour blocks    │◀──│                          │  │
  │  │  every 15s        │   │  on disk (PVC)    │   │  "What exists to         │  │
  │  │  from ALL targets  │   │  15-day retention │   │   monitor right now?"    │  │
  │  └────────┬─────────┘   └────────┬──────────┘   └──────────────────────────┘  │
  │           │                      │                                           │
  │           │                ┌─────┴──────┐                                    │
  │           │                │            │                                    │
  │           ▼                ▼            ▼                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────┐                 │
  │  │ ③ HTTP SERVER             │  │ ④ RULES ENGINE           │                 │
  │  │                            │  │                          │                 │
  │  │  • Prometheus UI (:9090)   │  │  • Recording Rules       │                 │
  │  │  • PromQL Query API        │  │    (pre-compute metrics) │                 │
  │  │  • Grafana data source     │  │  • Alerting Rules        │                 │
  │  │  • /api/v1/query endpoint  │  │    (evaluate conditions) │                 │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘  └──────────────┬───────────┘                 │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┘
                                                   │ Fires alerts to:
                                                   ▼
                                            ┌──────────────┐
                                            │ ALERTMANAGER │
                                            │  (External)  │
                                            │  Dedup/Group │
                                            │  Route/Silence│
                                            └──────┬───────┘
                                                   │
                                ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
                                ▼                  ▼                  ▼
                          ┌─────────┐       ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
                          │  Slack  │       │PagerDuty │       │  Email   │
                          └─────────┘       └──────────┘       └──────────┘

  ═══════════════════════ TARGETS (scraped every scrape_interval) ═══════════════
                    │                    │                    │
                    ▼                    ▼                    ▼
          ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
          │  anihpj      │    │ Node Exporter│    │ PostgreSQL   │
          │  Django Pod  │    │  :9100       │    │ Exporter     │
          │  :8000/metrics│   │              │    │  :9187       │
          └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘
🔍
① Retrieval (Scraper)

The workhorse. Visits every target's /metrics endpoint on a schedule (every 15s by default). Pulls the plain-text metrics, parses them, and hands them to the TSDB. On K8s, scrapes anihpj pods, node exporters, kube-state-metrics, and more — all via HTTP GET.

💾
② TSDB (Time-Series Database)

Stores metrics in compressed 2-hour blocks on disk. Append-only — never modifies old data. Each block is immutable once written. Default retention: 15 days. On K8s, ALWAYS use a PersistentVolumeClaim so data survives pod restarts!

🌐
③ HTTP Server

Serves the Prometheus UI at :9090, the PromQL query API at /api/v1/query, and acts as Grafana's data source. Also exposes /metrics (Prometheus monitors itself!) and /-/healthy for health checks.

⚙️
④ Rules Engine

Evaluates two types of rules every evaluation_interval: Recording rules pre-compute expensive queries into new metrics (dashboard speed). Alerting rules check conditions (error rate > 5%) and fire alerts to Alertmanager when true.

🔎
⑤ Service Discovery

Answers: "What exists to monitor RIGHT NOW?" Watches the K8s API for new pods/services. On K8s, when a new anihpj pod spins up, SD detects it within seconds — no manual config changes needed. Supports K8s, Consul, DNS, EC2, Azure, and file-based discovery.

3.2 The Pull Model — Prometheus COMES TO YOU

This is the most important architectural decision in Prometheus. Instead of waiting for apps to send data (Push), Prometheus actively visits every target's /metrics endpoint (Pull). This single decision shapes everything:

PULL MODEL vs PUSH MODEL — WHY PROMETHEUS CHOSE PULL
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │   PULL MODEL (Prometheus)              PUSH MODEL (Graphite/InfluxDB)        │
  │   ┌──────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐               │
  │   │                      │            │                      │               │
  │   │   ┌──────────────┐   │            │   ┌──────────────┐   │               │
  │   │   │  Prometheus  │   │            │   │  Monitoring  │   │               │
  │   │   │   SERVER     │   │            │   │   Server     │   │               │
  │   │   └──────┬───────┘   │            │   └──────┬───────┘   │               │
  │   │      ┌───┼───┐       │            │      ┌───┼───┐       │               │
  │   │      ▼   ▼   ▼       │            │      │   │   │       │               │
  │   │   ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐   │            │      ▼   ▼   ▼       │               │
  │   │   │App││App││App│   │            │   ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐   │               │
  │   │   └───┘└───┘└───┘   │            │   │App││App││App│   │               │
  │   │   /metrics endpoints │            │   └───┘└───┘└───┘   │               │
  │   │                      │            │   Apps PUSH to server │               │
  │   │  Server REACHES OUT  │            │   Server WAITS for    │               │
  │   │  to scrape           │            │   data to arrive      │               │
  │   └──────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────┘               │
  │                                                                              │
  │   ✅ PULL ADVANTAGES:                                                        │
  │   • Prometheus controls the pace — no overload from app floods               │
  │   • /metrics IS a health check — if unreachable, target is DOWN              │
  │   • Apps don't need to know Prometheus address — simpler code                │
  │   • Service Discovery auto-finds new targets — zero manual config            │
  │   • On K8s: if a pod dies, Prometheus just stops scraping — no error storms  │
  │                                                                              │
  │   ⚠️ PUSHGATEWAY — ONLY for short-lived batch jobs:                          │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │   │  CronJob/Batch → PUSH metrics → PushGateway → Prometheus scrapes │      │
  │   │  (job finishes)    (once!)        (holds temporarily)             │      │
  │   │                                                                   │      │
  │   │  ✅ USE FOR: batch jobs, CronJobs, CI/CD pipelines                │      │
  │   │  ❌ NEVER FOR: long-running services (expose /metrics instead!)   │      │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 PCA Exam: Why Pull?

The exam may ask: "Why did Prometheus choose the Pull model?" Answer: (1) Reliable health detection — if Prometheus can't reach /metrics, it KNOWS the target is down. In Push, silence could mean "down" OR "network issue." (2) Simpler app code — just expose /metrics, no server address to configure. (3) Prometheus controls scrape pace, preventing overload. Key takeaway: Pull = Prometheus is in control. Push = apps are in control.

3.3 Data Flow — From anihpj Pod to Grafana Dashboard

Let's follow a single metric's journey — from your Django code to the dashboard:

🐍
1. anihpj DjangoExposes /metrics at :8000
→
🔍
2. ScraperGET /metrics every 15s
→
💾
3. TSDBStore in 2-hr blocks
→
📊
4. GrafanaPromQL → Dashboard
→
👩‍💻
5. YOUSee trends, act!
Parallel path: Rules Engine evaluates conditions → fires alert → Alertmanager → Slack/PagerDuty

3.4 Configuration — prometheus.yml Deep Dive

The prometheus.yml file is the single configuration file that controls EVERYTHING. Here's a complete annotated example for monitoring anihpj on Kubernetes:

prometheus.yml — Complete anihpj Monitoring Config
# prometheus.yml — Monitoring configuration for anihpj on Kubernetes
global:                          # ← Applies to ALL jobs unless overridden
  scrape_interval: 15s           #    How often to scrape targets
  evaluation_interval: 15s       #    How often to evaluate rules
  external_labels:               #    Added to ALL metrics from this Prometheus
    cluster: anihpj-prod         #    Essential for HA/federation setups
    region: us-east-1

alerting:                        # ← Where to send alerts
  alertmanagers:
    - static_configs:
        - targets: ['alertmanager:9093']

rule_files:                      # ← External rule files
  - 'rules/anihpj_alerts.yml'    #    Alerting rules
  - 'rules/anihpj_recording.yml' #    Recording rules

scrape_configs:                  # ← WHAT to scrape and HOW
  # Job 1: Prometheus monitors itself
  - job_name: 'prometheus'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['localhost:9090']

  # Job 2: anihpj Django application
  - job_name: 'anihpj-django'
    scrape_interval: 10s         # ← Override: scrape app faster
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - 'anihpj-web:8000'    # Django /metrics endpoint
          - 'anihpj-worker:8000' # Worker pod /metrics
        labels:
          team: platform
          component: backend

  # Job 3: Node Exporter (system metrics per K8s node)
  - job_name: 'node-exporter'
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - 'node-exporter:9100' # CPU, memory, disk, network

  # Job 4: PostgreSQL monitoring
  - job_name: 'postgres-exporter'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['pg-exporter:9187']

  # Job 5: Redis cache monitoring
  - job_name: 'redis-exporter'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['redis-exporter:9121']

  # Job 6: Blackbox probe — is the site REACHABLE?
  - job_name: 'blackbox'
    metrics_path: /probe         # ← Different path (not /metrics!)
    params:
      module: [http_2xx]         # ← Check for HTTP 200 response
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - 'https://anihpj.com'
          - 'https://api.anihpj.com/healthz'
    relabel_configs:
      - source_labels: [__address__]
        target_label: __param_target
      - target_label: __address__
        replacement: blackbox-exporter:9115
🌍
global

Sets defaults for ALL scrape jobs. scrape_interval: 15s is the sweet spot — fast enough for alerts, slow enough not to overload. external_labels distinguish metrics from different Prometheus instances in HA setups.

🎯
scrape_configs

The heart of prometheus.yml. Each job_name is a logical group of targets. On K8s, you'd use kubernetes_sd_configs instead of static_configs (see Chapter 4).

⚙️
rule_files

External YAML files for alerting and recording rules. Keeps prometheus.yml clean. Rules are evaluated every evaluation_interval (15s).

🔔
alerting

Configures how Prometheus connects to Alertmanager. Multiple Alertmanager instances can be specified for HA. Prometheus pushes alerts to Alertmanager — this is the ONE push interaction in the system.

3.5 Running Prometheus — Installation & First Scrape

Let's get Prometheus running with Docker and scrape your anihpj Django app:

STEP-BY-STEP — Installing Prometheus with Docker for anihpj
  STEP 1: Create prometheus.yml (use the config from section 3.4)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  STEP 2: Run Prometheus in Docker
  ─────────────────────────────────
  docker run -d --name prometheus -p 9090:9090 \
    -v $(pwd)/prometheus.yml:/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml \
    prom/prometheus

  STEP 3: Verify Prometheus is running
  ─────────────────────────────────────
  curl http://localhost:9090/-/healthy
  → "Prometheus Server is Healthy."

  STEP 4: Check targets in Prometheus UI
  ───────────────────────────────────────
  Open http://localhost:9090/targets
  → Should see your scrape jobs listed
  → State: "UP" ✅ (green) for healthy targets

  STEP 5: Run your FIRST PromQL query
  ────────────────────────────────────
  Go to http://localhost:9090/graph
  Type: up
  → Returns 1 for each UP target, 0 for DOWN targets

  STEP 6: Query anihpj metrics specifically
  ──────────────────────────────────────────
  Type: http_requests_total{job="anihpj-django"}
  → Shows total HTTP requests from your Django app!
  → Try: rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj-django"}[5m])
  → Shows requests per SECOND (much more useful!)

3.6 Exposition Format — How /metrics Looks (PCA Exam Topic!)

When Prometheus scrapes /metrics, it receives plain text. Every line follows a strict format. The PCA exam may show you a /metrics output and ask you to identify metric names, types, labels, or values:

EXPOSITION FORMAT — WHAT /metrics LOOKS LIKE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests                               │
  │  # TYPE http_requests_total counter                                           │
  │  http_requests_total{method="GET",endpoint="/api/jobs",status="200"} 1247    │
  │  http_requests_total{method="POST",endpoint="/api/jobs",status="201"} 89     │
  │  http_requests_total{method="GET",endpoint="/api/skills",status="200"} 567   │
  │                                                                              │
  │  # HELP node_cpu_seconds_total CPU seconds consumed                           │
  │  # TYPE node_cpu_seconds_total counter                                        │
  │  node_cpu_seconds_total{cpu="0",mode="idle"} 1234567.89                      │
  │  node_cpu_seconds_total{cpu="0",mode="system"} 234567.12                     │
  │                                                                              │
  │  LINE FORMAT (Prometheus Text Format):                                        │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                                        │
  │  metric_name{label1="val1",label2="val2"} value timestamp(ms)                │
  │  ───────────── ─────────────────────────── ───── ────────────                │
  │       ▲                  ▲                    ▲        ▲                      │
  │    METRIC              LABELS              VALUE   TIMESTAMP                  │
  │    NAME                (optional)                  (optional — Prometheus     │
  │                                                    adds it on scrape!)        │
  │                                                                              │
  │  METADATA LINES (prefixed with #):                                            │
  │  • # HELP    — Human-readable description                 │
  │  • # TYPE           — counter|gauge|histogram|summary|untyped    │
  │                                                                              │
  │  PCA EXAM NOTE: The exam may show you /metrics output and ask:                │
  │  "What is the metric name?" "What type is this?" "What is the value           │
  │  of {status='200'}?" Timestamps are OPTIONAL — Prometheus adds them.         │
  │  OpenMetrics format adds # EOF at the end — Prometheus text format doesn't.  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ PCA Exam Trap — OpenMetrics vs Prometheus Text Format

The PCA exam may ask about the difference. Prometheus Text Format: Simple, no EOF marker, timestamps optional. OpenMetrics: Newer standard, requires # EOF at end, stricter format, supports more types (like Info and Stateset). Prometheus can scrape BOTH formats. Know that OpenMetrics is the emerging standard but Prometheus text format is still widely used.

  • Architecture mnemonic: S.T.R.A.P. — Service Discovery → TSDB → Retrieval → Alerting → PromQL. 5 components, in that order.
  • Pull vs Push: Prometheus PULLS (scrapes /metrics). PushGateway is the EXCEPTION for short-lived jobs. Alertmanager receives PUSHED alerts.
  • Retention default: 15 days. Extend: --storage.tsdb.retention.time=30d. TSDB uses 2-hour blocks on disk — needs SSD, not NFS.
  • Scrape interval tradeoff: 15s = standard. 5s = faster detection but 3× storage. 60s = conservative. Exam may ask about impact on TSDB size.

3.7 TSDB Deep Dive — How Prometheus Stores Your Data

🗄️ TSDB INTERNALS — 2-Hour Blocks, WAL, and Compaction
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     PROMETHEUS TSDB — DATA FLOW                                │
  │                                                                               │
  │  SCRAPE ──▶ WAL (Write-Ahead Log) ──▶ Head Block (in memory)                 │
  │               │                         │                                      │
  │               │ durability              │ 2 hours → flushed to disk           │
  │               │ if crash, replay        ▼                                      │
  │               │                   ┌──────────────┐                            │
  │               │                   │ Block on Disk │  Immutable ─▶ never       │
  │               │                   │ 2-hour window │  changes after write      │
  │               │                   └──────┬───────┘                            │
  │               │                          │                                     │
  │               │                   ┌──────▼───────┐  COMPACTION                │
  │               │                   │ Compactor    │  merges old blocks         │
  │               │                   │ every 2h     │  for efficiency            │
  │               │                   └──────┬───────┘                            │
  │               │                          ▼                                     │
  │               │                   ┌──────────────┐                            │
  │               │                   │ Merged Block  │  larger blocks             │
  │               │                   │ (up to 10%    │  = fewer files             │
  │               │                   │  of retention)│  = faster queries          │
  │               │                   └──────────────┘                            │
  │                                                                               │
  │  KEY CONCEPTS:                                                                 │
  │  • WAL: Write-Ahead Log — survives crashes. Replayed on restart.              │
  │  • Head Block: Last 2 hours, in memory + on disk (mmap). Fastest queries.     │
  │  • Compaction: Merges overlapping blocks. Removes deleted (tombstoned) data.  │
  │  • Retention: Blocks older than --storage.tsdb.retention.time are DELETED.    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 3 (Architecture)

Q1

Name the 5 core components of the Prometheus Server.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Retrieval (Scraper), TSDB (Storage), HTTP Server (API/UI), Rules Engine, Service Discovery.

💡 EXPLANATION

All 5 run inside a single Prometheus binary. Retrieval pulls /metrics. TSDB stores in 2-hour blocks. HTTP serves queries + UI. Rules evaluate recording/alerting rules. SD discovers targets automatically. The PCA exam expects you to know what each does — especially the difference between Recording Rules (pre-compute) and Alerting Rules (fire alerts).

  ┌────────────────── PROMETHEUS SERVER (single binary) ──────────────────┐
  │                                                                        │
  │  ① SERVICE        ② RETRIEVAL       ③ TSDB          ④ RULES ENGINE    │
  │  DISCOVERY        (Scraper)         (Storage)                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐      │
  │  │K8s API   │───▶│ Pull     │────▶│ 2h blocks│     │ Recording│      │
  │  │file_sd   │    │ /metrics │     │ 15d ret  │     │ Alerting │      │
  │  │static_cfg│    │ every 15s│     │ on SSD   │     │ ─────────│      │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘     └────┬─────┘      │
  │                                        │                │             │
  │                                   ⑤ HTTP SERVER ←────────┘             │
  │                                   ┌──────────────┐                    │
  │                                   │ :9090/graph   │  Query UI         │
  │                                   │ :9090/api/v1  │  REST API         │
  │                                   │ :9090/metrics │  Self-metrics     │
  │                                   └──────────────┘                    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Q2

Why did Prometheus choose the PULL model instead of PUSH? Give 3 reasons.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1. Reliable health detection — if Prometheus can't reach /metrics, it KNOWS the target is down. 2. Simpler app code — apps just expose /metrics, no server address to configure. 3. Pace control — Prometheus controls scrape speed, preventing overload from app floods.

💡 EXPLANATION

In Push: if data stops arriving, is the app down or the network broken? Hard to tell. In Pull: Prometheus actively reaches out. No response = target is unreachable. Also: /metrics endpoint doubles as a health check — if your app is broken, it probably won't serve /metrics correctly. This is a classic PCA exam question.

Q3

What is the PushGateway and when should you use it?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The PushGateway is an intermediary for short-lived batch jobs (CronJobs, CI/CD pipelines) that finish too quickly for Prometheus to scrape. Use ONLY for batch jobs. NEVER for long-running services.

💡 EXPLANATION

A CronJob that runs for 5 seconds would be missed by Prometheus's 15-second scrape interval. The PushGateway solves this: batch job pushes metrics → PushGateway holds them → Prometheus scrapes PushGateway. But for long-running services (Django pods, databases), ALWAYS expose /metrics directly. The PushGateway adds complexity and is a single point of failure.

Q4

In prometheus.yml, what does scrape_interval: 15s in the global section do?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Sets the default scrape interval for ALL jobs unless a specific job overrides it with its own scrape_interval. Controls how often Prometheus fetches /metrics from each target.

💡 EXPLANATION

15s is the standard. Too fast (1s) = overloads targets and Prometheus itself. Too slow (5min) = miss important spikes. The global setting is a default — individual scrape_configs can override it. For example, anihpj's Django pods might be scraped every 10s (faster) while node exporters stay at 15s (default).

Q5

What are external_labels in prometheus.yml used for?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Distinguishing metrics from different Prometheus instances in HA or federation setups. They're added to EVERY metric scraped by this Prometheus instance.

💡 EXPLANATION

When running 2 Prometheus servers for HA, both scrape the same targets. Without external_labels, their metrics are identical and you can't tell which Prometheus collected which data. Add external_labels: {cluster: "anihpj-prod", replica: "prometheus-1"} to differentiate. Essential for Alertmanager deduplication and federation.

Q6

In the /metrics output, what does # HELP and # TYPE indicate?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

# HELP = human-readable description of the metric. # TYPE = the metric type: counter, gauge, histogram, summary, or untyped.

💡 EXPLANATION

These are metadata lines — they don't contain data values. They help Prometheus understand what each metric is. A single /metrics endpoint may have hundreds of metrics, each with its own HELP/TYPE pair. The PCA exam may show a /metrics snippet and ask: "What type is this metric?" Look for the # TYPE line!

Q7

Where does Prometheus store scraped metrics and for how long by default?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In the TSDB (Time-Series Database) on local disk, in 2-hour blocks. Default retention: 15 days. On Kubernetes, use a PersistentVolumeClaim to persist across pod restarts.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each 2-hour block is immutable once written. Blocks are compressed (1-2 bytes per sample). Retention is configurable via --storage.tsdb.retention.time=30d. For longer retention, use remote_write to Thanos/Cortex. On K8s, WITHOUT a PVC, restarting the Prometheus pod LOSES all historical data!

Q8

What's the difference between Recording Rules and Alerting Rules?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Recording Rules pre-compute expensive PromQL queries into new metrics (performance). Alerting Rules evaluate conditions and fire alerts to Alertmanager when true (notifications).

💡 EXPLANATION

Recording rule: job:http_errors:rate5m pre-computes the error rate every 60s so dashboards load instantly. Alerting rule: AnihpjHighErrorRate checks if error rate > 5% and fires an alert. Both are evaluated by the Rules Engine at evaluation_interval. Recording rules reduce dashboard load time from 15s to <1s.

Q9

You see this in prometheus.yml: metrics_path: /probe. What does it mean?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The scrape target does NOT expose metrics at the default /metrics path — it uses /probe instead. This is common for the Blackbox Exporter, which exposes probe results at /probe rather than /metrics.

💡 EXPLANATION

By default, Prometheus scrapes /metrics. The Blackbox Exporter uses /probe?target=anihpj.com&module=http_2xx — you tell it WHAT to probe via query parameters. The metrics_path field overrides the default path. Other exporters that use non-standard paths might also need this. The default is always /metrics.

Q10

On Kubernetes, your Prometheus pod restarts. What happens to the TSDB data without a PersistentVolumeClaim?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

ALL historical data is LOST. The TSDB stores metrics on the pod's ephemeral filesystem. Without a PVC, the new pod starts with an empty TSDB — losing all metrics from before the restart.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is why kube-prometheus-stack creates PVCs automatically. The PVC survives pod restarts and even node failures (if using network-attached storage). Without it, you'd lose up to 15 days of monitoring data on every restart. For production, always use SSD-backed StorageClasses for better TSDB write performance.

  • ❌ Gotcha #1: Using PushGateway for long-running services. Correct: Expose /metrics; PushGateway is ONLY for batch jobs.
  • ❌ Gotcha #2: Forgetting PVC for TSDB on K8s. Correct: Always use PersistentVolumeClaim!
  • ❌ Gotcha #3: Confusing Recording Rules with Alerting Rules. Correct: Recording = pre-compute; Alerting = fire alerts.
  • ❌ Gotcha #4: Not knowing that timestamps in /metrics are OPTIONAL. Correct: Prometheus adds them on scrape.
  • ❌ Gotcha #5: Confusing global scrape_interval with per-job overrides. Correct: Per-job settings override global defaults.
  • ✅ Can you name all 5 Prometheus Server components and what each does?
  • ✅ Can you explain why Pull beats Push for monitoring?
  • ✅ Can you identify each section of prometheus.yml (global, scrape_configs, rule_files, alerting)?
  • ✅ Can you read a /metrics output and identify metric name, type, labels, and value?
  • ✅ Do you know when to use PushGateway vs /metrics endpoint?
  • ✅ Do you know that TSDB stores 2-hour blocks and default retention is 15 days?

Chapter 4: Service Discovery 🟢 Beginner

Finding What to Monitor — Automatically on Kubernetes

In dynamic K8s environments, pods come and go every minute. You CANNOT hardcode target IPs in prometheus.yml. Service Discovery answers: "What should Prometheus scrape RIGHT NOW?" — and updates automatically as pods are created, destroyed, or rescheduled. This chapter covers static configs, file-based SD, Kubernetes SD, relabeling, and how jobs/instances identify every target.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~2 hours📊 PCA 20%☸️ K8s-Core

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why hardcoding IPs fails on Kubernetes and how Service Discovery solves it
  • ✅ Static configs — when to use them (dev/staging) and their limitations
  • ✅ File-based SD — the bridge between static and fully dynamic
  • ✅ Kubernetes Service Discovery — auto-discover pods, services, endpoints, nodes
  • ✅ Relabeling — filter, modify, and control which targets get scraped
  • ✅ Jobs & Instances — the two automatic labels that identify every target
🎓 PCA Relevance
Fundamentals 20%Service Discovery
PCA Exam Focus — K8s SD + Relabeling

The PCA exam will test: (1) Why Service Discovery matters on K8s, (2) The difference between relabel_configs (before scrape) and metric_relabel_configs (after scrape), (3) What job and instance labels mean. Know that every scraped target gets these two labels automatically!

🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 4 Must-Knows
Why SDK8s pods are ephemeral — IPs change on restart. SD watches K8s API for current targets.
SD Typesstatic_configs → file_sd_configs → kubernetes_sd_configs (from simple to dynamic).
Relabelingrelabel_configs (BEFORE scrape) vs metric_relabel_configs (AFTER scrape). Exam trap!
job + instanceAuto-labels on every metric. job=job_name, instance=host:port. Never defined in app code.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Service Discovery in Plain English

Service Discovery = automatic phone book for Prometheus. In the old days, you'd write down every server's phone number (IP address) in prometheus.yml. Server died? Phone number changed? You'd manually update the file. On Kubernetes, pods die and restart with new IPs every few minutes — manual updates are impossible.

Think of it like a restaurant finder app: You don't maintain a paper list of every restaurant in the city with their exact addresses. You open an app, it queries a live database, and shows you what's open RIGHT NOW. That's what Prometheus does with K8s Service Discovery — it asks the K8s API: "What pods exist right now with label app=anihpj?" and scrapes those.

Three levels of sophistication:

Level 1 — static_configs (the post-it note): "Scrape localhost:8000, localhost:9187, localhost:9121." Simple. Manual. Breaks if anything changes. OK for dev laptops, not for production.

Level 2 — file_sd_configs (the shared spreadsheet): Your CI/CD pipeline writes a JSON file with current targets. Prometheus reads it every 5 minutes. Better than static, but 5-minute lag means you miss ephemeral pods.

Level 3 — kubernetes_sd_configs (the live database): Prometheus directly watches the K8s API. Pod created → scraped within seconds. Pod deleted → removed instantly. Scale from 3 to 300 pods → zero config changes. This is the gold standard for K8s.

The critical PCA distinction: relabel_configs runs BEFORE scraping (controls WHICH targets to scrape). metric_relabel_configs runs AFTER scraping (controls WHICH metrics to keep/drop). The exam WILL ask you to distinguish these!

4.1 Why Service Discovery? — You Can't Hardcode Everything

Imagine anihpj running on Kubernetes with 5 Django pods. Every time a pod restarts, it gets a NEW IP address and a NEW pod name. If you hardcoded targets: ['10.244.1.23:8000', '10.244.2.45:8000'] in prometheus.yml, your monitoring would break within minutes. Service Discovery solves this by watching the K8s API and automatically updating the target list:

❌ Hardcoded IPs (STATIC)
  • Pod restarts → new IP → prometheus.yml is WRONG
  • Must MANUALLY update targets every deploy
  • Scale to 20 pods → edit config again
  • New namespace → forget to add → blind spot
  • Works for: tiny, never-changing setups
✅ Service Discovery (DYNAMIC)
  • Watches K8s API → auto-detects new pods
  • Pod dies → auto-removed from targets
  • Scale to 100 pods → zero config changes
  • New namespace with label → auto-included
  • Works for: ANY dynamic environment

4.2 Static Configs — The Simplest Way

For small, stable environments (local dev, staging), static_configs is perfectly fine. You explicitly list every target. For anihpj development, you might have just 3 targets:

prometheus.yml — Static Configs for anihpj Dev
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'anihpj-dev'
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - 'localhost:8000'        # Django dev server
          - 'localhost:9187'        # PostgreSQL exporter
          - 'localhost:9121'        # Redis exporter
💡 When to Use Static Configs

✅ Local development, staging, small production (3-5 stable servers). ❌ Kubernetes (pods are ephemeral), auto-scaling groups, any dynamic environment. The rule: if targets change without your manual intervention, use dynamic SD instead.

4.3 File-Based Service Discovery — The Bridge

File-based SD reads target lists from JSON or YAML files on disk. Your CI/CD pipeline or configuration management tool updates these files, and Prometheus automatically picks up changes. Perfect for environments between static and fully dynamic:

prometheus.yml — File-Based SD
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'anihpj-file-sd'
    file_sd_configs:
      - files:
          - '/etc/prometheus/targets/anihpj.json'
        refresh_interval: 5m   # Check for changes every 5 min

# /etc/prometheus/targets/anihpj.json
[
  {"targets": ["web-1:8000","web-2:8000"],"labels":{"env":"prod"}},
  {"targets": ["pg-exporter:9187"],"labels":{"component":"database"}}
]

4.4 Kubernetes Service Discovery — The Real World

kubernetes_sd_configs watches the K8s API server for pods, services, endpoints, nodes, and ingresses. This is how Prometheus auto-discovers your anihpj pods without ANY manual target configuration:

K8S SERVICE DISCOVERY — HOW PROMETHEUS FINDS anihpj PODS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │   ┌──────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐                     │
  │   │  Prometheus  │  WATCH   │   Kubernetes API Server  │                     │
  │   │              │◀─────────│                          │                     │
  │   │  kubernetes_ │  pods,   │  Knows about ALL:        │                     │
  │   │  sd_configs  │ services,│  • Pods (IP + labels)    │                     │
  │   │              │ endpoints│  • Services (selector)   │                     │
  │   │              │ nodes    │  • Endpoints (pod IPs)   │                     │
  │   └──────┬───────┘          │  • Nodes (host IPs)      │                     │
  │          │                  │  • Ingresses (hosts)     │                     │
  │          │                  └──────────────────────────┘                     │
  │          ▼                                                                   │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │   │  DISCOVERED (Before Relabeling): ALL pods from ALL namespaces     │      │
  │   │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐            │      │
  │   │  │kube-system│ │ anihpj   │ │monitoring│ │ default  │  500+ pods │      │
  │   │  │  pods    │ │  pods    │ │  pods    │ │  pods    │            │      │
  │   │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘            │      │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │          ▼                                                                   │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │   │  RELABELING: Filter to only anihpj pods                          │      │
  │   │  ───────────────────────────────────                              │      │
  │   │  relabel_configs:                                                 │      │
  │   │    - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_namespace]                 │      │
  │   │      action: keep                                                 │      │
  │   │      regex: anihpj|monitoring           ← Only these namespaces   │      │
  │   │    - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app]             │      │
  │   │      action: keep                                                 │      │
  │   │      regex: anihpj.*                   ← Only anihpj-labeled pods │      │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │          ▼                                                                   │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │   │  FINAL TARGETS: Only 5 anihpj pods → scraped at :8000   │      │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 K8s SD Role Types

pod: Discovers individual pods with their IPs. service: Discovers K8s Service objects. endpoints: Discovers Service endpoints (individual pod IPs behind a Service). node: Discovers cluster nodes. ingress: Discovers Ingress objects. For anihpj, use role: pod to discover Django pods directly by label.

4.5 Relabeling — The Swiss Army Knife

Relabeling lets you filter, modify, and control which targets are scraped and what labels they get. There are TWO types — and confusing them is a PCA exam trap:

TypeWhen AppliedPurposeExample
relabel_configsBEFORE scrapeFilter targets, set labels for scrapingKeep only pods with app=anihpj
metric_relabel_configsAFTER scrapeFilter/modify ingested metricsDrop expensive metrics, rename labels
🔧
Common Operations

keep: Only scrape targets matching regex. drop: Exclude targets matching regex. replace: Change label values. labelmap: Map K8s metadata labels to Prometheus labels. hashmod: Shard targets across multiple Prometheus instances.

☸️
K8s Metadata Labels

K8s SD exposes pod metadata as __meta_kubernetes_* labels. Use relabeling to map them to real labels: __meta_kubernetes_namespace → namespace, __meta_kubernetes_pod_name → pod. The __ prefix means "internal" — they're removed before ingestion unless you explicitly map them.

4.6 Jobs and Instances — Prometheus Target Identity

Every scraped target gets TWO automatic labels. These are the most important labels in all of Prometheus — used for aggregation, alert routing, and dashboard filtering:

JOBS & INSTANCES — HOW PROMETHEUS IDENTIFIES TARGETS
  scrape_configs:
    - job_name: 'anihpj-django'          ← JOB = "what service is this?"
      static_configs:
        - targets:
            - 'web-1:8000'               ← INSTANCE = "which specific one?"
            - 'web-2:8000'
            - 'web-3:8000'

  RESULT: Every metric from these targets gets auto-labeled:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  up{job="anihpj-django", instance="web-1:8000"} 1            │
  │  up{job="anihpj-django", instance="web-2:8000"} 1            │
  │  up{job="anihpj-django", instance="web-3:8000"} 1            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  QUERY PATTERNS:
  • up{job="anihpj-django"}              — All Django instances
  • up{instance=~"web-.*:8000"}          — All web instances
  • count(up{job="anihpj-django"} == 1)  — How many are UP?
  • avg(node_cpu) by (job)               — Avg CPU per service
PCA Exam: job + instance = Primary Dimensions

The PCA exam EXPECTS you to know that job and instance are automatically added to every scraped metric. You NEVER define them in your application code — Prometheus adds them during scraping. They're used for: grouping dashboards, routing alerts to the right team, and aggregating metrics across instances.

4.7 Relabeling Deep Dive — The Most Powerful Config Feature

🏷️ RELABELING — TWO PHASES, DIFFERENT PURPOSES
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     RELABELING — Before vs After Scrape                        │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PHASE 1: relabel_configs (BEFORE scraping)                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Runs when Prometheus DISCOVERS the target but BEFORE scraping it.     │    │
  │  │ Controls: WHICH targets to scrape, WHAT labels to assign.             │    │
  │  │ Actions: keep (scrape if match), drop (skip), replace (change addr)   │    │
  │  │          labelmap (copy SD metadata → metric labels)                  │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PHASE 2: metric_relabel_configs (AFTER scraping)                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Runs AFTER Prometheus has scraped the target and received metrics.     │    │
  │  │ Controls: WHICH metrics to KEEP or DROP — COST REDUCTION!            │    │
  │  │ Actions: keep (store if match), drop (discard → save storage!),       │    │
  │  │          replace (rename/transform labels)                             │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Relabeling Examples — Common Patterns
# KEEP only production targets (relabel_configs — BEFORE scrape)
relabel_configs:
  - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_namespace]
    regex: 'production'
    action: keep

# DROP noisy metrics (metric_relabel_configs — AFTER scrape, save storage!)
metric_relabel_configs:
  - source_labels: [__name__]
    regex: 'node_netstat_.*'       # Drop all node_netstat metrics
    action: drop
  - source_labels: [__name__]
    regex: 'go_.*'                 # Drop Go runtime metrics
    action: drop

☸️
K8s SDWatches API, auto-discovers pods/services

🏷️
job + instanceAuto-labels on every metric — core dimensions

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 4 (Service Discovery)

Q1

Why is Service Discovery necessary on Kubernetes?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Because pods are ephemeral — they get new IPs on restart, scale up/down dynamically. Hardcoded IP addresses become stale within minutes. Service Discovery watches the K8s API and automatically updates the target list.

💡 EXPLANATION

K8s pods are cattle, not pets. A new pod gets a new IP from the CNI. If you hardcode 10.244.1.23:8000, that pod dies → new pod gets 10.244.5.78:8000 → your monitoring breaks. K8s SD solves this by querying "what pods with label app=anihpj exist RIGHT NOW?"

Q2

What's the difference between relabel_configs and metric_relabel_configs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

relabel_configs = applied BEFORE scrape (filter targets). metric_relabel_configs = applied AFTER scrape (filter/modify ingested metrics).

💡 EXPLANATION

Use relabel_configs to decide WHICH pods to scrape (keep only app=anihpj). Use metric_relabel_configs to drop expensive metrics (like container_memory_*) or rename labels after ingestion. The __ prefix labels are only available during relabel_configs — they're gone by the time metric_relabel_configs runs.

Q3

What two labels are automatically added to every scraped metric?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

job (the job_name from scrape_configs) and instance (the host:port being scraped).

💡 EXPLANATION

These are added automatically during scraping — you never define them in your application code. job = logical grouping (e.g., "anihpj-django", "node-exporters"). instance = specific target (e.g., "10.244.1.5:8000"). Used for: grouping dashboards by job, routing alerts to the right team, and aggregating metrics across instances. The PCA exam expects you to know these two labels.

Q4

Which K8s SD role should you use to discover individual pods?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

role: pod — discovers individual pods with their IPs and labels.

💡 EXPLANATION

K8s SD roles: pod (individual pods), service (K8s Service endpoints), endpoints (endpoint slices), node (cluster nodes), ingress (ingress objects). role: pod gives you every pod's IP + all its labels. Use relabel_configs to filter which pods to actually scrape. Without filtering, you'd try to scrape every pod in the cluster!

Q5

What does action: keep do in relabel_configs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Keeps only targets whose source labels match the regex. All non-matching targets are dropped (not scraped).

💡 EXPLANATION

Relabel actions: keep (match regex → scrape), drop (match regex → skip), replace (set target_label from source_labels), hashmod (shard targets across N Prometheuses), labelmap (map all matching labels). The PCA exam focuses on keep, drop, and replace. keep with regex="anihpj-.*" = only scrape pods with names starting with "anihpj-".

Q6

On a 500-pod K8s cluster, how do you scrape only the 5 anihpj pods?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use kubernetes_sd_configs with role: pod and relabel_configs with action: keep filtering by namespace and pod labels.

💡 EXPLANATION

Without relabeling, K8s SD discovers ALL pods in ALL namespaces. On a 500-pod cluster, Prometheus would try to scrape 500 /metrics endpoints — overwhelming both Prometheus and the pods. The solution: SD discovers everything → relabel_configs filters to only anihpj pods → Prometheus scrapes only those 5. This is the K8s SD + relabeling pattern.

Q7

What format does file-based SD use for target files?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

JSON or YAML — arrays of target groups with optional labels.

💡 EXPLANATION

File SD reads target files from disk, refreshing every refresh_interval (default 5m). Format: [{"targets": ["host:port"], "labels": {"env": "prod"}}]. Useful when you can't use K8s SD (VMs, bare metal) but still want dynamic target updates. Files can be generated by CMDB scripts, Ansible inventories, or CI/CD pipelines.

Q8

What does __meta_kubernetes_namespace provide?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The Kubernetes namespace of the discovered pod. Use relabeling to map it to a regular label like namespace.

💡 EXPLANATION

Meta labels (with __ prefix) are temporary — they exist only during relabeling and are removed before metrics are stored. To preserve K8s metadata, you must relabel them: source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_namespace]; target_label: namespace. Common meta labels: namespace, pod_name, pod_ip, pod_label_app, pod_annotation_*.

Q9

When should you use static_configs instead of SD?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

For small, stable environments where targets rarely change — local dev, staging, or small production with fixed IPs.

💡 EXPLANATION

static_configs is the simplest method: hardcode IP:port targets. Best for local Docker Compose setups (service names are stable DNS), small VMs, or fixed infrastructure. Drawback: any target change requires editing prometheus.yml and reloading. For dynamic environments (K8s, auto-scaling), always use Service Discovery instead.

Q10

How do you query all instances of a specific job?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

up{job="anihpj-django"} — filters by the auto-assigned job label.

💡 EXPLANATION

The up metric is special — Prometheus creates it automatically for every scrape target. up=1 means the last scrape succeeded (HTTP 200). up=0 means it failed. Query up{job="anihpj-django"} to see all Django instances. Add == 0 to find DOWN targets: up{job="anihpj-django"} == 0. This is the #1 most basic health check query!

  • ❌ Confusing relabel_configs (before scrape) with metric_relabel_configs (after scrape).
  • ❌ Hardcoding IPs for K8s pods — they change on every restart!
  • ❌ Forgetting to filter K8s SD results — scraping ALL 500 pods crashes Prometheus.
  • ✅ Can you explain why SD is essential on Kubernetes?
  • ✅ Do you know the difference between the two relabeling types?
  • ✅ Can you name the two auto-labels (job, instance) and their source?

Chapter 5: Exporters — Teaching Old Apps New Tricks 🟢 Beginner

Prometheus Speaks One Language — Exporters Translate Everything Else

PostgreSQL doesn't speak Prometheus. Neither does Redis, Nginx, or your Linux kernel. Exporters bridge this gap — they run alongside your services, collect app-specific metrics, and expose them in Prometheus format at /metrics. This chapter covers Node Exporter, Blackbox Exporter, application-specific exporters, and how to write your own for anihpj custom business metrics.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~2 hours📊 PCA 20%☸️ K8s Sidecars

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What exporters are and why they're essential — "Prometheus speaks one language"
  • ✅ Node Exporter — CPU, memory, disk, network metrics for every K8s node
  • ✅ Blackbox Exporter — probing from the outside: HTTP, DNS, TCP, ICMP
  • ✅ Application-specific exporters for PostgreSQL, Redis, Nginx
  • ✅ How to write your own exporter in Python for anihpj custom metrics
🎓 PCA Relevance
Fundamentals 20%Instrumentation 16%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 5 Must-Knows
Exporter = TranslatorConverts app-specific metrics → Prometheus format at /metrics. No app code changes needed.
Port NumbersNode:9100, PostgreSQL:9187, Redis:9121, Blackbox:9115, Nginx:9113. Memorize these!
Node ExporterDaemonSet on K8s — one per node. CPU/mem/disk/network. Most deployed exporter.
BlackboxUses /probe (NOT /metrics!). Probes external URLs from user perspective.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Exporters in Plain English

Exporters are translators, not the real thing. Imagine you're at the UN. The Chinese ambassador speaks Mandarin. You only understand English. An exporter is the interpreter sitting between you — the Chinese ambassador says something in Mandarin, the interpreter hears it, then tells you in English. The ambassador NEVER had to learn English.

Same with Prometheus and PostgreSQL: PostgreSQL speaks "SQL protocol" (SELECT * FROM pg_stat_activity). Prometheus only understands "exposition format" (metric_name{label="value"} number). The PG Exporter sits between them: it queries PostgreSQL in SQL, converts the results to Prometheus format, and serves them at :9187/metrics. PostgreSQL doesn't even know Prometheus exists — zero code changes.

Key mental model: Exporters run as separate processes. They are NOT plugins, NOT modifications to the application. They are independent binaries that connect to the application (via TCP/SQL/HTTP) and expose a new /metrics endpoint. This means: (1) You can add monitoring without touching production code. (2) If the exporter crashes, the app keeps running. (3) You can upgrade the exporter independently.

🧠 Decision: Exporter or Client Library?
How should I expose metrics from my application?
Is it a third-party app (PostgreSQL, Redis, Nginx)?
USE EXPORTER — You can't modify the source code. Deploy the official exporter as a sidecar/separate process. Example: PG Exporter at :9187.
Is it your OWN Python/Django/Go code?
USE CLIENT LIBRARY — Add prometheus_client to your code. Expose custom business metrics (job applications, active listings) at your app's /metrics. More control, zero extra processes.
Is it infrastructure (CPU, memory, disk)?
USE EXPORTER — Node Exporter DaemonSet. One per K8s node. No code to instrument — it reads /proc and /sys directly.
Need to monitor from OUTSIDE (HTTP/DNS/SSL)?
USE BLACKBOX EXPORTER — Probes external endpoints via /probe. Checks: "Can users reach anihpj.com? Is the SSL cert valid?"

5.1 What Are Exporters?

Exporters are translators. They collect metrics from a system that doesn't natively speak Prometheus (PostgreSQL, Redis, Linux kernel) and expose them in Prometheus format at a /metrics endpoint. Think of them as adapters — they convert proprietary metrics into the universal Prometheus language:

🔌
Exporter = Adapter

PostgreSQL has 200+ internal metrics (connections, queries, locks, replication lag). The PostgreSQL Exporter queries these via SQL and exposes them at :9187/metrics in Prometheus format. No code changes to PostgreSQL needed.

📦
Run as Sidecars on K8s

On Kubernetes, exporters run as sidecar containers in the same pod as the service they monitor, or as separate Deployments/DaemonSets. Node Exporter runs as a DaemonSet (one per node). Prometheus discovers and scrapes them just like any other target.

5.2 Node Exporter — Linux/System Metrics

The Node Exporter exposes hardware and OS metrics from every K8s node. It runs as a DaemonSet (one pod per node) and exposes at :9100/metrics. This is how you monitor the infrastructure running your anihpj pods:

CategoryKey MetricWhat It Tells You
CPUnode_cpu_seconds_totalCPU time per mode (idle, system, user)
Memorynode_memory_MemAvailable_bytesAvailable memory for new pods
Disknode_filesystem_avail_bytesFree disk space — alert before full!
Networknode_network_receive_bytes_totalNetwork traffic per interface

5.3 Blackbox Exporter — Probing from the Outside

The Blackbox Exporter tests your services from the user's perspective. Instead of scraping /metrics, you tell it WHAT to probe (URLs, DNS names, TCP ports) and it reports success/failure and timing. "Is anihpj.com reachable? Is the SSL cert about to expire?"

Blackbox Exporter Config in prometheus.yml
- job_name: 'blackbox'
  metrics_path: /probe         # NOT /metrics!
  params:
    module: [http_2xx]         # Check for HTTP 200
  static_configs:
    - targets:
        - 'https://anihpj.com'
        - 'https://api.anihpj.com/healthz'
  relabel_configs:
    - source_labels: [__address__]
      target_label: __param_target
    - target_label: __address__
      replacement: blackbox-exporter:9115

5.4 Application-Specific Exporters

anihpj MONITORING STACK — ALL EXPORTERS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                              │
  │  ┌───────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐        │
  │  │  anihpj Django    │   │  Node Exporter   │   │ PostgreSQL       │        │
  │  │  :8000/metrics    │   │  :9100/metrics   │   │ Exporter         │        │
  │  │                   │   │                  │   │  :9187/metrics   │        │
  │  │ jobpost_http_*    │   │ node_cpu_*       │   │ pg_stat_*        │        │
  │  │ django_http_*     │   │ node_memory_*    │   │ pg_locks_*       │        │
  │  └────────┬──────────┘   │ node_disk_*      │   └────────┬─────────┘        │
  │           │              └────────┬─────────┘            │                  │
  │  ┌────────┴──────────┐   ┌───────┴──────────┐  ┌────────┴─────────┐        │
  │  │  Redis Exporter   │   │ Blackbox         │  │  NGINX Exporter  │        │
  │  │  :9121/metrics    │   │ Exporter         │  │  :9113/metrics   │        │
  │  │                   │   │  :9115/metrics   │  │                  │        │
  │  │ redis_commands_*  │   │ probe_success    │  │ nginx_http_*     │        │
  │  │ redis_memory_*    │   │ probe_duration_* │  │ nginx_conn_*     │        │
  │  └───────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘        │
  │                                                                              │
  │              ALL SCRAPED BY PROMETHEUS EVERY 15s                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5.5 Writing Your Own Exporter in Python

When no existing exporter covers your needs, write your own! Here's a minimal Python exporter for anihpj custom business metrics:

Python — Custom anihpj Business Metrics Exporter
from prometheus_client import start_http_server, Gauge, Counter
import random, time

# Define custom metrics
active_listings = Gauge('jobpost_active_listings', 'Currently active job posts')
applications_total = Counter('jobpost_applications_total', 'Total applications', ['status'])
skills_per_job = Gauge('jobpost_skills_per_job', 'Avg skills per job posting')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    start_http_server(8000)  # Expose at :8000/metrics
    while True:
        # Poll your Django DB or business logic
        active_listings.set(get_active_job_count())
        skills_per_job.set(calculate_avg_skills())
        time.sleep(15)
  • Port numbers mnemonic: Node=9100, PG=9187, Redis=9121, Blackbox=9115. All start with 9! Nginx=9113. Guaranteed exam question.
  • Blackbox uses /probe: NOT /metrics. Each probe specifies target via query params. Module determines protocol.
  • Node Exporter on K8s: Always DaemonSet (one per node). Needs hostNetwork + hostPID. Deployment = WRONG for exam.
  • Exporter = no code changes: Separate process, external connection. PostgreSQL doesn't know PG Exporter exists. This is THE key difference from Client Libraries.

5.6 Writing a Custom Exporter — Python in 30 Lines

Complete Custom Exporter for anihpj Job Statistics
from prometheus_client import start_http_server, Gauge, Counter
import time, random

# Define metrics at MODULE level (never inside functions!)
jobpost_active = Gauge('jobpost_active_listings', 'Currently active jobs')
jobpost_apps_total = Counter('jobpost_applications_total', 'Total applications', ['status'])
jobpost_skills_avg = Gauge('jobpost_avg_skills', 'Average skills per job')

def collect_metrics():
    """Simulate collecting business metrics — replace with real DB queries"""
    # In production: query Django models or database directly
    active = 247  # JobPost.objects.filter(status='active').count()
    jobpost_active.set(active)
    
    jobpost_apps_total.labels(status='pending').inc(3)
    jobpost_apps_total.labels(status='accepted').inc(5)
    
    jobpost_skills_avg.set(4.2)  # Average from analytics

if __name__ == '__main__':
    start_http_server(8000)  # Expose at :8000/metrics
    print("Custom anihpj exporter running on :8000/metrics")
    while True:
        collect_metrics()
        time.sleep(15)  # Update every 15 seconds
🐍
Key Rules for Custom Exporters

Define metrics at MODULE level (outside functions). Use start_http_server() for standalone exporters. Update metrics in a loop — Prometheus scrapes the latest values. For production: query real data sources instead of hardcoded values.

☸️
Deploying as DaemonSet on K8s

Custom exporters run as sidecars or DaemonSets. Add prometheus.io/scrape annotations for auto-discovery. Use ConfigMaps for exporter configuration. Always set resource limits — exporters are lightweight.
