📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 5 (Exporters)

Q1

What is an Exporter in Prometheus?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A translator that collects metrics from a system that doesn't natively speak Prometheus and exposes them in Prometheus format at /metrics.

💡 EXPLANATION

Exporters bridge the gap. PostgreSQL has 200+ internal metrics but speaks SQL, not Prometheus exposition format. The PG Exporter queries PG's internal tables, converts results to Prometheus format, and serves at :9187/metrics. Key point: exporters run as SEPARATE processes — zero code changes to the monitored application. This is a fundamental PCA concept.

Q2

What default port does Node Exporter use?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

:9100 — runs as a DaemonSet on K8s (one per node).

💡 EXPLANATION

Node Exporter is the most deployed Prometheus exporter. It exposes hardware metrics: CPU (node_cpu_seconds_total), memory (node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes), disk (node_filesystem_avail_bytes), network (node_network_receive_bytes_total). On K8s, deploy as DaemonSet (one per node). On VMs, install as a systemd service. Memorize port 9100 — guaranteed exam question!

Q3

Why does the Blackbox Exporter use /probe instead of /metrics?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Because it probes external targets (URLs, DNS names) rather than exposing its own metrics. Each probe request specifies a target via query parameters.

💡 EXPLANATION

Blackbox Exporter is different from all other exporters. Instead of the exporter having metrics ("here's my CPU"), you tell it WHAT to probe: /probe?target=https://anihpj.com&module=http_2xx. It returns: probe_success (1/0), probe_duration_seconds, probe_ssl_earliest_cert_expiry. This is external/blackbox monitoring — testing from the user's perspective.

  REGULAR EXPORTER                    BLACKBOX EXPORTER
  ────────────────                    ─────────────────
  App ──▶ Exporter ──▶ /metrics      Prom → /probe?target=X → Internet → X
  (internal metrics)                  (external check)
  
  PG Exporter:                        Blackbox Exporter:
  ┌──────┐   SQL    ┌─────────┐      ┌──────┐  HTTP GET  ┌──────────┐
  │  PG  │◀────────│   PG    │      │Prom  │──────────▶│ anihpj   │
  │  DB  │────────▶│Exporter │      │:9090 │◀─200 OK───│ .com     │
  └──────┘ metrics └────┬────┘      └──────┘           └──────────┘
                        │            Result:
                   :9187/metrics     probe_success=1
                                     probe_duration=0.34s
  Endpoint: /metrics ✅              Endpoint: /probe ✅ (NOT /metrics!)
Q4

What port does the PostgreSQL Exporter use?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

:9187

💡 EXPLANATION

Port numbers are PCA exam favorites. Memorize: Node Exporter=9100, PostgreSQL=9187, Redis=9121, Blackbox=9115, Nginx=9113, MySQL=9104, Elasticsearch=9114. The exam WILL ask: "Which port does the XYZ Exporter use?" These are easy points if you memorize them.

Q5

On K8s, how should you deploy Node Exporter?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

As a DaemonSet — one pod per node, monitoring that node's hardware.

💡 EXPLANATION

DaemonSet guarantees exactly one pod per node. Node Exporter needs to monitor each node's hardware (CPU, disk, network) — each node is unique. If deployed as Deployment, you might get 0 instances on some nodes and 3 on others. DaemonSet + hostNetwork + hostPID gives Node Exporter access to the node's /proc filesystem for accurate metrics.

Q6

Which Python library is used to create custom exporters?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

prometheus_client — provides Gauge, Counter, Histogram, Summary classes.

💡 EXPLANATION

pip install prometheus-client. Core classes: Counter (.inc()), Gauge (.set(), .inc(), .dec()), Histogram (.observe()), Summary (.observe()). Use start_http_server(8000) for standalone exporters, or integrate with web frameworks (django-prometheus for Django, prometheus_flask_exporter for Flask). Official libraries exist for Go, Java, Python, Ruby, Rust.

Q7

What port does the Redis Exporter use?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

:9121

💡 EXPLANATION

Redis Exporter (oliver006/redis_exporter) connects to a Redis instance and exposes: redis_connected_clients, redis_commands_processed_total, redis_memory_used_bytes, redis_keyspace_hits/misses_total. It queries Redis via the INFO command — no modification to Redis needed. Port 9121 is another memorization target for PCA.

Q8

Can you use the Blackbox Exporter to check SSL certificate expiry?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes. The Blackbox Exporter can probe HTTP, HTTPS, DNS, TCP, and ICMP — including SSL certificate validation and expiry checking via the http module.

💡 EXPLANATION

Blackbox modules: http_2xx (check HTTP 200), http_post_2xx (POST + check response), tcp_connect (check port is open), dns (check DNS resolution), icmp (ping). For SSL: use the http module and check probe_ssl_earliest_cert_expiry. Set alert: "cert expires in less than 7 days → warning, less than 1 day → critical."

Q9

Do exporters require modifying the application they monitor?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No. Exporters run as separate processes/sidecars. They connect to the application externally (via SQL, Redis protocol, etc.) — no application code changes needed.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is the key difference between Exporters and Client Libraries. Exporters = external process, no code changes. Client Libraries = add code to your app (Chapter 9). Exporters for databases, caches, infrastructure. Client libraries for custom application metrics. The PG Exporter queries PostgreSQL via SQL — PostgreSQL doesn't even know it exists. Same for Redis, Nginx, etc.

Q10

What are the key Node Exporter metric categories?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

CPU (node_cpu_seconds_total), Memory (node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes), Disk (node_filesystem_avail_bytes), Network (node_network_receive_bytes_total).

💡 EXPLANATION

Node Exporter exposes 1000+ metrics across these categories. CPU: node_cpu_seconds_total (per-mode: idle, system, user, iowait). Memory: MemAvailable (best for alerting), MemTotal, Buffers, Cached. Disk: filesystem_avail_bytes (free space), disk_io_time_seconds_total (I/O). Network: receive/transmit bytes, packets, errors, drops. These 4 categories = complete hardware visibility.

  • ❌ Confusing Blackbox /probe path with default /metrics path.
  • ❌ Deploying Node Exporter as Deployment instead of DaemonSet on K8s.
  • ❌ Forgetting that exporters run as SEPARATE processes — they don't modify the app.
  • ✅ Can you explain what an exporter does and why they're needed?
  • ✅ Do you know the default ports: Node=9100, PG=9187, Redis=9121, Blackbox=9115?
  • ✅ Can you write a basic Python exporter using prometheus_client?

Chapter 6: PromQL Fundamentals 🟡 Intermediate

Thinking in PromQL — The Language of Time-Series

PromQL is the query language of Prometheus. Unlike SQL which asks "What's the current state?", PromQL asks "How has this metric changed over time?" This chapter introduces instant vectors, range vectors, selectors, and matchers — the building blocks of every PromQL query. Mastering these fundamentals is essential for the PCA exam (28% weight).

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours💎 PCA 28%🔑 Core

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ PromQL vs SQL — key differences and when to use each
  • ✅ Instant Vectors — querying "right now" values
  • ✅ Range Vectors — querying values over time windows
  • ✅ 4 Selector types — exact match, not-equal, regex match, not-regex
  • ✅ Hands-on with the Prometheus Expression Browser at :9090
🎓 PCA Relevance
PromQL 28%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 6 Must-Knows
Instant VectorSingle value per series at query time. Example: http_requests_total{job="anihpj"}
Range VectorMultiple values over [duration]. Example: http_requests_total[5m] — adds [5m] at end!
4 Matchers= exact, != not, =~ regex match, !~ regex not. Exam trap: confusing = and =~

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — PromQL in Plain English

PromQL is NOT SQL. If you know SQL, forget everything for a moment:

1. SQL asks "What rows match this filter RIGHT NOW?" — SELECT * FROM requests WHERE status = 500. PromQL asks "How has this metric CHANGED over the last 5 minutes?" — rate(http_requests_total{status="500"}[5m]).

2. Instant Vector = Current value. Just the metric name and labels: up{job="anihpj"} → returns 1 or 0 for each target right now. One number per time series.

3. Range Vector = Values over time. Add [5m] at the end: up{job="anihpj"}[5m] → returns ~20 values (scraped every 15s × 5 minutes). Range vectors are INPUT to functions like rate(). You almost never use a range vector by itself — you feed it to rate(), increase(), etc.

4. The [5m] is MANDATORY for rate(). rate(metric) without [5m] will ERROR. This is the #1 beginner mistake. Always: rate(metric_name[5m]).

6.1 PromQL by Analogy — SQL for Time-Series

ConceptSQLPromQL
Query focus"What's the current state?""How has this changed over time?"
Data modelTables with rows/columnsTime series with labels
FilteringWHERE clauseLabel matchers in {}
AggregationGROUP BYsum/avg by (label) / without (label)
Time rangeBETWEEN timestamps[5m] range vector selector

6.2 Instant Vectors — A Snapshot in Time

An instant vector returns the single most recent value for each matching time series. Think of it as: "What is the value RIGHT NOW?"

Instant Vector Examples
# All HTTP requests for anihpj — returns ONE value per time series
http_requests_total{job="anihpj-django"}

# CPU usage per node — returns ONE value per node
node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}

# Active job listings (Gauge) — ONE value
jobpost_active_listings

6.3 Range Vectors — A Window of Time

A range vector returns ALL values within a time window. Created by adding [duration] to an instant vector selector. Range vectors are required as input for rate(), increase(), avg_over_time(), etc.

Range Vector Examples
# ALL values from the last 5 minutes
http_requests_total{job="anihpj-django"}[5m]

# ALL values from the last 1 hour
node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}[1h]

# Then use with functions:
rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj-django"}[5m])   # per-second rate
increase(http_requests_total[1h])                      # total increase

6.4 Selectors and Matchers — Finding Your Data

MatcherSyntaxExamplePCA Note
Exact match=method="GET"Most common
Not equal!=method!="POST"Excludes matching
Regex match=~endpoint=~"/api/.*"CAUTION: not ==~
Not regex!~endpoint!~"/healthz|/readyz"Exclude by pattern

6.5 The PromQL Expression Browser

Access at http://localhost:9090/graph. Use the Table view for exact values, Graph view for trends. Auto-complete helps with metric names and labels.

  • Instant vs Range trick: Function needs to see CHANGE? → Range vector [d]. Just current value? → Instant. rate(), increase(), _over_time ALL need [d].
  • 4 Matchers mnemonic: = exact, != not, =~ regex yes, !~ regex no. Tilde = regex. Exam trap: ==~ is INVALID.
  • [5m] = standard: 15s scrape → 20 samples. Shorter = jumpy. Longer = smooth but slow to react.
  • Expression Browser: :9090/graph — Table for values, Graph for trends. Ctrl+Space = auto-complete. Not for production (use Grafana).
🔧 Troubleshooting: "My PromQL query returns nothing!"

Step 1: Check label matchers — using = when you need =~? Step 2: Data older than retention? Step 3: Metric exists? Try {__name__=~".*your_metric.*"}. Step 4: Staleness — target stopped scraping >5 min ago? Step 5: Missing [duration] on rate()? Syntax errors = empty result in some UIs.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 6 (Fundamentals)

Q1

What's the difference between an instant vector and a range vector?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Instant vector = single value per series at query time. Range vector = multiple values over a time window (created by adding [5m]).

💡 EXPLANATION

This is THE most fundamental PromQL distinction. http_requests_total → instant vector (one value per series). http_requests_total[5m] → range vector (~20 values at 15s scrape). Range vectors are INPUT to rate(), increase(), avg_over_time(). Without [duration], rate() errors. PCA exam WILL test: "What does rate() require as input?" → Answer: range vector.

  INSTANT VECTOR = One snapshot        RANGE VECTOR = Time window
  ─────────────────────────────        ──────────────────────────
  metric{job="X"}                      metric{job="X"}[5m]
  ┌──────────────────┐                ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ Now: 247 req     │                │ T-5m: 200  T-1m: 245│
  │ One value ONLY   │                │ T-4m: 215  T-0s: 247│
  └──────────────────┘                │ T-3m: 230  ...      │
                                       │ T-2m: 240  ~20 vals│
  Grafana: Stat panel ✅              └──────────────────────┘
  Alerting: comparison ✅
                                       rate() input ✅ ← MUST have [d]!
  rate() input ❌ (needs range!)       increase() input ✅
                                       _over_time input ✅
Q2

Which operator matches labels using regex?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

=~ (regex match) — NOT ==~ (common exam trap!).

💡 EXPLANATION

4 matcher types: = exact, != not-equal, =~ regex match, !~ regex-not. The tilde (~) means regex. PCA exam will confuse you with ==~ as a distractor — that's invalid syntax! Regex uses RE2 syntax: "5.." matches "500", "501", "502". "/api/.*" matches all /api/ endpoints.

Q3

Can you use rate() on an instant vector?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No. rate() requires a RANGE vector. You must add [duration]: rate(metric[5m]).

💡 EXPLANATION

rate() needs to see HOW the counter changed over time, so it needs MULTIPLE samples (a range vector). Without [5m], Prometheus can't calculate a rate — it only has one value. This is the #1 beginner syntax error. Always: rate(counter[DURATION]). The same applies to irate(), increase(), and all _over_time() functions.

Q4

Write a query to select all HTTP requests with status codes 500-599.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}

💡 EXPLANATION

Regex pattern 5..: "5" followed by any two characters ("." = any char in RE2). Matches 500, 501, 502, ..., 599. Equivalent to writing {status="500"} or {status="501"} or ... but much cleaner. The PCA exam expects you to recognize regex patterns in label matchers and distinguish = from =~ from !~.

Q5

How do you exclude /healthz and /readyz endpoints?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

http_requests_total{endpoint!~"/healthz|/readyz"}

💡 EXPLANATION

!~ = regex NOT match. The | inside regex means OR. So this says: "match any endpoint that does NOT have /healthz OR /readyz." This is how you filter out noise. Alternative: use {endpoint!="healthz",endpoint!="readyz"} with multiple exact not-equal matchers (both labels must match, so it excludes both).

Q6

What does http_requests_total{job="anihpj"}[5m] return?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A range vector — all values scraped in the last 5 minutes (~20 samples at 15s intervals).

💡 EXPLANATION

Adding [5m] transforms an INSTANT vector into a RANGE vector. At 15s scrape interval: 5min ÷ 15s = 20 samples. At 30s interval: 10 samples. More samples = smoother rate() but uses more memory during computation. Standard [5m] is sufficient for most queries. [1m] is too jumpy; [1h] may mask recent changes.

Q7

What's wrong with rate(http_requests_total)?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Missing [duration]. Correct: rate(http_requests_total[5m]). rate() requires a range vector.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is a classic PCA exam distractor. The exam will show you rate(some_metric) and ask "what's wrong?" The answer is always: missing [duration]. Similarly, increase(metric) without [1h], avg_over_time(metric) without [5m] — all will error. Memorize: functions that operate OVER TIME always need a range vector [d].

Q8

How does PromQL differ from SQL fundamentally?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

SQL queries current state of tables. PromQL queries how metrics change over time. PromQL has no JOINs — each query operates independently on time series.

💡 EXPLANATION

SQL: SELECT * FROM requests WHERE status = 500 — "What rows have status 500 right now?" PromQL: rate(http_requests_total{status="500"}[5m]) — "How fast are 500 errors accumulating?" SQL is about STATE; PromQL is about CHANGE. No JOINs — PromQL uses label matching (on, ignoring, group_left/right) for cross-series operations. This distinction appears on the exam.

Q9

What label matcher selects all endpoints starting with /api/?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

{endpoint=~"/api/.*"} — regex match operator is =~, pattern uses .* for wildcard.

💡 EXPLANATION

RE2 regex syntax: .* = any characters (zero or more), .+ = one or more, \d+ = digits. Anchors: ^/api/ (starts with) and $" (ends with) are implicit. The regex is matched against the FULL label value. PCA exam may show regex like "prod|staging" and ask which environments match.

Q10

Where do you access the PromQL Expression Browser?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

http://localhost:9090/graph — the Prometheus UI's built-in query interface. Use Table view for values, Graph for trends.

💡 EXPLANATION

The Prometheus Expression Browser at :9090/graph is where you practice PromQL. Table view shows exact numeric values (best for debugging). Graph view shows trends (best for understanding patterns). Auto-complete (Ctrl+Space) helps with metric names and labels. This is a development tool — production dashboards use Grafana, not the expression browser.

  • ❌ Confusing = (exact match) with =~ (regex match). Regex uses tilde!
  • ❌ Forgetting [5m] after rate() — causes syntax error.
  • ❌ Thinking PromQL works like SQL — no JOINs, no WHERE clauses, no SELECT.
  • ❌ Using ==~ for regex — correct syntax is =~ (single equals + tilde).
  • ✅ Can you distinguish instant vectors (current value) from range vectors ([5m] window)?
  • ✅ Do you know all 4 matcher types: =, !=, =~, !~?
  • ✅ Can you write rate(metric[5m]) without forgetting the [duration]?

Chapter 7: PromQL Operators & Functions 🟡 Intermediate

The Complete Toolbox — 80% of PCA PromQL Lives Here

Master rate(), irate(), increase(), histogram_quantile(), aggregation operators, binary operators, _over_time functions, and more. This is THE HEART of the PCA exam — 28% of all questions will test your PromQL knowledge.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~4 hours💎 PCA 28%🔑 CRITICAL

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — PromQL Operators Made Simple

If Chapter 6 taught you the vocabulary (instant vector, range vector, matchers), this chapter teaches you the grammar — how to combine pieces into meaningful sentences. Here's how to think about the key concepts:

1. rate() = Speedometer. Your car's odometer (Counter) always goes up. rate() converts "total miles" to "miles per hour." rate(http_requests_total[5m]) = "how many requests per second in the last 5 minutes?" Without rate(), the graph always trends upward — useless. With rate(), you see real traffic patterns.

2. sum() by() = Grouping. You have 3 web pods, each reporting request counts. sum by (endpoint) says: "add up all 3 pods' numbers, but keep the endpoint label separate." Result: one number per endpoint (combined across all pods), not 3 per endpoint. This is how dashboards work.

3. histogram_quantile() = The percentile calculator. Your histogram tracks: "how many requests took ≤0.1s? ≤0.5s? ≤1s?" histogram_quantile(0.99, ...) answers: "what's the SLOWEST response time for the fastest 99%?" Always feed it rate()'d buckets — NEVER raw buckets!

4. label_replace() = Renaming tool. Instance names like "10.244.1.5:8000" are ugly on dashboards. label_replace() can extract "10.244.1.5" or rename labels entirely. Think of it as PromQL's string manipulation.

5. offset = Time machine. Want to compare today's traffic to last week? rate(requests[5m]) / rate(requests[5m] offset 1w) → "1.0 = same as last week, 1.5 = 50% growth." Essential for anomaly detection.

7.1 Arithmetic Operators

+ - * / % ^ — Do math on metrics. "What's the error RATIO?"

PromQL
rate(http_errors_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]) / rate(http_total[5m])  # Error ratio
node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes / 1024 / 1024 / 1024                 # GB available

7.2 Comparison Operators — Filtering by Condition

📐SYNTAX — Comparison Operators
# Comparison operators: == != > < >= <=
# They FILTER time series — only keep those where the condition is true.
# Result is the SAME type as input (instant vector → instant vector).

# == equal — select exact matches
http_requests_total{status="200"}    # Only HTTP 200 responses

# != not equal — exclude matches
up{job="anihpj"} != 1                 # All targets that are NOT up

# > greater than — alerting threshold
rate(http_errors_total[5m]) > 0.1     # Error rate exceeds 0.1/s

# < less than — detect low values
node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes < 1073741824  # Less than 1GB free

# >= greater or equal — inclusive threshold
rate(http_requests_total[5m]) >= 100  # 100+ requests/sec

# <= less or equal — inclusive ceiling
histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m])) <= 0.5  # p99 under 500ms

# Comparison in ALERTING (the primary use case):
#   alert: HighErrorRate
#   expr: rate(http_500s[5m]) / rate(http_total[5m]) > 0.05
#         ↑ comparison triggers the alert

# Comparison on BOOLEAN output (0 or 1):
#   rate(errors[5m]) > bool 5
#   → Returns 1 for series above 5, 0 for below (instead of filtering)
#   Use: count how many series exceed a threshold.

# ⚠️  Comparisons return FILTERED vectors — missing series are dropped.
# ⚠️  Use > bool / < bool to get 0/1 instead of filtering.
# ⚠️  PCA: "Which operator is used in alerting rule expr:" → comparison operators!
✅ WITHOUT bool (filtering)
  • cpu_usage > 80
  • Returns: ONLY series where cpu > 80
  • Other series disappear from result
  • Use in: alerting rules, Grafana filters
🔢 WITH bool (counting)
  • cpu_usage > bool 80
  • Returns: 1 where cpu>80, 0 elsewhere
  • ALL series remain in result
  • Use: count(scalar) > N patterns

7.3 Aggregation Operators — sum/avg/min/max by/without

📐SYNTAX — Aggregation Operators
# Aggregate ACROSS time series (combine multiple series into fewer)
# Syntax: <aggregator> [by|without (<label-list>)] (<expression>)

<aggregator>: sum | avg | min | max | count | stddev | stdvar
               | topk | bottomk | quantile | count_values

# by(label1,label2) — KEEP these labels, aggregate everything else
# Example: sum by (endpoint) (rate(http_total[5m]))
#          → One result per unique endpoint value

# without(label1,label2) — DROP these labels, keep everything else
# Example: sum without (instance) (rate(http_total[5m]))
#          → One result per {job,endpoint} combo

# 💡 PCA TIP: by() = "GROUP BY" (keep named). without() = "DROP" (remove named).
#             They are OPPOSITES — exam WILL test this distinction!
AGGREGATION — BEFORE AND AFTER (3 pods × 2 endpoints)
  BEFORE: 6 time series                     AFTER sum(...) by (endpoint):
  {pod="web-1", endpoint="/api/jobs"}  45    {endpoint="/api/jobs"}  → 145
  {pod="web-2", endpoint="/api/jobs"}  52    {endpoint="/api/skills"}→  38
  {pod="web-3", endpoint="/api/jobs"}  48
  {pod="web-1", endpoint="/api/skills"}12    KEY: by(X) = KEEP X, aggregate rest
  {pod="web-2", endpoint="/api/skills"}15         without(X) = DROP X, keep rest
  {pod="web-3", endpoint="/api/skills"}11

7.4 rate(), irate(), increase() — The Holy Trinity

📐SYNTAX — Counter Functions (MUST use on Counters ONLY)
# rate(v range-vector) — per-second AVERAGE rate over the time window
#   Calculates: (last_value - first_value) / window_seconds
#   Smooth, stable — BEST for dashboards and alerting
#   Example: rate(http_requests_total[5m]) → req/sec averaged over 5 min

# irate(v range-vector) — per-second INSTANT rate (last 2 samples only)
#   Calculates: (last_sample - prev_sample) / sample_interval
#   Spiky, responsive — BEST for debugging sudden changes
#   Example: irate(http_requests_total[5m]) → req/sec from last 2 points

# increase(v range-vector) — TOTAL increase over the time window
#   Calculates: (last_value - first_value) with extrapolation
#   Integer-like result — BEST for "how many in the last hour?"
#   Example: increase(http_requests_total[1h]) → total requests in 1h

# ⚠️  rate() and increase() extrapolate to window edges — handles counter resets.
# ⚠️  irate() is NOT suitable for alerting (too spiky, misses trends).
# ⚠️  ALL three REQUIRE a range vector [d] — without it, SYNTAX ERROR!
# ⚠️  NEVER use on Gauges — returns mathematically meaningless garbage.
RATE vs INCREASE vs IRATE
  rate(http_total[5m])     → Per-second AVERAGE over 5 min (SMOOTH — for graphs/alerts)
  irate(http_total[5m])    → Per-second rate using LAST 2 points (SPIKY — for debugging)
  increase(http_total[1h]) → TOTAL increase over 1 hour (ABSOLUTE — "how many in last hour?")
  
  ⚠️ GOLDEN RULE: NEVER use rate() on a Gauge! rate() requires a Counter (monotonically increasing).
     Applying rate() to a Gauge returns garbage data — this is the #1 PCA exam trap!

7.5 Histogram Functions — histogram_quantile()

📐SYNTAX — Histogram Functions
# histogram_quantile(φ scalar, v instant-vector) — calculate φ-quantile from buckets
#   φ: quantile between 0 and 1 (0.50=p50, 0.95=p95, 0.99=p99)
#   v: rate()'d histogram buckets aggregated by (le)
#
# CRITICAL PIPELINE (every step required):
#   1. Raw _bucket counter → 2. rate(bucket[5m]) → 3. sum by(le) → 4. histogram_quantile()
#
# Example — p99 latency:
#   histogram_quantile(0.99,
#     sum by (le) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])))
#
# ⚠️  MUST rate() buckets FIRST — raw buckets are cumulative counters!
# ⚠️  MUST sum by(le) to preserve bucket boundaries across instances!
# ⚠️  The 'le' label IS the bucket boundary — dropping it destroys the histogram!

# histogram_fraction(lower, upper, v) — fraction of observations in [lower,upper]
#   Less common. Know it exists for PCA.
HISTOGRAM BUCKETS — HOW THEY WORK
  Buckets: 0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.5, 1.0, +Inf
  Observed: 0.008, 0.012, 0.045, 0.08, 0.09, 0.12, 0.45, 0.55, 0.6, 1.2
  
  ≤0.01: █ 1   ≤0.05: ███ 3   ≤0.1: █████ 5   ≤0.5: ████████ 8   ≤1.0: █████████ 9   +Inf: 10
  
  histogram_quantile(0.50, rate(bucket[5m])) = p50 ≈ 0.1s
  histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m])) = p99 ≈ 1.2s
  
  ✅ CORRECT: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m]))
  ❌ WRONG:   histogram_quantile(0.99, bucket)  ← ALWAYS rate() first!

7.6 Time-Based Functions

📐SYNTAX — Time-Based Functions
# predict_linear(v range-vector, t scalar) — predict value t seconds in the future
#   Uses linear regression on the data in v to extrapolate forward
#   Example: predict_linear(node_filesystem_free_bytes[1h], 4*3600) < 0
#            → "Will disk be full in 4 hours based on the last hour's trend?"
#   ⚠️  PCA favorite! Combines function knowledge + alert logic.

# delta(v range-vector) — difference between last and first value (for GAUGES)
#   Example: delta(node_memory_available[1h]) → how much memory changed in 1h
#   ⚠️  Use delta() for Gauges, NOT for Counters (use increase() for counters).

# idelta(v range-vector) — difference between last TWO samples (for GAUGES)
#   Like irate() but for Gauges — instant change between most recent samples.

# resets(v range-vector) — number of counter resets in the time window
#   Use: detect application restarts. Counter drops to 0 → reset detected.
#   Example: resets(http_requests_total[1h]) → 2 (app restarted twice)

# changes(v range-vector) — number of times the value changed in the window
#   Use: detect instability/flapping. Example: changes(up[5m]) → 5

# deriv(v range-vector) — per-second derivative using linear regression
#   Use: detect trends (memory leaks, gradual disk growth). Smoother than delta().
#   Example: deriv(container_memory_working_set_bytes[6h]) > 0 → memory growing

# ⚠️  delta/idelta = Gauges. increase/rate = Counters. Never cross them!
# ⚠️  predict_linear needs at least 2 data points in the range vector.
# ⚠️  deriv() uses linear regression — smoother than delta() for noisy data.
FunctionWhat It DoesPCA Note
offset 1dCompare with yesterdayCommon exam pattern
predict_linear()Predict disk full timeKnow the formula
resets()Detect counter resets (app restart)Debugging tool
delta() / idelta()Difference for GaugesFor Gauges, not Counters

7.7 Subqueries & @ Modifier — Advanced Time Manipulation

📐SYNTAX — Subquery & @ Modifier
# SUBQUERY: <instant-vector-expression>[<range>:<resolution>]
#   Runs the inner expression at <resolution> intervals over <range> duration.
#   Returns a RANGE VECTOR of the inner expression's results.
#
#   Example: rate(http_requests_total[5m])[1h:1m]
#     Inner: rate(http_requests_total[5m]) — 5-min rate (instant vector)
#     Outer: [...[1h:1m]] — run it every 1 minute for the last hour
#     Result: 60 data points (one rate value per minute)
#
#   Example: max_over_time(rate(http_errors[5m])[1h:5m])
#     → "What was the highest 5-min error rate in the last hour?"
#     → Checks the error rate every 5 min for 1h, returns the maximum.
#
#   Example: avg_over_time(memory_usage[6h:1h])
#     → "What was the average memory usage over 6 hours, sampled hourly?"

# @ MODIFIER: <expression> @ <unix_timestamp>
#   Evaluates the expression AT a specific Unix timestamp instead of "now."
#
#   Example: http_requests_total @ 1609459200
#     → "What was the value at Jan 1, 2021 00:00:00 UTC?"
#
#   Example: rate(http_requests_total[5m] @ (time() - 3600))
#     → "What was the 5-min rate as of 1 hour ago?"
#
#   Example: http_requests_total @ end()
#     → "What was the value at the end of the query's time range?"

# ⚠️  Subquery resolution determines how many data points you get.
#     [1h:1m] = 60 points. [1h:5m] = 12 points. [1h:30s] = 120 points.
# ⚠️  @ modifier does NOT shift the range — only the evaluation timestamp.
# ⚠️  Combine subquery + @: rate(metric[5m] @ 1609459200)[1h:1m]
#     → "1-hour subquery of 5-min rates, all evaluated at Jan 1, 2021"
🔄 SUBQUERY — How It Works Step by Step
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SUBQUERY: rate(http_requests_total[5m])[30m:5m]                             │
  │                                                                               │
  │  Timeline (last 30 minutes):                                                  │
  │                                                                               │
  │  T-30m          T-25m          T-20m          T-15m          T-10m           │
  │    │              │              │              │              │              │
  │    ▼              ▼              ▼              ▼              ▼              │
  │  [───5m───]    [───5m───]    [───5m───]    [───5m───]    [───5m───]         │
  │  rate=8 r/s    rate=12 r/s   rate=11 r/s   rate=9 r/s    rate=14 r/s        │
  │                                                                               │
  │  RESULT: Range vector with 6 values: [8, 12, 11, 9, 14, 10]                  │
  │                                                                               │
  │  NOW you can apply _over_time functions to this range vector:                │
  │    max_over_time(rate(http_total[5m])[30m:5m]) → 14 (peak)                   │
  │    avg_over_time(rate(http_total[5m])[30m:5m]) → 10.7 (average)             │
  │                                                                               │
  │  💡 Without subquery: you'd need a recording rule OR external tool.           │
  │     With subquery: it's one line of PromQL!                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⏰ @ MODIFIER — Time Travel in PromQL
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  @ MODIFIER: Evaluate at a specific point in time                              │
  │                                                                               │
  │  Normal query:                    @ Modifier query:                           │
  │  ─────────────                    ────────────────                            │
  │  rate(metric[5m])                 rate(metric[5m] @ 1609459200)               │
  │  "Rate RIGHT NOW"                 "Rate at Jan 1, 2021 00:00 UTC"            │
  │                                                                               │
  │  USE CASES:                                                                    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ 🔍 Debugging:                                                         │     │
  │  │    metric @ 1700000000 → "What was the value when the incident        │     │
  │  │                          started at 3:33 PM?"                         │     │
  │  │                                                                        │     │
  │  │ 📊 Comparing:                                                          │     │
  │  │    metric / (metric @ (time() - 86400)) → "How does NOW compare       │     │
  │  │                                            to 24h ago?"               │     │
  │  │                                                                        │     │
  │  │ 🕐 Range boundaries:                                                   │     │
  │  │    metric @ start() → "Value at dashboard's start time"               │     │
  │  │    metric @ end()   → "Value at dashboard's end time"                 │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
PCA Exam: Subqueries & @ Modifier

The PCA exam increasingly tests subqueries. Know the syntax: expression[range:resolution]. The inner expression must return an instant vector. The outer brackets convert it to a range vector. Common distractor: rate(metric[5m][1h:1m]) — WRONG (double brackets). Correct: rate(metric[5m])[1h:1m]. For @ modifier: the timestamp goes AFTER the expression, and it works with BOTH instant and range vectors.

7.8 Staleness — Why Metrics Disappear (PCA Exam Topic!)

⏳ STALENESS — The 5-Minute Lookback Window
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     STALENESS TIMELINE — Pod Death Scenario                    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  T-15s: Prom scrapes pod → up=1, cpu=45%    ← LAST SUCCESSFUL SCRAPE         │
  │  T-5s:  Pod DIES 💀                                                           │
  │  T=0s:  Prom tries to scrape → FAILS (connection refused)                     │
  │         up{} is NOW 0 (failed scrape)                                         │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │  THE 5-MINUTE LOOKBACK WINDOW (default --query.lookback-delta=5m)            │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  T+1m:   Query cpu_usage → returns 45% ← LAST known value (lookback!)        │
  │  T+2m:   Query cpu_usage → returns 45% ← STILL returning cached value         │
  │  T+3m:   Query cpu_usage → returns 45%                                        │
  │  T+4m:   Query cpu_usage → returns 45%                                        │
  │  T+5m:   Query cpu_usage → returns 45% ← JUST before staleness kicks in      │
  │  T+5m1s: Query cpu_usage → NOTHING (empty) ← STALE! Series disappeared!      │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │  KEY INSIGHT:                                                                 │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  up{} == 1 → returns 1 for 0-5min after death (lookback returns last=1)     │
  │  up{} == 0 → returns 1 IMMEDIATELY after failed scrape ✅                     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ❌ ALERTING on up{} == 1 → 5 MINUTE DELAY before alert fires!               │
  │  ✅ ALERTING on up{} == 0 → instant detection (within 1 scrape interval)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📐 Staleness — Configuration & Best Practices
# STALENESS DEFAULT: 5 minutes (--query.lookback-delta=5m)
#   This is why metrics "linger" for 5 min after a target disappears.

# CORRECT dead-pod detection (instant — within 1 scrape interval):
alert: PodDown
expr: up{job="anihpj"} == 0
for: 1m   # Wait 1 min to avoid false alarms from brief network issues

# WRONG dead-pod detection (5-minute delay!):
alert: PodDown_WRONG
expr: up{job="anihpj"} == 1   # ← returns 1 for 5 min after death!
#  unless absent(up{job="anihpj"})   # still broken — see below

# DETECTING COMPLETELY DELETED JOBS (no instances at all):
alert: JobHasNoInstances
expr: absent(up{job="anihpj"}) == 1
#   ↑ This is the ONLY way! up{} == 0 returns NOTHING if the job has 0 instances.

# STALENESS CONFIGURATION (advanced — rarely changed):
#   --query.lookback-delta=5m   (default: 5 minutes)
#   Shorter = faster cleanup but may cause gaps in graphs.
#   Longer = more resilience but slower to notice dead targets.

# DETECT STALE METRICS IN DASHBOARDS:
#   timestamp(up{job="anihpj"}) — returns Unix timestamp of last scrape
#   time() - timestamp(up) > 300 → "not scraped in 5+ minutes"
❌ DON'T: Detect dead pods with up == 1
  • up==1 returns 1 for 5 MINUTES after pod death (lookback window)
  • Your alert has a 5-minute blind spot!
  • If pod restarts quickly, you may never notice
  • Worse: up==1 with absent() can miss edge cases
✅ DO: Detect dead pods with up == 0
  • up==0 returns IMMEDIATELY after first failed scrape
  • Combined with for:1m to filter transient network issues
  • Use absent(up{job="X"}) for jobs that should exist but don't
  • Use timestamp(up) to find stale metrics on dashboards
PCA Exam: Staleness is a GUARANTEED topic

The exam WILL test: (1) How long after a pod dies do its metrics disappear? → 5 minutes (lookback delta). (2) Why does up==1 return 1 for 5 min after death? → lookback returns last known value. (3) Which expression detects dead pods INSTANTLY? → up==0. (4) How to detect a job with zero instances? → absent(up{job="X"}). Know the difference between "pod is down" (up==0) and "job has no pods at all" (absent). These are separate PCA concepts!

7.9 Aggregating OVER TIME — _over_time Functions

These are DIFFERENT from aggregation operators! Aggregation (sum/avg) works ACROSS series. _over_time functions work WITHIN a single series over time:

📐SYNTAX — _over_time Functions (all take a range vector → return instant vector)
# avg_over_time(range-vector) — average of all values in the time window
#   Example: avg_over_time(node_cpu_usage[1h]) → avg CPU over last hour

# max_over_time(range-vector) — maximum value in the time window
#   Example: max_over_time(http_latency[1h]) → peak latency in last hour

# min_over_time(range-vector) — minimum value in the time window
#   Example: min_over_time(memory_available[1h]) → lowest memory in last hour

# count_over_time(range-vector) — number of samples in the time window
#   Example: count_over_time(up[1h]) → how many scrapes in the last hour?

# quantile_over_time(φ, range-vector) — φ-quantile of values in the window
#   Example: quantile_over_time(0.95, cpu_usage[1h]) → p95 CPU over last hour

# stddev_over_time / stdvar_over_time — standard deviation / variance

# ⚠️  _over_time = WITHIN one series (vertical reduction over time)
# ⚠️  sum/avg by() = ACROSS series (horizontal reduction at one instant)
# ⚠️  PCA WILL test this distinction! "Which reduces over time vs across series?"

7.10 Binary Operators & Vector Matching

Arithmetic (+ - * / % ^), Comparison (== != > < >= <=), Logical (and or unless). Vector matching with on(), ignoring(), group_left, group_right.

📐SYNTAX — Vector Matching Keywords
# Vector matching controls HOW Prometheus pairs time series when doing math.

# on(label1, label2, ...) — match ONLY on specified labels
#   Example: rate(cpu[5m]) / on(instance) machine_cpu_cores
#   Left has {instance, mode}, Right has {instance} → match on instance only

# ignoring(label1, label2, ...) — match on ALL labels EXCEPT specified
#   Example: rate(errors[5m]) / ignoring(status) rate(total[5m])
#   Left has {job, endpoint, status="5xx"}, Right has {job, endpoint, status="all"}
#   → Match on job+endpoint, ignore the status mismatch

# group_left / group_right — many-to-one or one-to-many matching
#   group_left: left side has MORE series — add right labels to all of them
#   Example: rate(cpu_per_mode[5m]) / on(instance) group_left cpu_cores
#   → Each CPU mode gets divided by the same core count
#   group_right: right side has MORE series (less common)

# ⚠️  Without on()/ignoring(), Prometheus matches on ALL shared labels.
# ⚠️  If labels don't match → ERROR: "multiple matches for labels"
# ⚠️  PCA: "Which keyword controls which labels to match on?" → on()/ignoring()

7.11 Timestamp Metrics — When Was This Last Scraped?

📐SYNTAX — Timestamp & Time Functions
# timestamp(v instant-vector) — returns Unix timestamp of each sample
#   Returns the TIME when each metric value was scraped (in seconds since epoch)
#   Example: timestamp(up{job="anihpj"}) → 1700000000 (Jan 14, 2023)

# time() — returns the CURRENT query evaluation time (scalar)
#   Example: time() → 1700000300 (right now)

# STALE TARGET DETECTION — targets not scraped recently:
#   time() - timestamp(up{job="anihpj"}) > 300
#   → "More than 5 minutes since last successful scrape"
#   → Use in dashboards to highlight stale targets

# LAST SCRAPE AGE (in seconds):
#   time() - timestamp(up)
#   → For a healthy target: ~15 (scraped 15 seconds ago)
#   → For a dead target: grows until 300, then series disappears (staleness)

# AGE OF A METRIC VALUE:
#   timestamp(metric) — when was this sample collected?
#   Useful for: identifying lag, debugging scrape delays

# COMPARING SCRAPE TIMESTAMPS ACROSS TARGETS:
#   max by (job) (time() - timestamp(up))
#   → "Which job has the stalest data?"

# ⚠️  timestamp() works on instant vectors, not range vectors.
# ⚠️  timestamp() returns the SAMPLE timestamp, not the query time.
# ⚠️  Combine with staleness: after 5 min, timestamp() returns nothing!
# ⚠️  PCA: "How to find targets not scraped in 5 min?" → time()-timestamp(up)>300
⏱️ TIMESTAMP — Real-World Debugging
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  time() - timestamp(up) — How Fresh Is Each Target's Data?                    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  HEALTHY TARGETS:                         STALE TARGETS:                      │
  │  ────────────────                         ──────────────                      │
  │  web-1: time()-ts = 12s ✅                web-3: time()-ts = 180s ⚠️           │
  │  web-2: time()-ts = 8s  ✅                pg-exp: time()-ts = 290s 🚨          │
  │  pg-db: time()-ts = 15s ✅                                                     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  GRAFANA STAT PANEL: "Oldest scrape: {{ max(time()-timestamp(up)) }}s"       │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ALERT RULE:                                                                   │
  │    alert: StaleTarget                                                          │
  │    expr: time() - timestamp(up) > 120                                         │
  │    for: 1m                                                                     │
  │    annotations:                                                                │
  │      summary: "Target {{ $labels.instance }} not scraped in 2+ minutes"       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • timestamp vs time: timestamp(up) = when Prometheus LAST scraped that target. time() = RIGHT NOW. The difference = scrape age.
  • Staleness companion: timestamp() is the companion function to staleness (s7-8). timestamp() shows HOW stale; staleness shows WHEN it disappears.
  • Dashboard trick: Add a Grafana Stat panel showing max(time()-timestamp(up)) — instantly see the stalest target.

7.12 Label Manipulation — label_replace() & label_join()

📐SYNTAX — Label Manipulation Functions
# label_replace(v instant-vector, dst_label, replacement, src_label, regex)
#   Extracts portion of src_label using regex, stores in dst_label
#   Parameters:
#     v             — input time series (instant vector)
#     dst_label     — NEW label name to create/overwrite
#     replacement   — replacement string with $1,$2 capture groups
#     src_label     — EXISTING label to extract from
#     regex         — RE2 regex with capture groups (parentheses)
#   Example: label_replace(up, "host", "$1", "instance", "(.*):9100")
#
# label_join(v instant-vector, dst_label, separator, src_label1, src_label2, ...)
#   Joins multiple label values into one new label
#   Example: label_join(up, "full_id", "-", "namespace", "pod")
#            → full_id="anihpj-web-7d4f8"
#
# ⚠️  label_replace does NOT modify existing labels — it creates a NEW one.
# ⚠️  If regex doesn't match, original time series is passed through unchanged.
# ⚠️  Use $1, $2, etc. (not \1, \2) for capture groups in the replacement.
🔧 LABEL MANIPULATION — Transforming Labels on the Fly
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  label_replace(v instant-vector, dst_label string, replacement string,       │
  │                src_label string, regex string)                                │
  │                                                                               │
  │  EXAMPLE 1 — Extract HTTP method from __name__ label:                         │
  │    label_replace(http_requests_total, "method", "$1", "__name__",             │
  │                  "http_(GET|POST|PUT|DELETE)_.*")                              │
  │    → Adds label {method="GET"} extracted from metric name "http_GET_total"   │
  │                                                                               │
  │  EXAMPLE 2 — Rename a label:                                                  │
  │    label_replace(up, "hostname", "$1", "instance", "(.*):9100")               │
  │    → Creates new label hostname="web-01" from instance="web-01:9100"         │
  │                                                                               │
  │  label_join(v instant-vector, dst_label string, separator string,             │
  │             src_label_1, src_label_2, ...)                                     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  EXAMPLE 3 — Combine labels:                                                  │
  │    label_join(up, "full_id", "-", "namespace", "pod")                         │
  │    → Creates label full_id="anihpj-web-7d4f8" by joining namespace+pod       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

PCA TRAP: label_replace uses $1, $2 for regex capture groups. The replacement is applied to the src_label value using the regex. If the regex doesn't match, the original label is kept unchanged. Use these for: extracting info from metric names, cleaning up instance labels, creating composite keys for dashboard variables.

7.13 Sorting & Top-K — Finding Outliers Fast

📐SYNTAX — Sorting Functions
# topk(k int, v instant-vector) — returns the k time series with LARGEST values
#   Example: topk(5, sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint))
#            → Top 5 endpoints by request rate

# bottomk(k int, v instant-vector) — returns the k time series with SMALLEST values
#   Example: bottomk(3, up) → 3 targets with lowest up value (likely 0 = down)

# sort(v instant-vector) — returns time series sorted by value ASCENDING
#   Example: sort(sum(rate(errors[5m])) by (endpoint))

# sort_desc(v instant-vector) — returns time series sorted by value DESCENDING
#   Example: sort_desc(sum(rate(errors[5m])) by (endpoint))

# ⚠️  topk/bottomk return top N at EACH evaluation step — may change over time!
# ⚠️  All sorting functions operate on instant vectors, not range vectors.
# ⚠️  PCA: "Which function shows the 3 busiest endpoints?" → topk(3, ...)
Sorting Functions — Instant Answers for "What's the worst?"
# TOP 5 endpoints by request rate
topk(5, sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint))

# BOTTOM 5 endpoints (least traffic — maybe unused?)
bottomk(5, sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint))

# Sort ALL series ascending (find the quietest)
sort(sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint))

# Sort descending (find the busiest)
sort_desc(sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint))
🧠 When to Use Which?
Which sorting function should I use?
Need exact top N values?
topk(N, expr) — Returns N largest. Example: top 3 CPU consumers.
Need exact bottom N values?
bottomk(N, expr) — Returns N smallest. Example: 3 least-used endpoints.
Just want everything ranked?
sort(expr) or sort_desc(expr) — Full ranking.

IMPORTANT: topk/bottomk return the top N time series from EACH evaluation step. In a dashboard showing 1 hour, topk(3, ...) may show DIFFERENT pods at different times — the top 3 at each point, not necessarily the same 3 pods all hour. This is normal and expected behavior!

7.14 Vector Matching Deep Dive — on(), ignoring(), group_left, group_right

🔗 VECTOR MATCHING — 4 Patterns You MUST Know
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PATTERN 1: ONE-TO-ONE (labels already match)                                 │
  │    rate(http_errors[5m]) / rate(http_total[5m])                               │
  │    Both have {job, endpoint, status} → Prometheus auto-matches all labels    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PATTERN 2: ONE-TO-ONE with on() — match on SPECIFIC labels                  │
  │    rate(cpu_time[5m]) / on(instance) machine_cpu_cores                        │
  │    Left: {instance, mode}   Right: {instance}                                 │
  │    on(instance) tells Prometheus: "match ONLY on instance, ignore mode"      │
  │    Result: {instance, mode} with CPU usage per mode per core                 │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PATTERN 3: MANY-TO-ONE with group_left                                      │
  │    rate(cpu_time{mode!="idle"}[5m])                                           │
  │      / on(instance) group_left(count by (instance) (cpu_cores))              │
  │    Left: many series per instance (one per CPU mode)                          │
  │    Right: one series per instance (core count)                                │
  │    group_left: "left side has MORE series — add right labels to all of them" │
  │    Result: each mode gets divided by total core count → CPU % per mode       │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PATTERN 4: ignoring() — match on ALL labels EXCEPT specified                │
  │    rate(http_errors[5m]) / ignoring(status) rate(http_total[5m])             │
  │    Left: {job, endpoint, status="5xx"}  Right: {job, endpoint, status="all"} │
  │    ignoring(status): "match on job+endpoint, ignore the status mismatch"     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
KeywordMeaningUse When
on(label1, label2)Match ONLY on these labelsLeft and right have different extra labels
ignoring(label1)Match on ALL labels EXCEPT theseOnly 1-2 labels differ between sides
group_leftLeft side is "many", right is "one"Dividing per-pod metric by per-node constant
group_rightRight side is "many", left is "one"Reverse of group_left (less common)

7.15 Logical & Set Operators — and, or, unless

📐SYNTAX — Logical/Set Operators
# These are SET operators (not boolean!), operating on label-matched time series.

# A and B — INTERSECTION: returns A where B also exists (matching labels)
#   Use: "Show me errors ONLY on endpoints that have traffic"
#   Example: http_errors > 0 and http_total > 100

# A or B — UNION: returns A plus B where labels match
#   Use: "Alert if EITHER errors OR latency is high"
#   Example: error_rate > 0.05 or p99_latency > 1

# A unless B — DIFFERENCE: returns A where B does NOT exist
#   Use: "Show errors EXCEPT on health check endpoints"
#   Example: rate(errors[5m]) unless rate(total[5m]) < 10

# ⚠️  and/or/unless match on LABEL SETS — different from &&, || boolean logic!
# ⚠️  They require matching labels between left and right sides.
# ⚠️  PCA: "Which operator returns intersection?" → and
🧮 LOGICAL/SET OPERATORS — How They Filter Time Series
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OPERATOR       BEHAVIOR              EXAMPLE                                 │
  │  ────────       ────────              ───────                                 │
  │                                                                               │
  │  and            INTERSECTION          http_errors > 0 and http_total > 100    │
  │                 "Only A where         "Show errors ONLY on endpoints          │
  │                  B also exists"        that have >100 total requests"         │
  │                                                                               │
  │  or             UNION                 http_errors > 10 or http_latency > 2    │
  │                 "A plus B for         "Alert if EITHER errors are high        │
  │                  matching labels"      OR latency is high"                    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  unless          DIFFERENCE           rate(errors[5m]) unless                  │
  │                 "A minus B"           rate(total[5m]) < 10                    │
  │                  "Only A where        "Show errors EXCEPT on very quiet       │
  │                   B does NOT exist"    endpoints (< 10 req/s)"               │
  │                                                                               │
  │  💡 PCA TIP: These are SET operators, not boolean logic! They work on         │
  │     MATCHING LABEL SETS, not on true/false values.                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Real-World Examples
# Which pods have high CPU AND high memory? (intersection)
rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total[5m]) > 0.5
  and
container_memory_working_set_bytes > 500 * 1024 * 1024

# Alerting: high errors OR high latency (union)
rate(http_errors_total[5m]) > 1
  or
histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_bucket[5m])) > 1

# Exclude health check endpoints from error analysis (difference)
rate(http_errors_total{endpoint!~"/healthz|/readyz"}[5m])
  unless
rate(http_errors_total{endpoint=~"/healthz|/readyz"}[5m])

7.16 Missing & Absent Detection — When Metrics Disappear

📐SYNTAX — Absent Detection Functions
# absent(v instant-vector) — returns 1 if the vector is EMPTY, otherwise returns nothing
#   Use: "Alert if there are zero instances of this job!"
#   Example: absent(up{job="anihpj"}) → empty (job exists)
#   Example: absent(up{job="DELETED"}) → 1 (job has no instances!)
#
#   ⚠️  This is the ONLY way to detect that a metric has disappeared entirely.
#   ⚠️  up{job="X"} == 0 works only if the metric EXISTS with value 0.
#   ⚠️  If the job is deleted, up{job="X"} returns NOTHING — no comparison possible!

# absent_over_time(v range-vector) — returns 1 if NO samples exist in the time window
#   Use: "Was this target scraped at all in the last 10 minutes?"
#   Example: absent_over_time(up{job="anihpj"}[10m])
#            → 0 (samples exist) or 1 (no samples — target gone for 10+ min)

# ⚠️  PCA GUARANTEED: "Which function detects a deleted job?" → absent()
# ⚠️  PCA TRAP: candidate answer "up{} == 0" — WRONG for deleted jobs!
absent() and absent_over_time() — Detecting "Nothing"
# absent() — Returns 1 if the metric has NO time series AT ALL
absent(nonexistent_metric)                     → 1 (empty vector = absent)
absent(up{job="anihpj"})                       → empty (metric exists → no output)
absent(up{job="DELETED_JOB"})                  → 1 (no such job → absent!)

# absent() in alerting — "Alert if there are NO production pods!"
alert: NoProductionPods
expr: absent(kube_pod_info{namespace="anihpj", env="production"})
for: 1m

# absent_over_time() — Returns 1 if NO samples existed in the time window
absent_over_time(up{job="anihpj"}[10m])        → 0 or 1
# "Was this target ever up in the last 10 minutes?"

# 🔥 CRITICAL USE CASE — Detect missing scrape targets:
# Without absent(), if a job has zero matching targets, the metric
# simply doesn't exist — no alert fires because there's nothing to evaluate!
# absent() returns 1 when the vector is empty, enabling "missing target" alerts.
PCA Exam: absent() is HEAVILY tested

The exam WILL ask: "How do you detect that a job has zero instances?" → absent(up{job="myjob"}) == 1. "Why doesn't up{job="deleted"} == 0 work for a deleted job?" → Because the metric doesn't exist — there's nothing to compare to 0. absent() is the ONLY way to detect the absence of time series.

7.17 Scalar Operations & Conversions — When You Need a Number

📐SYNTAX — Scalar Functions & Conversions
# scalar(v instant-vector) — extract a single float64 from an instant vector
#   ⚠️  ERRORS if v has more than 1 time series! Use carefully.
#   Example: scalar(up{job="anihpj",instance="web-1:8000"}) → 1.0

# vector(s scalar) — convert a scalar BACK to an instant vector (no labels)
#   Example: vector(3600) → {} 3600

# clamp_min(v instant-vector, min scalar) — ensure all values >= min
#   Example: clamp_min(disk_free, 0) → replaces negatives with 0

# clamp_max(v instant-vector, max scalar) — ensure all values <= max
#   Example: clamp_max(cpu_usage, 100) → caps at 100%

# round(v, [to_nearest]) — round values (default: to nearest integer)
#   Example: round(rate(http_total[5m]), 0.1) → round to 1 decimal

# floor(v) — round DOWN to nearest integer
# ceil(v) — round UP to nearest integer
# abs(v) — absolute value
# sgn(v) — sign: -1, 0, or 1

# ⚠️  scalar() is risky — only use when you're CERTAIN only 1 series exists.
# ⚠️  clamp_* functions are useful for dashboard cleanup (remove negatives).
Scalar Functions
# scalar() — Extract a single number from an instant vector
# ERROR if the vector has >1 time series!
scalar(up{job="anihpj-django", instance="web-1:8000"})
# → 1 (single value extracted)

# vector() — Convert a scalar BACK to an instant vector (no labels)
vector(3600)
# → { } 3600 (anonymous time series with value 3600)

# Practical use: Compare to a constant
rate(http_requests_total[5m]) > scalar(max_over_time(rate(http_requests_total[5m])[1h]) * 0.5)
# "Current rate > 50% of the max rate in the last hour" → SPIKE DETECTION!

Other useful scalar functions: clamp_min(v, min) / clamp_max(v, max) — cap values to a range. round(v, to_nearest) / floor(v) / ceil(v) — rounding. sgn(v) — sign of value (-1, 0, 1). abs(v) — absolute value. These are less common on PCA but useful in dashboards.

7.18 Offset & Historical Comparisons — "Compared to Yesterday"

📐SYNTAX — offset Modifier
# offset DURATION — shift the query time window BACK by DURATION
#   Syntax: <range-vector> offset <duration>
#   Duration units: s, m, h, d, w, y
#
#   Example: rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1d)
#            → "What was the rate at this exact time YESTERDAY?"
#
#   Example: rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1w)
#            → "What was the rate at this exact time LAST WEEK?"
#
# @ TIMESTAMP — evaluate expression AT a specific Unix timestamp
#   Example: http_requests_total @ 1609459200 → "value at Jan 1, 2021 00:00 UTC"
#   Example: rate(http_total[5m] @ (time() - 3600)) → "rate as of 1 hour ago"
#
# offset + @ combined:
#   rate(http_total[5m] offset 1d @ (time() - 86400))
#   → "Yesterday's rate, evaluated exactly 24h ago"
#
# ⚠️  offset goes AFTER [range] — NOT before!
#     ✅ rate(metric[5m] offset 1d)   ❌ rate(metric offset 1d[5m])
# ⚠️  PCA WILL test offset syntax order!
⏮️ OFFSET — Time Travel in PromQL
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  offset DURATION — Shift the query window BACK in time                        │
  │                                                                               │
  │  rate(http_requests_total[5m])           ← NOW (last 5 minutes)               │
  │  rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1h) ← 1 hour ago (same 5-minute window) │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │  PRODUCTION PATTERNS:                                                         │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  WEEK-OVER-WEEK COMPARISON (ratio):                                           │
  │    rate(http_requests_total[5m])                                              │
  │    /                                                                          │
  │    rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1w)                                    │
  │    → 1.0 = same as last week, >1.0 = growth, <1.0 = decline                  │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ANOMALY DETECTION (current vs. yesterday same time):                         │
  │    abs(                                                                       │
  │      rate(http_requests_total[5m])                                            │
  │      -                                                                        │
  │      rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1d)                                  │
  │    ) / rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1d) > 0.5                          │
  │    → "Current traffic differs >50% from yesterday at this time"              │
  │                                                                               │
  │  COMBINING OFFSET WITH @ MODIFIER:                                            │
  │    rate(http_requests_total[5m] @ 1609746000)                                 │
  │    → "What was the rate AT Jan 4, 2021 07:00 UTC?"                           │
  │                                                                               │
  │  OFFSET AT A SPECIFIC TIME:                                                   │
  │    rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1d @ (time() - 86400))                 │
  │    → "Yesterday's rate, evaluated at exactly 24h ago"                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
PCA Exam: offset is a guaranteed topic

The exam will ask about comparing current data to historical data. offset syntax: metric[range] offset DURATION. Duration units: s, m, h, d, w, y. Note: offset goes AFTER the range vector, not before! Common distractor: rate(metric offset 1d[5m]) — WRONG. Correct: rate(metric[5m] offset 1d) — offset AFTER [range].
