📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 7 (Operators & Functions)

Q1

What does rate(http_requests_total[5m]) return?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Per-second average rate of HTTP requests over the last 5 minutes.

💡 EXPLANATION

rate() divides the total increase over [5m] by 300 seconds, giving requests/sec. If increase(http_requests_total[5m]) = 3000, then rate = 10 req/s. Use rate() for dashboards (smooth). Use irate() for spotting instant spikes (only uses last 2 samples). rate() is the THE most-tested PromQL function on the PCA exam — you WILL see it in multiple questions.

Q2

What's the difference between sum() by (X) and sum() without (X)?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

by(X): KEEP label X, aggregate everything else. without(X): DROP label X, keep everything else.

💡 EXPLANATION

If a metric has labels {job, instance, endpoint}: sum by (endpoint) → one result per endpoint (job+instance dropped). sum without (instance) → one result per {job, endpoint} combo (instance dropped). Trick: by(endpoint) = keep ONLY endpoint. without(instance) = drop ONLY instance, keep job AND endpoint. Exam loves this distinction!

Q3

What happens if you apply rate() to a Gauge?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Returns meaningless garbage data. rate() requires monotonically increasing Counters.

💡 EXPLANATION

Rate() expects values that only go UP. A gauge going 8→4→6 would produce: rate over first drop = negative, rate over rise = positive. The result oscillates wildly. Use gauge values directly: memory_usage > 90 (no rate needed). For gauge changes: deriv(memory[6h]) gives per-second trend. PCA exam TRAP: showing rate(node_memory) and asking "is this correct?" → NO!

Q4

What's the correct way to calculate p99 latency from a histogram?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) — ALWAYS rate() buckets FIRST!

💡 EXPLANATION

The full pipeline: bucket counter → rate() → sum by(le) → histogram_quantile(). Every step is required. Raw buckets are cumulative counters (always increasing). histogram_quantile() needs the RATE of observations falling into each bucket. If you forget rate(), you get nonsense values. If you forget sum by(le), bucket boundaries are destroyed. This pipeline is GUARANTEED on the PCA exam.

  HISTOGRAM PIPELINE — 4 Essential Steps (B-R-S-H)
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════
  
  Step 1: BUCKETS (raw counter)
    http_duration_bucket{le="0.1"}  = 500    ← cumulative!
    http_duration_bucket{le="0.5"}  = 2300
    http_duration_bucket{le="1.0"}  = 4800
    http_duration_bucket{le="+Inf"} = 5000
  
  Step 2: RATE() — convert to per-second
    rate(http_duration_bucket{le="0.1"}[5m])  = 1.2/s
    rate(http_duration_bucket{le="0.5"}[5m])  = 5.8/s
    
  Step 3: SUM by(le) — combine across pods
    sum by (le) (rate(...)) ← preserves bucket boundaries!
    
  Step 4: HISTOGRAM_QUANTILE() — calculate percentile
    histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by(le) (rate(...)))
    → p99 = 1.2 seconds
  
  ❌ FORGET rate() → nonsense    ❌ FORGET by(le) → destroyed
Q5

A K8s pod dies. How long until its metrics disappear from queries?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

5 minutes (default lookback delta). Use up{} == 0 for instant detection.

💡 EXPLANATION

Staleness: Prometheus doesn't immediately remove metrics when scraping stops. It waits 5 minutes (default --query.lookback-delta). During those 5 minutes, queries still return the LAST scraped value. After 5 min, the series disappears. This is why up{job="X"} == 0 is critical — it detects DOWN targets immediately (within one scrape interval), without waiting for staleness.

Q6

What does predict_linear() do?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Uses linear regression to predict when a value will hit a threshold (e.g., "disk full in 4 hours").

💡 EXPLANATION

predict_linear(disk_free[1h], 4*3600) < 0 means: "based on last hour's trend, will disk free space hit zero in 4 hours (14,400 seconds)?" If yes, alert. This is predictive alerting — you detect problems BEFORE they happen. Use [1h] or longer for stable predictions. PCA exam favorite: combines function knowledge + alert logic + real-world scenario.

Q7

What's the difference between avg_over_time() and avg() by()?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

avg_over_time: averages values WITHIN one series over time. avg() by(): averages ACROSS multiple series at one point.

💡 EXPLANATION

avg_over_time(cpu_usage[1h]) → one value per pod: "pod-1 averaged 45% CPU over the last hour." avg by (namespace) (cpu_usage) → one value per namespace: "all pods in 'anihpj' average 52% CPU RIGHT NOW." Key: _over_time functions reduce a time range to a single value per series. Aggregation operators (sum/avg) combine multiple series at one instant.

Q8

What keyword controls vector matching when labels differ between left and right?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

on() — specify which labels to match. ignoring() — match on all EXCEPT specified. group_left/group_right — many-to-one matching.

💡 EXPLANATION

When dividing/multiplying metrics with different labels: rate(cpu_time[5m]) / on(instance) group_left count by (instance) (cpu_cores). Left has {instance, mode} labels, right has {instance} only. on(instance) matches on instance. group_left adds right-side labels to left result. Without on()/ignoring(), Prometheus errors on label mismatch.

Q9

What does rate(metric[5m])[1h:1m] do?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A subquery: calculates 5-min rate at 1-min intervals for the last hour — returns 60 data points.

💡 EXPLANATION

Subquery syntax: expression[duration:step]. rate(metric[5m])[1h:1m] = "run rate(metric[5m]) every 1 minute for the last hour" → 60 values. This is different from rate(metric[1h]) which returns ONE rate over the full hour. Subqueries are useful for: computing max_over_time of a rate, or feeding rate() results to another function.

Q10

How do you query a metric at a specific Unix timestamp?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use the @ modifier: http_requests_total @ 1609746000

💡 EXPLANATION

The @ modifier evaluates the query AT a specific Unix timestamp instead of "now." metric @ 1609746000 = "what was the value at Jan 4, 2021 07:00:00 UTC?" Useful for: debugging historical incidents, comparing "now" to "same time yesterday," and correlating metrics with known events. Combine with offset: metric @ (time() - 86400) = 24 hours ago.

  • ❌ #1: Applying rate() to a Gauge. Returns garbage — rate() is ONLY for Counters!
  • ❌ #2: Using histogram_quantile() on raw buckets. ALWAYS rate() buckets FIRST!
  • ❌ #3: Confusing by(X) (keep X) with without(X) (drop X) in aggregation.
  • ❌ #4: Using up{} == 1 to detect dead pods — lags 5 min. Use up{} == 0 instead!
  • ✅ Can you write rate(metric[5m]), increase(metric[1h]), and histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m]))?
  • ✅ Do you know when to use by() vs without()?
  • ✅ Can you explain staleness and why metrics disappear 5 min after a pod dies?

Chapter 8: PromQL Deep Dive — Real-World anihpj 🟡 Intermediate

From Theory to Practice — Monitoring a Real Django App

Apply everything from Chapters 6-7 to real anihpj/jobpost monitoring scenarios. Build complete RED dashboards, database health queries, cache hit-rate monitoring, and business metrics — all with production-ready PromQL.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours💎 PCA 28%🐍 Django

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — PromQL Patterns That Pay the Bills

Chapters 6-7 gave you the toolbox. This chapter shows you how to build a house. Real monitoring isn't about knowing every function — it's about knowing the right PATTERNS for common problems.

The 4 patterns every production engineer memorizes:

1. RED Pattern (every service endpoint): Rate = rate(total[5m]), Errors = rate(total{status=~"5.."}[5m]), Duration = histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m])). Copy-paste these three, change the metric name, and you have monitoring for ANY API. That's 80% of application monitoring.

2. Cache Health Pattern (any cache): rate(hits[5m]) / (rate(hits[5m]) + rate(misses[5m])) * 100. Works for Redis, Memcached, database buffer cache, CDN — anything with hit/miss counters. Target: >90% for Redis, >95% for DB cache.

3. Resource Saturation Pattern (any resource): current_usage / max_capacity * 100. Memory: used / total * 100. Disk: (total - free) / total * 100. File descriptors: allocated / max * 100. Alert when >80% (warning) or >90% (critical).

4. Error Budget Pattern (SLO tracking): (good_requests / total_requests) * 100. Compare to SLO target (e.g., 99.5%). Burn rate: error_rate / (1 - SLO). If burn rate > 10×, stop deployments! This pattern bridges engineering and business.

The mental model: Don't try to memorize every PromQL function. Memorize these 4 PATTERNS. When you encounter a new monitoring need, ask: "Which of the 4 patterns applies here?" Most problems fit one of them.

8.1 API Performance Monitoring (RED Method in PromQL)

RED Dashboard Queries for anihpj jobpost API
# RATE — requests per second per endpoint
sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint)

# ERRORS — 5xx error rate per second
sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint)

# DURATION — p99 latency per endpoint
histogram_quantile(0.99, sum(rate(jobpost_http_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) by (le, endpoint))

# ERROR RATE PERCENTAGE
sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint)
  /
sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint)
  * 100

8.2 Database Monitoring Queries

PostgreSQL Health for anihpj
# Active database connections
pg_stat_database_numbackends{datname="anihpj"}

# Database size in GB
pg_database_size_bytes{datname="anihpj"} / 1024 / 1024 / 1024

# Cache hit ratio (should be > 99%)
rate(pg_stat_database_blks_hit{datname="anihpj"}[5m]) /
  (rate(pg_stat_database_blks_hit{datname="anihpj"}[5m]) +
   rate(pg_stat_database_blks_read{datname="anihpj"}[5m])) * 100

8.3 Cache Monitoring Queries (Redis)

Redis Cache Health for anihpj
# Cache hit rate (target: > 90%)
rate(redis_keyspace_hits_total[5m]) /
  (rate(redis_keyspace_hits_total[5m]) + rate(redis_keyspace_misses_total[5m])) * 100

# Memory usage
redis_memory_used_bytes / redis_memory_max_bytes * 100

# Evictions (should be near zero)
rate(redis_evicted_keys_total[5m])

8.4 Business Metrics — Beyond Infrastructure

These come from YOUR Django code, not exporters:

Business Metrics for jobpost
# Active job listings (Gauge)
jobpost_active_listings

# Applications per hour
rate(jobpost_applications_total[1h]) * 3600

# Applications by status
sum(rate(jobpost_applications_total[5m])) by (status)

8.5 Building a Complete Dashboard — anihpj 8-Panel Overview

anihpj COMPLETE DASHBOARD — ALL 8 PromQL QUERIES
  PANEL 1 — Request Rate:    sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint)
  PANEL 2 — Error Rate (%):   (sum(rate(total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint) /
                               sum(rate(total[5m])) by (endpoint)) * 100
  PANEL 3 — p95 Latency:     histogram_quantile(0.95, sum(rate(bucket[5m])) by (le,endpoint))
  PANEL 4 — DB Connections:  pg_stat_database_numbackends{datname="anihpj"}
  PANEL 5 — Redis Hit Rate:  rate(hits[5m]) / (rate(hits[5m])+rate(misses[5m])) * 100
  PANEL 6 — Active Listings: jobpost_active_listings
  PANEL 7 — Apps/Hour:       rate(jobpost_applications_total[1h]) * 3600
  PANEL 8 — Pod Restarts:    increase(kube_pod_restarts_total{ns="anihpj"}[1h])

8.6 SLO/SLI Monitoring — Measuring Reliability with PromQL

🎯 SLO/SLI — The 4 Golden Signals Dashboard
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        SLO MONITORING FRAMEWORK                                │
  │                                                                               │
  │  SLI (Service Level Indicator) → The MEASUREMENT                               │
  │  SLO (Service Level Objective) → The TARGET                                    │
  │  SLA (Service Level Agreement) → The CONTRACT (with consequences)             │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │  EXAMPLE: anihpj Job Post API — 99.5% availability SLO                        │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  AVAILABILITY SLI (percentage of successful requests):                         │
  │    sum(rate(http_requests_total{status!~"5.."}[30d]))                         │
  │    /                                                                          │
  │    sum(rate(http_requests_total[30d])) * 100                                  │
  │    → Target SLO: ≥ 99.5%                                                      │
  │                                                                               │
  │  LATENCY SLI (p99 over 30 days):                                              │
  │    histogram_quantile(0.99,                                                   │
  │      sum(rate(http_duration_bucket[30d])) by (le))                            │
  │    → Target SLO: ≤ 500ms                                                      │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ERROR BUDGET — How much "bad" is acceptable before we freeze releases:       │
  │    (1 - (successful_requests / total_requests)) * 100                         │
  │    → Budget: 0.5% (100% - 99.5% SLO)                                          │
  │    → Budget burned: current_error_rate * 100                                  │
  │    → "We've burned 60% of our monthly error budget — stop deploying!"        │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │  ERROR BUDGET BURN RATE ALERT:                                                │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │    (                                                                          │
  │      sum(rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[1h]))                        │
  │      /                                                                        │
  │      sum(rate(http_requests_total[1h]))                                       │
  │    )                                                                          │
  │    >                                                                          │
  │    (                                                                          │
  │      (1 - 0.995)                                    # Error budget per hour  │
  │      * 14                                           # 14× = critical burn    │
  │    )                                                                          │
  │    → "Error budget is burning 14× faster than sustainable!"                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
SLO Monitoring Queries for anihpj
# AVAILABILITY: What % of requests succeeded in the last 30 days?
(
  sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total{status!~"5.."}[30d]))
  /
  sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[30d]))
) * 100

# LATENCY: p99 over 30 days (must be < 500ms to meet SLO)
histogram_quantile(0.99,
  sum(rate(jobpost_http_duration_seconds_bucket[30d])) by (le))

# ERROR BUDGET REMAINING this month
100 - (
  (sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[30d]))
   / sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[30d]))) * 100
) / (100 - 99.5) * 100    # as percentage of total budget

# BURN RATE ALERT — spending budget > 10× the sustainable rate
(
  sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[1h]))
  / sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[1h]))
) > 10 * (1 - 0.995)
PCA Exam: SLO concepts are tested

Know the difference: SLI = what you measure, SLO = your target, SLA = your promise. The exam may ask: "Which PromQL query calculates availability?" → success/total × 100. "What's an error budget?" → the amount of errors allowed before violating the SLO. These concepts bridge monitoring and business objectives.

8.7 Debugging with PromQL — The On-Call Engineer's Toolkit

🔧 DEBUGGING FLOW — "The Site is Slow!" — Step by Step
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP 1: ISOLATE THE PROBLEM — Which endpoint is slow?                        │
  │    topk(5,                                                                     │
  │      histogram_quantile(0.99,                                                 │
  │        sum(rate(http_duration_bucket[5m])) by (le, endpoint)))               │
  │    → "Top 5 slowest endpoints by p99"                                        │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 2: CHECK FOR ERRORS — Is it failing or just slow?                       │
  │    sum(rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint)           │
  │    → "Error rate per endpoint — are users getting 500s?"                     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 3: DATABASE CHECK — Are queries slow?                                   │
  │    histogram_quantile(0.99,                                                   │
  │      sum(rate(pg_stat_database_blks_read[5m])) by (datname))                 │
  │    → "High disk reads = queries not using indexes"                           │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 4: RESOURCE CHECK — Are we out of CPU/Memory?                           │
  │    100 - (avg by (instance) (rate(node_cpu{mode="idle"}[5m])) * 100)         │
  │    → "CPU usage per node — if > 90%, throttling may occur"                   │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 5: CORRELATE — Did a deployment cause this?                             │
  │    changes(kube_deployment_status_replicas_available{namespace="anihpj"}[15m])│
  │    → "Pod count changed in the last 15 minutes → likely a deployment"        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

More debugging patterns:

Quick Diagnostic Queries
# Which pods are restarting frequently? (CrashLoopBackOff detection)
topk(5, rate(kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total{namespace="anihpj"}[15m]))

# Are we running out of file descriptors?
node_filefd_allocated / node_filefd_maximum * 100

# Is disk I/O saturated? (> 90% utilization is bad)
rate(node_disk_io_time_seconds_total{device!~"dm-.*"}[5m]) * 100

# Which endpoint has the highest error-to-success ratio?
topk(3,
  sum(rate(http_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint)
  /
  sum(rate(http_total{status!~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint)
)

# Memory leak detection — is memory growing monotonically?
deriv(container_memory_working_set_bytes{namespace="anihpj"}[6h]) > 0
# → Positive derivative over 6h = memory is steadily increasing

8.8 Multi-Environment Comparisons — Dev vs Staging vs Production

🌍 CROSS-ENVIRONMENT MONITORING — One Query, Three Environments
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCENARIO: You just deployed a new feature to production. Is it performing    │
  │  the same as it did in staging? Use PromQL to compare enviroments side-by-side.│
  │                                                                               │
  │  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════     │
  │  COMPARE REQUEST RATE ACROSS ENVIRONMENTS:                                     │
  │    sum(rate(http_requests_total{env="production"}[5m]))                       │
  │    /                                                                          │
  │    sum(rate(http_requests_total{env="staging"}[5m]))                          │
  │    → Production/staging traffic ratio (normalize for capacity diff)          │
  │                                                                               │
  │  COMPARE LATENCY (p95) — Production vs Staging:                               │
  │    histogram_quantile(0.95,                                                   │
  │      sum(rate(http_duration_bucket{env="production"}[5m])) by (le, endpoint))│
  │    /                                                                          │
  │    histogram_quantile(0.95,                                                   │
  │      sum(rate(http_duration_bucket{env="staging"}[5m])) by (le, endpoint))   │
  │    → "Is production 2× slower than staging? If so, investigate!"            │
  │                                                                               │
  │  CANARY ANALYSIS — comparing canary vs stable pods:                           │
  │    histogram_quantile(0.99,                                                   │
  │      sum(rate(http_duration_bucket{version="v2.1-canary"}[5m])) by (le))     │
  │    /                                                                          │
  │    histogram_quantile(0.99,                                                   │
  │      sum(rate(http_duration_bucket{version="v2.0-stable"}[5m])) by (le))     │
  │    → "Is the canary performing worse than stable? Roll back if ratio > 1.5!" │
  │                                                                               │
  │  DAY-OVER-DAY TRAFFIC PATTERN:                                                │
  │    rate(http_requests_total[5m])                                              │
  │    /                                                                          │
  │    rate(http_requests_total[5m] offset 1d)                                    │
  │    → "Is today's traffic normal for this time of day?"                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🏆 Production Best Practice

Always add an env label (dev/staging/production) to your metrics via external_labels or during instrumentation. This single label enables all cross-environment comparisons without changing queries. Use Grafana dashboard variables with $environment to toggle between environments in one dashboard. For canary deployments, add a version or track label to distinguish canary from stable pods.

  • RED Method 3-in-1: Rate = rate(total[5m]), Errors = rate(total{status=~"5.."}[5m]), Duration = histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(bucket[5m])). Copy-paste these 3 for ANY service.
  • Error budget burn rate: If error rate > 10 × (1 - SLO), stop deployments. Example: SLO=99.5% → budget = 0.5%. Burning at 5% = 10× too fast!
  • SLO shortcut: 99% = 7.2h downtime/month. Every additional 9 divides downtime by 10. 99.9% = 43min, 99.99% = 4.3min.
  • Debugging flow: Top 5 slowest → check errors → check DB → check resources → check deployments. 5 steps, always in that order for on-call.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 8 (Real-World PromQL)

Q1

Write a PromQL query to show request rate per endpoint for anihpj.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint)

💡 EXPLANATION

This is THE most common production PromQL pattern. Breakdown: rate(metric[5m]) → per-second rate (Counter → rate). sum(...) by (endpoint) → aggregate ALL pods/instances per endpoint. Without sum by(), you'd see one line per pod — useful for debugging, not dashboards. This pattern appears in virtually every Grafana RED dashboard.

Q2

How do you calculate Redis cache hit rate percentage?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

rate(redis_keyspace_hits_total[5m]) / (rate(redis_keyspace_hits_total[5m]) + rate(redis_keyspace_misses_total[5m])) * 100

💡 EXPLANATION

Cache hit rate = hits ÷ (hits + misses) × 100. Both are counters — must rate() both before dividing. Result: 0-100 percentage. If hit rate drops below 80%, investigate: are cache keys expiring too fast? Is cache size too small? Are new query patterns bypassing the cache? This is a real production monitoring query.

Q3

What metric type is jobpost_active_listings?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Gauge — it goes up and down as jobs are posted and filled.

💡 EXPLANATION

Gauge = snapshot of current state. Query it directly: jobpost_active_listings returns the current number (e.g., 247). No rate() needed — you want the absolute value, not the rate of change. Counter vs Gauge decision: \"Does this value only go up?\" → Counter. \"Can it go up and down?\" → Gauge. Active listings = Gauge. Total listings ever created = Counter.

Q4

Which PromQL function converts a Counter into per-second rate?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

rate() or irate() — ALWAYS use on Counters before graphing.

💡 EXPLANATION

Never graph raw Counter values — they always go up, making every graph look like \"traffic is increasing!\" even when it's flat. Always apply rate() first. rate() = smooth average over [d]. irate() = instant rate (last 2 samples only, spikier). Use rate() for alerting and dashboards. Use irate() for debugging sudden spikes. This is a PCA exam fundamental.

Q5

How would you query total job applications in the last hour?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

increase(jobpost_applications_total[1h])

💡 EXPLANATION

increase() = total growth of a counter over [duration]. Equivalent to rate() × duration but handles counter resets and extrapolation correctly. increase(metric[1h]) = \"how many events in the last hour?\" (integer). rate(metric[1h]) = \"how many events per second?\" (float). increase() is more intuitive for business questions like \"how many applications today?\"

Q6

Write the PromQL for p95 latency of the jobpost API across all pods.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

histogram_quantile(0.95, sum(rate(jobpost_http_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) by (le))

💡 EXPLANATION

Complete pipeline: (1) rate(bucket[5m]) — get per-second rate of observations per bucket. (2) sum(...) by (le) — combine buckets across ALL pods, preserving bucket boundaries. (3) histogram_quantile(0.95, ...) — calculate the 95th percentile. Without by(le), bucket boundaries are destroyed and the result is garbage. This is the single most important PromQL pattern for production monitoring.

Q7

How do you monitor PostgreSQL cache hit ratio for anihpj?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

rate(pg_stat_database_blks_hit{datname="anihpj"}[5m]) / (rate(pg_stat_database_blks_hit[5m]) + rate(pg_stat_database_blks_read[5m])) * 100

💡 EXPLANATION

PostgreSQL exposes block-level cache stats via the PG exporter. blks_hit = blocks found in PG's shared buffers (cache). blks_read = blocks read from disk. Cache hit ratio = hits / total reads. A ratio above 95% is healthy. If it drops: increase shared_buffers, or investigate queries doing full table scans. This is a real-world DBA monitoring query.

Q8

What K8s metric shows pod restarts in the anihpj namespace?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

increase(kube_pod_restarts_total{namespace="anihpj"}[1h])

💡 EXPLANATION

kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total is a Counter from kube-state-metrics. Use increase() to see restarts per time window. If a pod restarts >5 times in 15 minutes, it's in CrashLoopBackOff — alert on this! Combine with kube_pod_status_phase{phase=\"Running\"} to distinguish running-but-crashing from permanently failed pods.

Q9

How do you convert rate(jobpost_applications_total[1h]) to applications per hour?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Multiply by 3600: rate(jobpost_applications_total[1h]) * 3600 — converts per-second rate to per-hour.

💡 EXPLANATION

PromQL always works in seconds. rate() returns per-second. To convert: × 60 = per-minute, × 3600 = per-hour, × 86400 = per-day. Alternative: use increase(metric[1h]) directly for the hourly total. Which to use? rate() for dashboards (consistent units), increase() for business reports (\"how many today?\").

Q10

Why should you use sum() by (le, endpoint) for histogram quantiles across pods?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Histogram buckets must be summed across pods BEFORE calculating quantiles. by (le, endpoint) preserves the bucket boundary label (le) and endpoint, enabling accurate aggregation.

💡 EXPLANATION

The le label is CRITICAL — it IS the bucket boundary. If you sum without by(le), Prometheus adds up ALL buckets into one number, losing the distribution entirely. by (le) = one sum per bucket boundary. by (le, endpoint) = one sum per bucket per endpoint. Then histogram_quantile() can correctly interpolate. Without by(le): meaningless single number. With by(le): accurate percentile.

  • ❌ #1: Applying histogram_quantile() on raw _bucket without rate() first. Always rate buckets!
  • ❌ #2: Summing histogram buckets WITHOUT by(le) — destroys bucket boundaries, quantile is meaningless.
  • ❌ #3: Using rate(request_duration_seconds_sum[5m]) / rate(request_duration_seconds_count[5m]) for average. Use histogram_quantile() for percentiles, not manual division.
  • ❌ #4: Monitoring "JobPost creation rate" via rate(post_create_total[5m]) but forgetting to label by path to distinguish API from admin-created posts.
  • ✅ Can you explain the full histogram_quantile() pipeline: bucket → rate() → sum() by(le) → histogram_quantile()?
  • ✅ Do you know why by(le) is mandatory when aggregating histograms?
  • ✅ Can you write the complete RED queries for the anihpj jobpost service?

Chapter 9: Client Libraries — Instrumenting Your Code 🟡 Intermediate

Teaching Your Application to Speak Prometheus

Client libraries are the bridge between your application code and Prometheus. They let you define custom metrics directly in your code — counters for tracking events, gauges for current state, histograms for latency distributions. This chapter covers the client library ecosystem, instrumenting Python/Django apps with prometheus_client, all 4 metric types with real anihpj/jobpost examples, best practices for naming and labels, and auto-instrumentation with OpenTelemetry. Instrumentation = 16% of the PCA exam.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🔧 PCA 16%🐍 Python

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The Prometheus client library ecosystem — official vs unofficial libraries
  • ✅ How to add prometheus_client to Django and expose /metrics
  • ✅ All 4 metric types in Python code with real anihpj/jobpost examples
  • ✅ Instrumentation best practices — what to measure, what NOT to measure
  • ✅ Auto-instrumentation with OpenTelemetry vs manual instrumentation
🎓 PCA Relevance
Instrumentation 16%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 9 Must-Knows
4 Metric TypesCounter (only goes up), Gauge (up/down), Histogram (buckets + sum + count), Summary (quantiles on client). Know the difference!
Naming Conventionnamespace_subsystem_name_unit — lowercase, underscores. Example: jobpost_http_requests_total
Label CardinalityKeep unique label combinations <100. NEVER use user IDs, emails, or request IDs as labels!
RED MethodRate (Counter), Errors (Counter), Duration (Histogram) — the 3 metrics every service needs.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — What "Instrumentation" Really Means

Instrumentation = adding measurement code to your application. Think of a car dashboard — you have a speedometer (gauge), an odometer (counter), and a fuel gauge (gauge). Instrumentation is like installing those gauges in your code so Prometheus can read them.

Two approaches:

1. Manual Instrumentation (this chapter): You write Python code like job_applications_total.inc() inside your Django views. Full control over WHAT you measure and HOW you name it. More work, higher quality.

2. Auto-Instrumentation (§9.5): A library automatically adds metrics to your app with ZERO code changes. Less control (generic metric names), but instant visibility. Great for getting started.

The /metrics endpoint is the key: After instrumenting your app, Prometheus scrapes http://your-app:8000/metrics every 15 seconds. That's how all your custom metrics flow into Prometheus.

9.1 The Prometheus Client Library Ecosystem

Prometheus provides official client libraries maintained by the Prometheus team, plus a rich ecosystem of community-maintained unofficial libraries. Every library exposes the same core functionality: define Counter/Gauge/Histogram/Summary metrics, register them, and serve them at /metrics.

🌍 PROMETHEUS CLIENT LIBRARY ECOSYSTEM
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     PROMETHEUS CLIENT LIBRARIES                          │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌── OFFICIAL (prometheus.io) ───┐    ┌── UNOFFICIAL (community) ────┐  │
  │  │                               │    │                                │  │
  │  │  ● Go      (promhttp)        │    │  ● .NET / C#                  │  │
  │  │  ● Java    (SimpleClient)   │    │  ● Node.js (siimon/prom-client)│  │
  │  │  ● Python  (prometheus_client)│   │  ● PHP     (Jimdo/prometheus)  │  │
  │  │  ● Ruby    (prometheus/client)│   │  ● C++     (jupp0r/prometheus) │  │
  │  │  ● Rust    (prometheus)      │    │  ● Haskell ● Erlang ● Elixir   │  │
  │  │                               │    │  ● Dart   ● Swift             │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────┘    └────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ALL libraries provide:                                                  │
  │  ✅ 4 metric types (Counter, Gauge, Histogram, Summary)                  │
  │  ✅ /metrics HTTP endpoint (text exposition format)                      │
  │  ✅ Label support + registration with CollectorRegistry                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For PCA exam: Know that libraries exist for most languages. The exam focuses on Python (prometheus_client) but questions may reference Go/Java patterns. The concepts (Counter, Gauge, Histogram, labels) are universal across all libraries.

🐍
Python — prometheus_client

pip install prometheus-client. The library we use for anihpj. Provides start_http_server() for standalone exporters and WSGI middleware for Django/Flask integration.

☸️
Go — promhttp

Built into the Go Prometheus client. Used by Kubernetes components (kube-apiserver, kubelet, etcd). Most K8s metrics you see are instrumented in Go.

9.2 Instrumenting Python/Django with prometheus_client

Adding Prometheus to your Django app takes 3 steps: install the library, add middleware, expose /metrics. Here's the complete setup for anihpj:

1
Install

pip install prometheus-client django-prometheus

2
Configure Django

Add to INSTALLED_APPS and MIDDLEWARE in settings.py

3
Expose /metrics

Add URL pattern — Prometheus scrapes :8000/metrics

Step 1: settings.py — Full Prometheus Configuration for anihpj
# anihpj/settings.py
INSTALLED_APPS = [
    'django_prometheus',           # ← Add this FIRST
    'django.contrib.admin',
    'django.contrib.auth',
    # ... other apps ...
    'jobpost',                     # Our app
]

MIDDLEWARE = [
    'django_prometheus.middleware.PrometheusBeforeMiddleware',   # ← Before
    'django.middleware.security.SecurityMiddleware',
    # ... other middleware ...
    'django_prometheus.middleware.PrometheusAfterMiddleware',    # ← After
]

# Database engine — use the Prometheus wrapper
DATABASES = {
    'default': {
        'ENGINE': 'django_prometheus.db.backends.postgresql',  # ← Wraps Postgres
        'NAME': 'anihpj',
        'USER': 'anihpj_user',
        'PASSWORD': 'supersecret',
        'HOST': 'db',
        'PORT': '5432',
    }
}

# Cache backend — Prometheus wrapper for Redis
CACHES = {
    'default': {
        'BACKEND': 'django_prometheus.cache.backends.redis.RedisCache',
        'LOCATION': 'redis://redis:6379/0',
    }
}
Step 2: urls.py — Expose /metrics Endpoint
# anihpj/urls.py
from django.urls import path, include

urlpatterns = [
    path('', include('django_prometheus.urls')),    # ← /metrics lives here
    path('admin/', admin.site.urls),
    path('jobs/', include('jobpost.urls')),
    # ... other URLs ...
]
# Now Prometheus can scrape:
#   http://anihpj:8000/metrics  ← django-prometheus auto-generates this!
💡 What django-prometheus gives you for FREE: Just by adding the middleware and DB wrapper, you automatically get: django_http_requests_total_by_view_transport_method (request count by view), django_http_requests_latency_seconds_by_view_method (latency histogram), django_db_query_duration_seconds (database query timing), plus cache hit/miss metrics if using the Redis cache backend. Zero custom code required for these!

9.3 The 4 Metric Types in Code — Python Examples for anihpj

TypeBehaviorMethodPCA Key Point
CounterOnly goes UP (or resets to 0).inc() / .inc(5)Use rate() to get per-second rate
GaugeGoes UP and DOWN.set() / .inc() / .dec()Current value — memory, queue depth
HistogramBucketed observations.observe(0.42)Creates _bucket, _sum, _count series
SummaryClient-side quantiles.observe(0.42)Quantiles calculated on client, not server
Python — All 4 Metric Types with anihpj/jobpost Use Cases
# jobpost/metrics.py — Central metrics definitions for anihpj
from prometheus_client import Counter, Gauge, Histogram, Summary

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# 1. COUNTER — Only increases (API calls, job posts, errors)
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
jobpost_jobs_created_total = Counter(
    'jobpost_jobs_created_total',
    'Total number of job postings created',
    ['status']  # Labels: status="active" | "draft" | "archived"
)

jobpost_applications_total = Counter(
    'jobpost_applications_total',
    'Total job applications submitted',
    ['status', 'source']  # Labels: status, source (web/mobile/api)
)

jobpost_errors_total = Counter(
    'jobpost_errors_total',
    'Total application errors',
    ['error_type']  # Labels: error_type="validation" | "db_error" | "timeout"
)

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# 2. GAUGE — Goes up AND down (active listings, queue depth)
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
jobpost_active_listings = Gauge(
    'jobpost_active_listings',
    'Number of currently active job postings'
)

jobpost_queue_depth = Gauge(
    'jobpost_queue_depth',
    'Number of pending background tasks',
    ['queue_name']  # Labels: queue_name="email" | "thumbnail" | "index"
)

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# 3. HISTOGRAM — Latency distribution (bucketed)
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
jobpost_request_duration_seconds = Histogram(
    'jobpost_request_duration_seconds',
    'Request latency for jobpost views',
    ['view', 'method'],  # Labels: view="JobListView", method="GET"
    buckets=[0.01, 0.025, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0, 2.5, 5.0, 10.0]
    #        10ms  25ms  50ms 100ms 250ms 500ms 1s   2.5s  5s   10s
)

jobpost_search_duration_seconds = Histogram(
    'jobpost_search_duration_seconds',
    'Search query latency',
    buckets=[0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0, 2.5, 5.0]
)

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# 4. SUMMARY — Client-side quantiles (less common, use Histogram!)
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
jobpost_response_size_bytes = Summary(
    'jobpost_response_size_bytes',
    'HTTP response size in bytes',
    ['view']
)

# PCA NOTE: Histograms are preferred over Summaries because
# you can aggregate histograms across instances using
# histogram_quantile(). Summaries CANNOT be aggregated!
🔄 HISTOGRAM vs SUMMARY — The Critical Difference for PCA
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                            │
  │   HISTOGRAM                          SUMMARY                               │
  │   ─────────                          ───────                               │
  │                                                                            │
  │   App                            App          App                          │
  │   │                              │            │                            │
  │   │ .observe(0.3s)              │ .observe(0.3s)                          │
  │   │                              │            │                            │
  │   ▼                              ▼            ▼                            │
  │   ┌──────────┐                  ┌──────────────┐                           │
  │   │ Buckets: │                  │ Quantiles    │                           │
  │   │ <0.5s: 1 │                  │ calculated   │                           │
  │   │ <1.0s: 1 │                  │ ON THE       │                           │
  │   │ <2.5s: 1 │                  │ CLIENT!      │                           │
  │   │ sum=0.3  │                  │ p50=0.3s     │                           │
  │   │ count=1  │                  │ p99=0.3s     │                           │
  │   └──────────┘                  └──────────────┘                           │
  │                                                                            │
  │   ✅ Can aggregate across       ❌ CANNOT aggregate across                  │
  │      instances! (sum buckets)      instances! (quantiles don't sum)         │
  │                                                                            │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │   │  🎓 PCA EXAM TIP: When in doubt, choose HISTOGRAM.               │    │
  │   │  "Use Histograms for latency measurements in distributed systems"│    │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

9.4 Best Practices for Instrumentation

✅ DO's — What to Measure & How
CategoryWhat to MeasureMetric Type
RED MetricsRate, Errors, Duration for EVERY endpointCounter, Histogram
Business KPIsJob posts created, applications, signupsCounter
SaturationQueue depth, connection pool usageGauge
DependenciesDB query time, cache hit rate, external API callsHistogram, Counter
Namingapp_subsystem_name_unit — lowercase_underscoresAll
Labelsendpoint, method, status_code (low cardinality)All
❌ DON'Ts — What to Avoid
Anti-PatternWhy It's Bad
❌ User IDs as labelsExplodes cardinality — 10M users = 10M time series
❌ Request IDs as labelsEvery request is unique — infinite cardinality
❌ Email addresses as labelsHigh cardinality + PII/security risk
❌ Timestamps as labelsCreates new series every second — Prometheus dies
❌ Request bodies as labelsHuge cardinality, potential security leak
❌ Measuring without rate()Raw Counter values are meaningless — always rate()
⚠️ Cardinality Explosion — The #1 Prometheus Killer: Every unique label combination creates a new time series. If you label by user_id and you have 100,000 users, you create 100,000 time series for that ONE metric. With 10 metrics, that's 1,000,000 time series. Prometheus starts choking around 10M active series. Rule of thumb: each label should have <100 unique values.
📏 WHERE to Put Metrics in Django — The 3 Layers
LayerWhat to InstrumentExample (anihpj)
MiddlewareHTTP request count, latency, status codesdjango-prometheus auto-handles this
ViewsBusiness logic: job posts, applications, searchesjobpost_jobs_created_total.inc() in view
ModelsDB interactions, object creation/deletionOverride save() to emit metrics

🤔 Which Metric Type Should I Use? — Decision Tree

Q1: Is the value only going UP over time (never decreases except on restart)?
YES → COUNTER
Requests, errors, jobs created, bytes sent. Use .inc().
NO (goes up & down) → Go to Q2
Q2: Do you need PERCENTILES (p50, p95, p99) aggregated across multiple pods?
YES → HISTOGRAM
Latency, response size, query time. Use .observe().
NO → GAUGE
Active listings, memory, queue depth, temperature. Use .set().
🔑 Rarely use Summary. It calculates quantiles on the client and cannot be aggregated across instances. Only use it for single-instance services.
🧠 RED + USE — The Complete Monitoring Methodology
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    TWO COMPLEMENTARY MONITORING METHODS                    │
  │                                                                           │
  │  ┌── RED (Request-Driven) ──────────┐  ┌── USE (Resource-Driven) ──────┐ │
  │  │  For SERVICES (Django views,      │  │  For RESOURCES (CPU, memory,   │ │
  │  │  API endpoints, every endpoint)   │  │  disk, network, DB, cache)     │ │
  │  │                                   │  │                                │ │
  │  │  R = RATE                         │  │  U = UTILIZATION               │ │
  │  │    requests/sec per endpoint      │  │    % CPU used, % disk full     │ │
  │  │    Counter → rate()[5m]           │  │    Gauge (0-100%)              │ │
  │  │                                   │  │                                │ │
  │  │  E = ERRORS                       │  │  S = SATURATION                │ │
  │  │    failed requests / sec          │  │    Queue depth, connections    │ │
  │  │    Counter{status="5xx"}          │  │    Gauge (queue length)        │ │
  │  │    → rate()[5m]                   │  │                                │ │
  │  │                                   │  │  E = ERRORS                    │ │
  │  │  D = DURATION                     │  │    Failed allocations, OOM     │ │
  │  │    p50, p95, p99 latency          │  │    Counter (error events)      │ │
  │  │    Histogram → histogram_quantile │  │                                │ │
  │  │                                   │  │  ──────────────────────        │ │
  │  │  ──────────────────────           │  │  anihpj examples:              │ │
  │  │  anihpj examples:                 │  │  • node_memory_MemAvailable    │ │
  │  │  • jobpost_request_duration       │  │  • node_disk_io_time           │ │
  │  │  • django_http_responses_total    │  │  • pg_stat_activity_count      │ │
  │  │  • jobpost_errors_total           │  │  • redis_connected_clients     │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────┘  └────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                           │
  │  🎓 PCA TIP: For the exam, know BOTH methods. RED = services, USE = infra. │
  │  Together they cover 100% of your monitoring needs.                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

9.5 Auto-Instrumentation — Metrics Without Code Changes

OpenTelemetry provides auto-instrumentation that monkey-patches Django to automatically emit metrics — no code changes needed. It's like getting django-prometheus features without modifying settings.py. The trade-off: less control over metric names, labels, and what gets measured.

AspectManual (prometheus_client)Auto (OpenTelemetry)
Setup effortWrite code for each metricOne command: opentelemetry-instrument python manage.py runserver
Control over metricsFull — custom names, labels, bucketsLimited — auto-generated names
Business metricsYes — any logic you wantNo — only HTTP/DB level
MaintenanceUpdate code when views changeZero maintenance
Best forProduction, deep observabilityQuick start, POCs, staging
OpenTelemetry Auto-Instrumentation for anihpj
# Install OpenTelemetry + Prometheus exporter
pip install opentelemetry-distro opentelemetry-exporter-prometheus
opentelemetry-bootstrap --action=install

# Run Django with auto-instrumentation
opentelemetry-instrument \
    --traces_exporter none \
    --metrics_exporter prometheus \
    python manage.py runserver 0.0.0.0:8000

# Now scrape http://localhost:8000/metrics (different port in production!)
# Auto-generated metrics include:
#   http_server_duration_milliseconds  (histogram by method, status)
#   http_server_request_size           (histogram)
#   http_server_response_size          (histogram)
🔄 METRIC LIFECYCLE — From Code to Dashboard
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        A METRIC'S JOURNEY                                     │
  │                                                                               │
  │  1. DEFINE                 2. RECORD                3. EXPOSE                 │
  │  ┌───────────┐            ┌───────────┐            ┌───────────┐            │
  │  │ metrics.py │            │ views.py  │            │ /metrics  │            │
  │  │            │            │           │            │           │            │
  │  │ Counter(   │──import──▶│ .inc()    │──serve──▶│ HTTP 200  │            │
  │  │  'jobs_total')         │ .observe()│           │ text/plain│            │
  │  └───────────┘            └───────────┘            └─────┬─────┘            │
  │                                                          │                   │
  │  4. SCRAPE                5. STORE                 6. QUERY                  │
  │  ┌───────────┐            ┌───────────┐            ┌───────────┐            │
  │  │Prometheus │──pull──▶  │   TSDB    │──read──▶  │  PromQL   │            │
  │  │ :9090     │  /metrics  │ (on disk) │           │ rate(...) │            │
  │  │every 15s  │            │           │           │           │            │
  │  └───────────┘            └───────────┘            └─────┬─────┘            │
  │                                                          │                   │
  │  7. VISUALIZE                                                                │
  │  ┌───────────┐                                                               │
  │  │ Grafana   │──panel──▶  📈 Dashboard                                       │
  │  │ :3000     │                                                               │
  │  └───────────┘                                                               │
  │                                                                               │
  │     CODE (you)          →     PROMETHEUS        →      HUMAN                 │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
PCA Exam: Client Libraries & Instrumentation

The PCA exam will test you on these specific points: (1) Can you identify which metric type to use for a given scenario? "Count requests" = Counter, "Track memory" = Gauge, "Measure latency percentiles" = Histogram. (2) Do you know that histogram_quantile() must be applied to rate(bucket[5m]), not raw buckets? (3) Can you spot cardinality explosions? If you see user_id or email as a label, that's wrong. (4) Do you know the naming convention: namespace_subsystem_name_unit with _total suffix for Counters?

  • 4 Types mnemonic: C.G.H.S. — Counter (only up), Gauge (up/down), Histogram (buckets), Summary (client quantiles). Know when to use each!
  • Cardinality rule: Unique values < 100 per label = safe. User IDs, request IDs, session IDs = NEVER labels. Endpoint, method, status = ALWAYS safe.
  • Metrics at module level: Define Gauges/Counters OUTSIDE functions (at file top). Inside a function = re-created every call = memory leak + wrong values.
  • django-prometheus 3 steps: pip install → add to INSTALLED_APPS/MIDDLEWARE/DB engine → add URL pattern. 3 lines of config, 80% of monitoring done.

9.6 Common Instrumentation Patterns — Production-Ready Recipes

📋 INSTRUMENTATION PATTERNS — What to Measure and How
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │               3 ESSENTIAL INSTRUMENTATION PATTERNS                            │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PATTERN 1: REQUEST COUNTING (Counter)                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Track every API call with labels: method, endpoint, status_code       │    │
  │  │ Counter: jobpost_http_requests_total{method,endpoint,status}          │    │
  │  │ .inc() on every request → gives you RED method coverage               │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PATTERN 2: OPERATION TIMING (Histogram)                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Wrap slow operations with .time() context manager:                    │    │
  │  │ with request_latency.labels(endpoint="/api/jobs").time():            │    │
  │  │     result = perform_database_query()                                 │    │
  │  │ → Automatically records duration in histogram buckets                 │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  PATTERN 3: STATE TRACKING (Gauge)                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Track current system state — update on every change:                   │    │
  │  │ active_users.set(User.objects.filter(is_active=True).count())         │    │
  │  │ queue_depth.set(task_queue.qsize())                                    │    │
  │  │ → Call .set() whenever state changes (signals, periodic tasks)        │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🎯
The Decorator Pattern

Create a @track_latency(endpoint) decorator. Apply to any Django view — automatically records request count + latency. One decorator, full RED coverage for any endpoint.

🔄
The Signal Pattern

Use Django signals (post_save, post_delete) to update Gauges. When a JobPost is created: jobpost_active_listings.inc(). When archived: .dec(). Gauge always reflects current DB state — no polling needed.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 9 (Client Libraries)

Q1

What are the 4 metric types in Prometheus client libraries?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Counter (only increases), Gauge (up/down), Histogram (bucketed observations), Summary (client-side quantiles). All 4 are available in every official client library.

💡 EXPLANATION

These 4 types are the foundation of Prometheus instrumentation. Counter: total HTTP requests, errors — use rate() to see per-second. Gauge: memory usage, active users — use avg_over_time(). Histogram: request duration with configurable buckets — aggregate across servers. Summary: request duration with quantiles (p50, p99) — CANNOT aggregate. PCA WILL test your ability to choose the right type.

  4 METRIC TYPES — Decision Matrix
  ════════════════════════════════
  
  COUNTER          GAUGE             HISTOGRAM          SUMMARY
  ───────          ─────             ─────────          ───────
  Only ↗️           ↗️ ↙️             Bucketed dist      Client quantiles
  .inc() only      .set() .inc()     .observe(0.34)     .observe(0.34)
                    .dec()
  ┌────────┐       ┌────────┐        ┌────────┐         ┌────────┐
  │Requests│       │Memory  │        │Latency │         │Latency │
  │Errors  │       │Active  │        │Size    │         │(p50,p99│
  │Jobs    │       │Users   │        │Duration│         │pre-calc│
  └────────┘       └────────┘        └────────┘         └────────┘
  Query: rate()    Query: raw       Query: histogram   Query: raw
  NEVER raw graph  NEVER rate()!      _quantile()       CANNOT sum()
  
  ✅ Aggregate    ✅ Aggregate      ✅ Aggregate       ❌ Aggregate!
     across pods     across pods       across pods        (can't avg %iles)
Q2

Which metric type should you use for counting job applications?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Counter — jobpost_applications_total.inc(). Counters only go up (or reset to 0 on restart). Use rate() to query the per-second application rate.

💡 EXPLANATION

Counters are for "events that happen" — applications submitted, logins, purchases, errors. They are monotonic (only .inc()). Never use .set() on a Counter! To track applications by job: jobpost_applications_total.labels(job_id="42").inc(). Query: rate(jobpost_applications_total[5m]) gives applications/second. The _total suffix is MANDATORY convention.

Q3

Why is Histogram preferred over Summary for distributed systems?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Histogram buckets can be aggregated across instances using sum() before histogram_quantile(). Summaries calculate quantiles on the client and cannot be aggregated — you can't average percentiles.

💡 EXPLANATION

Imagine 3 app instances behind a load balancer. Each Histogram reports: request took 0.12s → increment {le="0.25"} bucket. Prometheus: histogram_quantile(0.99, sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) by (le)) — this aggregates ALL instances' buckets into one correct p99. With Summaries: each instance computed its own p99 locally. You can't mathematically average 3 p99 values to get the overall p99. This is a PCA exam TRAP.

Q4

What is the naming convention for Prometheus metrics?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

namespace_subsystem_name_unit — all lowercase with underscores. Example: jobpost_http_requests_total. Counters end in _total, histograms use _seconds.

💡 EXPLANATION

Prometheus naming is strict for a reason — it enables automatic unit detection and tooling. namespace = project (jobpost), subsystem = component (django), name = what it measures (http_requests), unit = total/seconds/bytes. Always: lowercase, underscores, _total for counters, _seconds for duration. The exam may show you wrong names like "JobPostRequests" or "requestsCount" and ask what's wrong.

Q5

What is cardinality and why is it dangerous?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Cardinality = number of unique label combinations for a metric. High cardinality (e.g., using user IDs as labels) creates millions of time series, consuming excessive memory and slowing queries. Rule: keep label unique values <100.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each unique label combination = one time series stored in RAM. Metric http_requests_total{method, path, user_id} with 5 methods × 50 paths × 10,000 users = 2.5 MILLION time series — Prometheus dies. A typical Prometheus instance handles ~10M time series. Safe labels: method (5), status_code (~20), endpoint (~100). Dangerous: user_id, session_id, request_id, ip_address. PCA expects you to identify safe vs. dangerous labels instantly.

Q6

What does django-prometheus give you automatically?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

HTTP request counts by view, request latency histograms, database query duration metrics, cache hit/miss rates — all without writing any custom metric code. Just add the middleware and DB wrapper.

💡 EXPLANATION

django-prometheus is a GOLD MINE of free metrics. Just 3 steps: (1) pip install django-prometheus, (2) add 'django_prometheus' to INSTALLED_APPS + middleware + DB backend, (3) add path('', include('django_prometheus.urls')). You instantly get: django_http_requests_total_by_view_total, django_http_requests_latency_seconds, django_db_query_duration_seconds, django_cache_ops_total. That's RED method coverage with ZERO custom code!

Q7

Which of these should NOT be a label: endpoint, method, user_id, status_code?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

user_id — it has unbounded cardinality. Endpoint (~50 values), method (~5 values), and status_code (~20 values) are all safe, low-cardinality labels.

💡 EXPLANATION

The cardinality test: "Will this value grow with the number of users/requests?" If yes → DON'T use as label. Endpoint: fixed by codebase size (~50). Method: GET/POST/PUT/DELETE (5). Status_code: 200/301/404/500 (~20). User_id: grows forever with every new user. Instead of labeling by user_id, log user_id in your application logs and correlate via timestamps if needed. This is one of the most common PCA Gotcha questions.

Q8

How do you expose /metrics in a Django app?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Add path('', include('django_prometheus.urls')) to your URL configuration. This automatically creates the /metrics endpoint that Prometheus scrapes.

💡 EXPLANATION

The /metrics endpoint is the HTTP handler that renders all registered metrics in Prometheus text exposition format. For Django: from django.urls import path, include, then path('', include('django_prometheus.urls')). This gives you http://your-app:8000/metrics. Verify with: curl http://localhost:8000/metrics. For Flask: use prometheus_flask_exporter. For FastAPI: use prometheus_fastapi_instrumentator.

Q9

What is the RED method?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Rate (requests per second — Counter), Errors (failed requests — Counter), Duration (latency — Histogram). The 3 essential metrics every service endpoint should expose.

💡 EXPLANATION

Tom Wilkie's RED method is the PCA gold standard for service monitoring. Rate: rate(http_requests_total[5m]) — traffic level. Errors: rate(http_requests_total{status_code=~"5.."}[5m]) — failure rate. Duration: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) — latency. Together, these 3 metrics answer: "Is my service handling traffic? Is it failing? Is it slow?" Complemented by USE method (Utilization, Saturation, Errors) for infrastructure.

Q10

How does a Gauge differ from a Counter?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Counter only goes up (.inc()) — for totals like requests, errors, jobs created. A Gauge goes up AND down (.set(), .inc(), .dec()) — for current values like active listings, memory usage, queue depth.

💡 EXPLANATION

The mental model: Counter = odometer (only increases, reset to 0 on restart). Gauge = thermometer (goes up and down). Counter API: only .inc() and .inc(amount). Gauge API: .set(value), .inc(), .dec(), .set_to_current_time(). For active job listings: Gauge — it fluctuates as jobs are posted/filled. For total job posts ever: Counter — only increases. PCA tip: if the question asks about "current/active" values → Gauge. If it asks about "total/cumulative" → Counter.

  • ❌ #1: Using user_id or request_id as a label — explodes cardinality and kills Prometheus performance.
  • ❌ #2: Choosing Summary over Histogram for latency — Summaries can't be aggregated across instances!
  • ❌ #3: Naming a Counter without _total suffix — violates Prometheus naming conventions and confuses tooling.
  • ❌ #4: Defining metrics INSIDE a function or class — they get re-created on every call. Always define metrics at MODULE level!
  • ✅ Can you name all 4 metric types and explain when to use each?
  • ✅ Do you know the 3 layers of Django instrumentation (middleware → views → models)?
  • ✅ Can you explain why Histogram beats Summary for distributed systems?

Your Django App, Fully Monitored — End to End

This is where everything comes together. We take the anihpj Django project (with jobpost app) and give it a complete Prometheus monitoring stack: automatic Django middleware metrics, custom business metrics for job listings/applications, PostgreSQL and Redis exporters as sidecars, Node Exporter for system metrics, Docker Compose for local dev, and Kubernetes ServiceMonitor for production. By the end of this chapter, you'll have a production-ready monitoring setup that you can adapt to any Python/Django project.

🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 10 Must-Knows
ServiceMonitor CRDKubernetes custom resource that tells Prometheus Operator WHAT to scrape. Uses label selectors, NOT IP addresses.
