🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — How Everything Fits Together

This is the chapter where all previous chapters converge. You've learned about metric types (Ch 2), Prometheus architecture (Ch 3), exporters (Ch 5), PromQL (Ch 6-8), and client libraries (Ch 9). Now we assemble the complete puzzle for a real Django application.

Think of it like building a smart home:

🏠 The House = anihpj Django app. It has rooms (views), utilities (database, cache), and residents (users submitting job applications).

📡 The Sensors = Exporters + Client Libraries. Node Exporter = electricity meter (CPU/memory per node). PG Exporter = water meter (database queries). Redis Exporter = gas meter (cache). django-prometheus = smart thermostat (automatic metrics from the app itself). Custom metrics = doorbell counter (how many job applications today?).

📊 The Control Panel = Prometheus. It visits every sensor every 15 seconds, writes down the readings, and stores them. If a sensor stops reporting, Prometheus notices.

📺 The Dashboard Screen = Grafana. Instead of reading raw meter numbers, you see beautiful charts, gauges, and alerts on a screen.

The 3 deployment contexts:

1. Docker Compose (dev laptop): Everything in containers on one machine. Service names like pg-exporter:9187 resolve via Docker DNS. Fast feedback loop.

2. Kubernetes with Operator (production): ServiceMonitors auto-discover targets. Sidecar pattern — exporters share pod network namespace. PVC for Prometheus data persistence.

3. Kubernetes without Operator (hybrid): Use kubernetes_sd_configs directly. More manual but more control. Good for brownfield deployments.

10.1 The anihpj Monitoring Stack — Architecture Overview

🏗️ COMPLETE anihpj MONITORING ARCHITECTURE
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        anihpj MONITORING STACK                                 │
  │                                                                                │
  │  ┌── INFRASTRUCTURE LAYER ──────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │                                                                        │    │
  │  │  ┌───────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐              │    │
  │  │  │ Node Exporter │  │ Node Exporter │  │ Node Exporter │  DaemonSet   │    │
  │  │  │   :9100       │  │   :9100       │  │   :9100       │  1 per node  │    │
  │  │  │ CPU/Mem/Disk  │  │ CPU/Mem/Disk  │  │ CPU/Mem/Disk  │              │    │
  │  │  └───────────────┘  └───────────────┘  └───────────────┘              │    │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                                │
  │  ┌── APPLICATION LAYER (anihpj Pod) ──────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                                         │   │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐            │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj Django   │  │ PG Exporter    │  │ Redis         │            │   │
  │  │  │ :8000/metrics   │  │ :9187/metrics  │  │ Exporter      │            │   │
  │  │  │                 │  │                │  │ :9121/metrics │            │   │
  │  │  │ django_http_*   │  │ pg_stat_*      │  │ redis_*       │            │   │
  │  │  │ jobpost_*       │  │ pg_locks_*     │  │               │            │   │
  │  │  └────────┬────────┘  └───────┬────────┘  └───────┬───────┘            │   │
  │  │           │                   │                    │                    │   │
  │  │           ▼                   ▼                    ▼                    │   │
  │  │     Django ORM          PostgreSQL 15         Redis 7                   │   │
  │  │     (metrics via         (actual DB)          (cache/queue)             │   │
  │  │     DB wrapper)                                                        │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                                │
  │  ┌── MONITORING LAYER ────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                                                                         │   │
  │  │           ┌───────────────────┐     ┌───────────────────┐              │   │
  │  │           │    Prometheus     │────▶│     Grafana       │              │   │
  │  │           │     :9090         │     │     :3000         │              │   │
  │  │           │                   │     │                   │              │   │
  │  │           │  Scrapes ALL      │     │  RED Dashboards   │              │   │
  │  │           │  targets every    │     │  Business KPIs    │              │   │
  │  │           │  15 seconds       │     │  Alerts Panel     │              │   │
  │  │           └───────────────────┘     └───────────────────┘              │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                                │
  │  DATA FLOW:                                                                    │
  │    anihpj → /metrics → Prometheus scrape → TSDB → Grafana query → Dashboard   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

10.2 Django Middleware Metrics — What You Get for FREE

Just by adding django-prometheus middleware and DB wrapper (as shown in Chapter 9), your anihpj app automatically exposes these metrics with zero custom code:

Auto MetricTypeWhat It Tells YouExample Query
django_http_requests_total_by_view_transport_methodCounterRequest count per Django view, transport (http/https), and method (GET/POST)rate(django_http_requests_total_by_view_transport_method{view="jobpost.views.JobListView"}[5m])
django_http_requests_latency_seconds_by_view_methodHistogramResponse time distribution per view and methodhistogram_quantile(0.99, rate(django_http_requests_latency_seconds_by_view_method[5m]))
django_http_responses_total_by_status_view_methodCounterResponse count by HTTP status code, view, and methodrate(django_http_responses_total_by_status_view_method{status="500"}[5m])
django_db_query_duration_secondsHistogramDatabase query execution timehistogram_quantile(0.95, rate(django_db_query_duration_seconds[5m]))
django_db_errors_totalCounterDatabase error count by typerate(django_db_errors_total[5m])
django_cache_hits_totalCounterCache hit count (if using Redis cache backend)rate(django_cache_hits_total[5m])
django_cache_misses_totalCounterCache miss countrate(django_cache_misses_total[5m])
💡 The middleware metrics give you 80% of your observability needs for FREE. You can build complete RED dashboards (Rate, Errors, Duration) for every Django view without writing a single custom metric. The remaining 20% is custom business metrics (§10.3).

10.3 Custom Business Metrics for jobpost

Now we add metrics specific to the jobpost business logic — job creation, applications, searches, skills distribution. These go directly into your Django views:

Python — jobpost/views.py with Complete Instrumentation
# jobpost/views.py — Instrumented views for anihpj
from django.shortcuts import render, get_object_or_404
from django.http import JsonResponse
from django.db.models import Count, Avg
from .models import JobPost, Application, Skill
from .metrics import (
    jobpost_jobs_created_total,
    jobpost_applications_total,
    jobpost_active_listings,
    jobpost_request_duration_seconds,
    jobpost_search_duration_seconds,
    jobpost_skills_per_job,
)

import time

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# Job List View — List all active jobs with search
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
def job_list(request):
    start = time.time()
    query = request.GET.get('q', '')
    status_filter = request.GET.get('status', 'active')

    jobs = JobPost.objects.filter(status=status_filter)
    if query:
        jobs = jobs.filter(title__icontains=query) | jobs.filter(description__icontains=query)

    # Update Gauge: current active listings
    jobpost_active_listings.set(JobPost.objects.filter(status='active').count())

    # Track search latency
    jobpost_search_duration_seconds.observe(time.time() - start)

    # Track overall request latency per view
    jobpost_request_duration_seconds.labels(view='job_list', method=request.method).observe(time.time() - start)

    return render(request, 'jobpost/job_list.html', {'jobs': jobs})

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# Create Job — Track new job postings
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
def job_create(request):
    if request.method == 'POST':
        # ... form validation and job creation logic ...

        # INCREMENT Counter — a new job was created!
        jobpost_jobs_created_total.labels(
            status=job.status  # 'active' or 'draft'
        ).inc()

        # Update Gauge — active count just changed
        jobpost_active_listings.inc()

        # Track skill distribution
        avg_skills = JobPost.objects.aggregate(avg=Avg('skills__count'))['avg']
        jobpost_skills_per_job.set(avg_skills or 0)

    return render(request, 'jobpost/job_create.html')

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# Apply to Job — Track applications
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
def job_apply(request, job_id):
    job = get_object_or_404(JobPost, pk=job_id)

    if request.method == 'POST':
        try:
            # ... create Application object ...
            Application.objects.create(job=job, applicant=request.user, ...)

            # INCREMENT Counter: application submitted successfully
            jobpost_applications_total.labels(
                status='submitted',
                source='web'
            ).inc()

            return JsonResponse({'status': 'success'})

        except Exception as e:
            # Track application errors separately
            jobpost_applications_total.labels(
                status='error',
                source='web'
            ).inc()
            return JsonResponse({'status': 'error', 'message': str(e)}, status=500)

    return render(request, 'jobpost/job_apply.html', {'job': job})

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# Job Detail View
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
def job_detail(request, job_id):
    start = time.time()
    job = get_object_or_404(JobPost, pk=job_id)
    skills = job.skills.all()  # ManyToMany: Skills model

    jobpost_request_duration_seconds.labels(
        view='job_detail',
        method=request.method
    ).observe(time.time() - start)

    return render(request, 'jobpost/job_detail.html', {'job': job, 'skills': skills})
📈
Business Metrics Dashboard

With these metrics, you can build a Grafana dashboard showing: jobs created per hour, application rate, application success rate, average skills per job, most popular job categories — all from Prometheus data.

🔍
Search Performance

jobpost_search_duration_seconds lets you track if your search queries are slowing down. Set alert: "if p99 search latency > 2s for 5 minutes, investigate DB indexes."

10.4 Exporters as Sidecars — Docker Compose Setup

Here's the complete docker-compose.yml that spins up anihpj with ALL monitoring components. Every service gets its metrics endpoint — Prometheus scrapes them all:

docker-compose.yml — anihpj + Full Monitoring Stack
version: '3.8'
services:

  # ── anihpj Django Application ──
  web:
    build: .                     # Builds from Dockerfile
    ports:
      - "8000:8000"              # Django + /metrics endpoint
    environment:
      - DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE=anihpj.settings
      - DATABASE_URL=postgres://anihpj_user:supersecret@db:5432/anihpj
      - REDIS_URL=redis://redis:6379/0
    depends_on:
      - db
      - redis

  # ── PostgreSQL + Exporter ──
  db:
    image: postgres:15
    environment:
      - POSTGRES_DB=anihpj
      - POSTGRES_USER=anihpj_user
      - POSTGRES_PASSWORD=supersecret
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data

  pg-exporter:                     # ← PostgreSQL metrics at :9187
    image: prometheuscommunity/postgres-exporter
    ports:
      - "9187:9187"
    environment:
      - DATA_SOURCE_NAME=postgresql://anihpj_user:supersecret@db:5432/anihpj?sslmode=disable

  # ── Redis + Exporter ──
  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    volumes:
      - redisdata:/data

  redis-exporter:                  # ← Redis metrics at :9121
    image: oliver006/redis_exporter
    ports:
      - "9121:9121"
    command:
      - '--redis.addr=redis://redis:6379'

  # ── Prometheus ──
  prometheus:
    image: prom/prometheus:latest
    ports:
      - "9090:9090"              # Prometheus UI
    volumes:
      - ./prometheus.yml:/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml
      - prometheus_data:/prometheus
    command:
      - '--config.file=/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml'
      - '--storage.tsdb.path=/prometheus'
      - '--storage.tsdb.retention.time=15d'

  # ── Grafana ──
  grafana:
    image: grafana/grafana:latest
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"              # Grafana UI
    environment:
      - GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD=admin
    volumes:
      - grafana_data:/var/lib/grafana

  # ── Node Exporter (system metrics) ──
  node-exporter:
    image: prom/node-exporter:latest
    ports:
      - "9100:9100"
    volumes:
      - /proc:/host/proc:ro        # Read-only access to system
      - /sys:/host/sys:ro
      - /:/rootfs:ro
    command:
      - '--path.procfs=/host/proc'
      - '--path.sysfs=/host/sys'

volumes:
  pgdata:
  redisdata:
  prometheus_data:
  grafana_data:

# START EVERYTHING: docker compose up -d
# ACCESS: Prometheus → :9090 | Grafana → :3000 (admin/admin)
#         anihpj API → :8000/jobs/
Dockerfile — Production-Ready anihpj Image
# Dockerfile — anihpj Django App with Prometheus Instrumentation
FROM python:3.11-slim

# Prevent Python from buffering stdout/stderr (important for logging)
ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1

WORKDIR /app

# Install system dependencies
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
    gcc \
    libpq-dev \
    && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# Install Python dependencies
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# requirements.txt should include:
#   Django>=4.2
#   prometheus-client>=0.18
#   django-prometheus>=2.3
#   psycopg2-binary
#   redis
#   gunicorn

# Copy application code
COPY . .

# Collect static files
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

# Expose port 8000 (Django + /metrics)
EXPOSE 8000

# Health check — verify /metrics endpoint works
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --start-period=5s --retries=3 \
    CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/metrics || exit 1

# Run with gunicorn (production WSGI server)
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "4"]
prometheus.yml — Scrape Configuration for the Stack
global:
  scrape_interval: 15s
  evaluation_interval: 15s

scrape_configs:
  # Django application (self-instrumented)
  - job_name: 'anihpj-django'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['web:8000']
    metrics_path: '/metrics'

  # PostgreSQL exporter
  - job_name: 'anihpj-postgres'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['pg-exporter:9187']

  # Redis exporter
  - job_name: 'anihpj-redis'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['redis-exporter:9121']

  # Node exporter (system metrics)
  - job_name: 'node'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['node-exporter:9100']

  # Prometheus self-monitoring
  - job_name: 'prometheus'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['localhost:9090']

10.5 Instrumenting anihpj on Kubernetes

In production on Kubernetes, you use the Prometheus Operator with ServiceMonitor CRDs instead of static_configs. The Operator auto-discovers scrape targets by matching pod labels — no hardcoded IPs:

☸️ KUBERNETES SERVICE MONITOR — Auto-Discovery Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌────────────┐     ┌───────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐         │
  │  │ anihpj Pod │────▶│   Service     │────▶│ ServiceMonitor   │         │
  │  │ :8000      │     │ (label:       │     │ CRD              │         │
  │  │            │     │  app=anihpj)  │     │ selector:        │         │
  │  │ labels:    │     │               │     │  app=anihpj      │         │
  │  │ app=anihpj │     │ ClusterIP:    │     │ endpoints:       │         │
  │  │            │     │  anihpj-svc   │     │  port: metrics   │         │
  │  └────────────┘     └───────────────┘     └────────┬─────────┘         │
  │                                                     │                    │
  │                                                     ▼                    │
  │                                           ┌──────────────────┐         │
  │                                           │ Prometheus       │         │
  │                                           │ Operator         │         │
  │                                           │ (auto-generates  │         │
  │                                           │  scrape config)  │         │
  │                                           └──────────────────┘         │
  │                                                                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
K8s YAML — ServiceMonitor for anihpj Django
# anihpj-servicemonitor.yaml
apiVersion: monitoring.coreos.com/v1
kind: ServiceMonitor
metadata:
  name: anihpj-django
  namespace: anihpj
  labels:
    app: anihpj
    release: prometheus         # Must match Prometheus Operator's serviceMonitorSelector
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj               # ← Matches the Service's labels
  namespaceSelector:
    matchNames:
      - anihpj
  endpoints:
    - port: metrics             # ← Port name defined in Service
      interval: 15s
      path: /metrics
      scheme: http
---
# Service definition (must exist for ServiceMonitor to work)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-svc
  namespace: anihpj
  labels:
    app: anihpj                 # ← ServiceMonitor selector matches THIS
spec:
  selector:
    app: anihpj                 # ← Matches Pod labels
  ports:
    - name: metrics             # ← Endpoint name referenced above
      port: 8000
      targetPort: 8000
---
# PodMonitor — alternative, scrapes pods directly (no Service needed)
apiVersion: monitoring.coreos.com/v1
kind: PodMonitor
metadata:
  name: anihpj-postgres-exporter
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: postgres-exporter    # ← Matches exporter sidecar pod
  podMetricsEndpoints:
    - port: metrics
      interval: 30s
      path: /metrics
💡 ServiceMonitor vs PodMonitor: ServiceMonitor scrapes through a Kubernetes Service (recommended — handles pod restarts/IP changes). PodMonitor scrapes pods directly (useful for sidecar exporters that don't have their own Service). Both are CRDs provided by the Prometheus Operator.
anihpj ComponentK8s ObjectPortDiscovery Method
Django appServiceMonitor:8000Service label selector: app=anihpj
PostgreSQL ExporterPodMonitor:9187Pod label selector: app=postgres-exporter
Redis ExporterPodMonitor:9121Pod label selector: app=redis-exporter
Node ExporterDaemonSet (auto):9100Prometheus Operator auto-discovers

✅ Verifying Your Instrumentation — Step-by-Step Checklist

After deploying the stack, verify each layer works:

StepCommand / ActionExpected Result
1. Raw /metricscurl http://localhost:8000/metrics | head -20See django_http_requests_total, python_info, your custom metrics
2. Prometheus TargetsVisit http://localhost:9090/targetsAll 5 jobs show 🟢 UP with green status
3. Metric ExistsIn Prometheus UI: jobpost_active_listingsReturns a numeric value (not empty)
4. Generate Trafficcurl http://localhost:8000/jobs/ (repeat 10x)Counter and histogram values increase
5. Query Raterate(django_http_requests_total_by_view_transport_method{view="jobpost.views.job_list"}[5m])Returns non-zero rate
6. GrafanaVisit http://localhost:3000, add Prometheus data sourceCan query and graph anihpj metrics

🐍 Troubleshooting: If /metrics returns 404, check: (1) django_prometheus is in INSTALLED_APPS, (2) include('django_prometheus.urls') is in urls.py, (3) you restarted Django after changes.

PCA Exam: Docker Compose & Kubernetes Instrumentation

The exam will test: (1) Can you explain why exporters run as sidecars (same pod, localhost access)? (2) Do you know that depends_on only waits for container start, NOT readiness? Use healthcheck for readiness. (3) Can you distinguish ServiceMonitor (scrapes via Service) from PodMonitor (scrapes pods directly)? (4) Do you know that ServiceMonitor requires release: prometheus label to be discovered?

  • Memorize these ports: Node Exporter=9100, PostgreSQL=9187, Redis=9121, Blackbox=9115. These are guaranteed exam questions.
  • ServiceMonitor checklist: matchLabels on Service + port name in endpoints + release label on ServiceMonitor itself. All 3 must match!
  • Docker DNS rule: In Compose, use service names (not localhost) for cross-container communication. pg-exporter:9187 not localhost:9187.
🔧 Troubleshooting: "My ServiceMonitor isn't discovering targets!"

Symptom: kubectl get servicemonitor shows it exists, but Prometheus targets page shows nothing. Diagnosis steps: (1) Check ServiceMonitor labels match Prometheus CR's serviceMonitorSelector — usually release: prometheus. (2) Check Service exists with matching labels from ServiceMonitor's selector. (3) Check Service has correct port name matching the ServiceMonitor's port field. (4) Check pods behind the Service have the labels the Service selector expects. (5) Run kubectl describe prometheus -n monitoring and look at the Service Monitor Selector field. Root cause 90% of the time: Missing release: prometheus label on the ServiceMonitor.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 10 (Complete Setup)

Q1

What is a ServiceMonitor in the Prometheus Operator?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Kubernetes CRD that tells Prometheus what Services to scrape. It uses label selectors to match Services and automatically discovers endpoints without hardcoding IP addresses.

💡 EXPLANATION

ServiceMonitor is Kubernetes-native service discovery. Old way: edit prometheus.yml, add static_configs, reload Prometheus. New way: kubectl apply ServiceMonitor.yaml. The Prometheus Operator watches for ServiceMonitors that match its serviceMonitorSelector (usually release: prometheus). When matched, it reads the Service's endpoints and adds them as scrape targets. Pod dies and restarts with new IP → Service updates endpoints → Prometheus auto-updates. No more manual config!

Q2

How does django-prometheus add database metrics?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

By wrapping the database engine: set ENGINE: 'django_prometheus.db.backends.postgresql' in DATABASES. This intercepts all DB queries and emits timing/error metrics automatically.

💡 EXPLANATION

Django's DATABASES setting normally has ENGINE: 'django.db.backends.postgresql'. django-prometheus provides a drop-in replacement that wraps the real engine. Every time your view runs JobPost.objects.all(), the wrapper records: query duration (django_db_query_duration_seconds histogram), query count, and errors. This means you can see "the /jobs endpoint is slow because PostgreSQL is slow" without writing a single instrumentation line. Just change one ENGINE string.

Q3

What's the difference between ServiceMonitor and PodMonitor?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

ServiceMonitor scrapes through a Kubernetes Service (handles pod IP changes). PodMonitor scrapes pods directly by their labels. ServiceMonitor is preferred for application pods; PodMonitor is useful for sidecar exporters without their own Service.

💡 EXPLANATION

Think of it as a chain: Prometheus → ServiceMonitor → Service → Pods. The Service provides a stable DNS name and handles pod IP churn. ServiceMonitor gets the endpoint list from the Service. PodMonitor skips the Service entirely and scrapes pods by their labels directly. Use PodMonitor when you DON'T have a Service (e.g., Node Exporter DaemonSet where each pod is individually scraped). PCA may ask which one to use for a given scenario.

Q4

What metrics does django-prometheus expose automatically?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

HTTP request counts by view (Counter), request latency by view (Histogram), response counts by status code (Counter), database query duration (Histogram), DB errors (Counter), and cache hit/miss rates (Counter).

💡 EXPLANATION

This is the "80% of monitoring for 0% of effort" magic. Metrics breakdown: django_http_requests_total_by_view_total{view="jobpost.views.job_list"} (Counter — how many /jobs hits), django_http_requests_latency_seconds_by_view (Histogram — how fast), django_db_query_duration_seconds (Histogram — DB performance), django_cache_ops_total (Counter — Redis effectiveness). This gives you the RED method (Rate, Errors, Duration) for every view in your app automatically.

Q5

In the Docker Compose stack, why does Prometheus scrape 'pg-exporter:9187' and not 'localhost:9187'?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Because Prometheus runs in its own container. pg-exporter:9187 uses Docker's internal DNS — each container resolves other service names. localhost would refer to the Prometheus container itself, not the exporter.

💡 EXPLANATION

Docker Compose creates a default bridge network. Each service name (web, db, pg-exporter, prometheus) becomes a DNS name resolvable by all containers. Inside Prometheus container: localhost=itself, pg-exporter=other container. This is a common PCA trap! In kubernetes: use the Service name, e.g., pg-exporter-service:9187. Sidecar pattern exception: exporters in the SAME pod share localhost — then localhost:9187 IS correct.

Q6

How does a ServiceMonitor discover which pods to scrape?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

ServiceMonitor → matches Service by label → Service selects Pods by label → ServiceMonitor reads the Service's endpoint list → Prometheus scrapes each endpoint's /metrics.

💡 EXPLANATION

The complete discovery chain: (1) ServiceMonitor has selector: matchLabels: {app: anihpj}. (2) It finds a Service with label app: anihpj. (3) That Service has selector: {app: anihpj-web} matching pods. (4) The Service's Endpoints object lists: 10.244.1.5:8000, 10.244.2.3:8000. (5) ServiceMonitor tells Prometheus to scrape those IPs at /metrics every scrape_interval. This is how K8s service discovery replaces static_configs — dynamic, label-driven, zero manual IP management.

Q7

What's the sidecar pattern and why use it for exporters?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A sidecar is a container that runs in the same pod as the main application. Exporters as sidecars share the pod's network namespace, so they can access the app via localhost. They're deployed, scaled, and managed together with the app.

💡 EXPLANATION

In a Kubernetes Pod, all containers share the same network namespace (same IP, same localhost). This means the PG Exporter container can reach PostgreSQL at localhost:5432, and Redis Exporter can reach Redis at localhost:6379. Benefits: no separate Service needed for exporters, they scale with the app (3 replicas = 3 pairs), they share the pod's lifecycle (if app pod dies, exporter dies too — no orphaned monitoring). This is the K8s-native way to deploy exporters.

  SIDECAR PATTERN vs SEPARATE DEPLOYMENT
  ═══════════════════════════════════════
  
  SIDECAR (same pod)                  SEPARATE (different pods)
  ───────────────────                  ─────────────────────────
  ┌─────────────────────┐             ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │ anihpj Pod          │             │ anihpj   │  │ PG       │
  │ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ │             │ Pod      │  │ Exporter │
  │ │Django │ │PG Exp │ │             │ :8000    │  │ Pod      │
  │ │:8000  │ │:9187  │ │             └──────────┘  │ :9187    │
  │ └───┬───┘ └───┬───┘ │                           └──────────┘
  │     │         │     │             Need Service to connect!
  │     └───┬─────┘     │             PG Exp → Service → PG Pod
  │     localhost:5432   │
  │     ┌──────┐         │             ✅ SIDECAR advantages:
  │     │ PG   │         │              • localhost access
  │     │ DB   │         │              • scale together
  │     └──────┘         │              • shared lifecycle
  └─────────────────────┘              • no extra Service
Q8

What command starts the full anihpj monitoring stack?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

docker compose up -d — starts all 7 services: web, db, pg-exporter, redis, redis-exporter, prometheus, grafana, and node-exporter.

💡 EXPLANATION

The docker compose up -d command reads docker-compose.yml and starts all services as daemons (background). The anihpj stack has 8 services total: web (Django:8000), db (PostgreSQL:5432), pg-exporter (PG metrics:9187), redis (cache:6379), redis-exporter (Redis metrics:9121), prometheus (scraper:9090), grafana (dashboards:3000), node-exporter (host metrics:9100). Verify with: docker compose ps, check metrics: curl localhost:8000/metrics, check Prometheus: localhost:9090/targets.

Q9

What label must a ServiceMonitor have to be discovered by the Prometheus Operator?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The label specified in the Prometheus Operator's serviceMonitorSelector configuration (commonly release: prometheus). Without this matching label, the Operator ignores the ServiceMonitor.

💡 EXPLANATION

This is the #1 "why isn't it working?" issue. The Prometheus CR has serviceMonitorSelector: {matchLabels: {release: prometheus}}. Your ServiceMonitor MUST have that same label in its metadata.labels. Without it, kubectl get servicemonitor shows it exists, but Prometheus Operator ignores it entirely. Debug: kubectl describe prometheus -n monitoring and check the ServiceMonitor Selector field, then kubectl get servicemonitor -A --show-labels to verify yours has the matching label.

Q10

How do you track the number of currently active job postings?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Use a Gauge: jobpost_active_listings.set(JobPost.objects.filter(status='active').count()). Update it whenever a job is created, archived, or deleted. Query: jobpost_active_listings.

💡 EXPLANATION

Active listings is a Gauge because it goes up and down — not a Counter (jobs can be filled/archived). Implementation pattern: define jobpost_active_listings = Gauge('jobpost_active_listings', 'Currently active job postings') at module level. Then in your view/signal: after JobPost.save() → recalculate and .set(). Or better: use Django signals (post_save, post_delete) to keep the gauge updated automatically. This is a classic PCA scenario: choosing the right metric type for a real business KPI.

🏆 Production Recommendation for anihpj

Start with django-prometheus middleware — it gives you 80% of observability for zero effort (HTTP metrics, DB metrics, cache metrics). Then add custom business metrics for jobpost-specific KPIs (jobs created, applications, search latency). Use Docker Compose for local development with all sidecar exporters. For Kubernetes production, deploy with Prometheus Operator + ServiceMonitor for zero-config auto-discovery. Create a Grafana dashboard with RED panels (Rate, Errors, Duration) for every view plus a business KPI row. Set alerts on: p99 latency > 2s, error rate > 1%, and database query time > 500ms.

  • ❌ #1: Forgetting to add release: prometheus label to ServiceMonitor — Operator silently ignores it!
  • ❌ #2: Using localhost in Docker Compose scrape targets — containers can't see each other's localhost.
  • ❌ #3: Scraping exporters without depends_on — Prometheus starts before PostgreSQL Exporter is ready, causing scrape failures.
  • ❌ #4: Defining metrics inside functions — they get re-created on every call. Always define at module level!
  • ✅ Can you draw the anihpj monitoring architecture (infrastructure → app → monitoring layers)?
  • ✅ Do you know the difference between ServiceMonitor and PodMonitor?
  • ✅ Can you explain what django-prometheus gives you automatically vs what you must instrument manually?

Chapter 11: Alerting Rules — When Metrics Need Attention 🟡 Intermediate

From Data to Action — Making Prometheus Tell You When Things Break

Metrics are worthless if nobody looks at them. Alerting rules turn your PromQL queries into automated watchdogs that fire when thresholds are crossed. This chapter covers the complete alerting pipeline — from writing PromQL conditions in YAML to understanding severity levels and knowing exactly when to wake someone up at 3 AM. Alerting = 18% of the PCA exam.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🚨 PCA 18%📟 On-Call

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The complete Prometheus alerting pipeline — from scrape to notification
  • ✅ How to write alerting rules with `alert`, `expr`, `for`, `labels`, `annotations`
  • ✅ 8 critical alerts every anihpj deployment should have
  • ✅ Severity levels: critical, warning, info — and when to use each
  • ✅ The `for` duration — why it prevents flapping and false alarms
🎓 PCA Relevance
Alerting 18%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 11 Must-Knows
Alert Rule Anatomy5 fields: alert (name), expr (PromQL condition), for (duration), labels (severity/team), annotations (summary/runbook).
for = Flap ProtectionAlert must be true for for duration before firing. Prevents transient spikes from waking anyone up.
3 Severitiescritical (page NOW), warning (notify during hours), info (log for review).
Alert → AlertmanagerPrometheus fires alerts → sends to Alertmanager → routes to PagerDuty/Slack/Email.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Alerting in Plain English

Alerting = "If X happens for Y minutes, tell Z." That's it. You write "If error rate > 5% for 5 minutes, page the on-call engineer." Three moving parts:

1. The CONDITION (expr): A PromQL query that returns true/false. rate(errors[5m]) / rate(total[5m]) > 0.05 — "is the error rate above 5%?"

2. The WAIT (for): How long the condition must persist before alerting. for: 5m means "if it's been broken for 5 minutes straight, THEN fire." This prevents false alarms from momentary spikes. A 2-second CPU spike should NOT wake anyone up!

3. The DESTINATION (labels → Alertmanager): The severity label determines where it goes. severity: critical → PagerDuty (wakes someone up). severity: warning → Slack (check when convenient).

11.1 How Alerting Works in Prometheus

🚨 COMPLETE ALERTING PIPELINE — From Scrape to Wake-Up Call
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                          PROMETHEUS ALERTING PIPELINE                          │
  │                                                                               │
  │  1. SCRAPE          2. EVALUATE         3. FIRE            4. ROUTE           │
  │  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐       ┌─────────────┐    │
  │  │Prometheus│      │  Rules   │       │  Alert   │       │Alertmanager │    │
  │  │ scrapes  │─────▶│  Engine  │──────▶│  FIRES   │──────▶│             │    │
  │  │ /metrics │      │          │       │          │       │ Dedup +     │    │
  │  │every 15s │      │ Checks   │       │ "error   │       │ Group +     │    │
  │  │          │      │ PromQL   │       │  rate >  │       │ Route       │    │
  │  │          │      │ every    │       │  5%!"    │       │             │    │
  │  │          │      │ eval_    │       │          │       │             │    │
  │  │          │      │ interval │       │          │       │             │    │
  │  └──────────┘      └──────────┘       └──────────┘       └──────┬──────┘    │
  │                                                                  │           │
  │                          ┌───────────────────────────────────────┼───┐       │
  │                          │           ALERTMANAGER                │   │       │
  │                          │                                       │   │       │
  │                          │  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐  ┌───────┐ │   │       │
  │                          │  │ DEDUP   │  │ GROUP   │  │ ROUTE │ │   │       │
  │                          │  │ Same    │  │ Related │  │ Match │ │   │       │
  │                          │  │ alert   │  │ alerts  │  │labels │ │   │       │
  │                          │  │ from 2  │  │ → 1     │  │ →     │ │   │       │
  │                          │  │ Proms?  │  │ notif   │  │ dest  │ │   │       │
  │                          │  │ → 1     │  │         │  │       │ │   │       │
  │                          │  └─────────┘  └─────────┘  └───┬───┘ │   │       │
  │                          │                                │     │   │       │
  │                          │          ┌─────────────────────┼──┐  │   │       │
  │                          │          ▼                     ▼  ▼  │   │       │
  │                          │   ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────┐│   │       │
  │                          │   │PagerDuty │  │  Slack   │ │Email ││   │       │
  │                          │   │📟 WAKE UP│  │💬 NOTIFY │ │📧 LOG││   │       │
  │                          │   │(critical)│  │(warning) │ │(info)││   │       │
  │                          │   └──────────┘  └──────────┘ └──────┘│   │       │
  │                          └──────────────────────────────────────┘   │       │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ADDITIONAL ALERTMANAGER FEATURES:                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐                     │
  │  │  INHIBITION  │    │   SILENCES   │    │  TEMPLATES   │                     │
  │  │  If DC down, │    │  Mute during │    │  Customize   │                     │
  │  │  suppress    │    │  maintenance │    │  notif format│                     │
  │  │  app alerts  │    │  windows     │    │              │                     │
  │  └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The pipeline has 4 stages: (1) Prometheus scrapes metrics every 15s. (2) The rules engine evaluates alert expressions every evaluation_interval. (3) When an expression evaluates to true for the for duration, the alert FIRES. (4) Firing alerts are sent to Alertmanager, which deduplicates, groups, and routes them to the right destination.

11.2 Writing Alerting Rules (YAML)

Alerting rules live in YAML files referenced by prometheus.yml. Each rule has 5 key fields. Let's break down a complete rules file for anihpj:

rules/anihpj_alerts.yml — Complete Alerting Rules for anihpj
groups:
  - name: anihpj_critical        # ← Group name (organizational)
    rules:

      # ── ALERT 1: High Error Rate ──
      - alert: AnihpjHighErrorRate
        expr: |                    # ← Multi-line PromQL with pipe |
          (
            sum(rate(http_requests_total{
              job="anihpj-django",
              status=~"5.."}[5m]))
            /
            sum(rate(http_requests_total{
              job="anihpj-django"}[5m]))
          ) > 0.05                # ← 5% error rate threshold
        for: 5m                    # ← Must be true for 5 minutes
        labels:
          severity: critical       # ← Routes to PagerDuty
          team: platform
          component: anihpj-api
        annotations:
          summary: "anihpj error rate > 5%"
          description: "Error rate is {{ $value | humanize }}
            over the last 5 minutes"
          runbook_url: "https://wiki.anihpj.com/runbooks/errors"

      # ── ALERT 2: High Latency ──
      - alert: AnihpjHighLatency
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{
              job="anihpj-django"}[5m])) > 1.0
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: warning
          team: platform
        annotations:
          summary: "anihpj p99 latency > 1 second"
          description: "p99 latency: {{ $value }}s"

      # ── ALERT 3: Database Down ──
      - alert: AnihpjDatabaseDown
        expr: pg_up{job="postgres-exporter"} == 0
        for: 2m                    # ← Only 2 min for DB!
        labels:
          severity: critical
          team: database
        annotations:
          summary: "PostgreSQL is DOWN for anihpj"
          description: "pg_up = 0 for 2+ minutes"

      # ── ALERT 4: Redis Down ──
      - alert: AnihpjRedisDown
        expr: redis_up{job="redis-exporter"} == 0
        for: 2m
        labels:
          severity: critical
          team: platform
        annotations:
          summary: "Redis is DOWN for anihpj"

      # ── ALERT 5: Disk Filling Up (predictive!) ──
      - alert: AnihpjDiskFillingUp
        expr: |
          predict_linear(
            node_filesystem_free_bytes{mountpoint="/"}[1h],
            4 * 3600              # ← Predict 4 hours ahead
          ) < 0                    # ← Disk will be full
        for: 10m
        labels:
          severity: warning
        annotations:
          summary: "Disk predicted to fill in 4 hours"
FieldPurposeRequired?Example
alertHuman-readable alert name✅ YesAnihpjHighErrorRate
exprPromQL condition — must return a value✅ Yesrate(errors[5m]) > 0.05
forHow long condition must persistNo (default: 0s)5m
labelsAdditional labels for routingNoseverity: critical
annotationsHuman-readable info (not for routing)Nosummary:, runbook_url:
prometheus.yml — Connecting Prometheus to Alertmanager & Rule Files
# prometheus.yml — Key alerting configuration
global:
  scrape_interval: 15s
  evaluation_interval: 15s    # ← How often rules are evaluated

# ← Load alerting + recording rule files
rule_files:
  - 'rules/anihpj_alerts.yml'
  - 'rules/anihpj_recording.yml'

# ← Tell Prometheus WHERE to send firing alerts
alerting:
  alertmanagers:
    - static_configs:
        - targets:
            - 'alertmanager:9093'   # Alertmanager service
    # For HA, add multiple Alertmanager instances:
    #   - targets:
    #       - 'alertmanager-1:9093'
    #       - 'alertmanager-2:9093'

scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'anihpj-django'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['web:8000']
  # ... other scrape jobs ...
💡 Three places alerting is configured: (1) rule_files: in prometheus.yml — says WHERE to find the rule YAML files. (2) The rule YAML itself — defines WHAT conditions trigger alerts. (3) alerting: in prometheus.yml — says WHERE to send firing alerts (Alertmanager address). All three must be configured for alerting to work!

11.3 Critical Alerts for anihpj — 8 Alerts Every Deployment Needs

#AlertWhat It DetectsPromQL ExpressionforSeverity
1HighErrorRate5%+ of requests return 5xxrate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]) / rate(http_requests_total[5m]) > 0.055m🔴 critical
2HighLatencyp99 latency > 1 secondhistogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_bucket[5m])) > 15m🟡 warning
3DatabaseDownPostgreSQL unreachablepg_up{job="postgres-exporter"} == 02m🔴 critical
4RedisDownRedis unreachableredis_up{job="redis-exporter"} == 02m🔴 critical
5HighCPUNode CPU > 90% for 10 min100 - (avg(rate(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}[5m])) * 100) > 9010m🟡 warning
6DiskFillingUpDisk predicted to fill in 4hpredict_linear(node_filesystem_free_bytes[1h], 4*3600) < 010m🟡 warning
7CertExpiringTLS cert expiring in <7 daysprobe_ssl_earliest_cert_expiry - time() < 86400 * 71h🟡 warning
8TooManyRestartsPod restarting >5× in 15 minrate(kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total[15m]) > 55m🟡 warning
💡 The `for` duration is your friend: Without for, an alert fires the INSTANT the condition becomes true — even a 1-second spike. With for: 5m, it must be continuously true for 5 minutes. This prevents "flapping" alerts that fire and resolve every 30 seconds.

11.4 Alert Severity Levels — When to Wake Someone Up

🔴
critical

Page the on-call engineer IMMEDIATELY. Service is down or about to go down. User-facing impact. Requires human response within 5 minutes.

anihpj: DatabaseDown, RedisDown, HighErrorRate

🟡
warning

Notify during business hours. Something needs attention soon but service is still running. Check within 1-4 hours.

anihpj: HighLatency, HighCPU, DiskFillingUp, CertExpiring

🔵
info

Log for review, no notification. Interesting but not actionable. Useful for post-mortems and trend analysis.

anihpj: DeploymentCompleted, ConfigChange, NewUserSignup

🧭 Severity Decision Tree — Which Level Should I Use?

Q1: Are users currently unable to use the service?
YES → 🔴 critical
Page on-call NOW. PagerDuty. 5-min SLA.
NO → Go to Q2
Q2: Will this become user-impacting within 4 hours if ignored?
YES → 🟡 warning
Slack/Teams during work hours.
NO → 🔵 info
Log for review. No notification needed.
PCA Exam: Alerting Rules

The exam WILL test: (1) Can you identify the 5 fields of an alert rule? (2) Do you understand what for does (prevents flapping)? (3) Can you read a PromQL expr and explain what condition triggers the alert? (4) Do you know that labels are for ROUTING (Alertmanager reads them) while annotations are for HUMANS (summary, runbook)? (5) Can you distinguish the 3 severity levels?

  • 5 fields mnemonic: A.E.F.L.A. — Alert name, Expression (PromQL), For (duration), Labels (routing), Annotations (human info).
  • for values cheat sheet: DB/Redis down → 2m. Error rate/latency → 5m. CPU/disk → 10m. Cert expiry → 1h. Shorter for binary failures, longer for trends.
  • labels vs annotations: Labels = machine routing (Alertmanager reads them). Annotations = human reading (summary, description, runbook_url). NEVER put routing info in annotations.

11.5 Alert Rule Debugging & Testing — Before You Wake Up On-Call

🔍 ALERT DEBUGGING WORKFLOW — 5-Step Checklist
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     ALERT DEBUGGING FLOW                                       │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 1: TEST THE PromQL                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Go to :9090/graph → Paste the expr → Does it return data?            │    │
  │  │ If empty: check label matchers, metric name, time range              │    │
  │  │ If value is wrong: check rate() vs raw, unit conversions             │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 2: CHECK ALERT STATE                                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Go to :9090/alerts → Find your alert → State should be:              │    │
  │  │   • "inactive" = condition not met                                   │    │
  │  │   • "pending"  = condition met, waiting for `for` duration           │    │
  │  │   • "firing"   = condition met for `for` duration → alert active!    │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 3: VERIFY rule_files                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Check prometheus.yml → rule_files: MUST include your alert file!     │    │
  │  │ Prometheus silently ignores rules not in rule_files — no error!      │    │
  │  │ Fix: add file path, then POST /-/reload or restart Prometheus.      │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 4: CHECK ALERTMANAGER CONNECTION                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ :9090/status → Runtime Information → Alertmanagers                   │    │
  │  │ Should show URL + "UP". If "DOWN":                                   │    │
  │  │   • Check alerting: block in prometheus.yml                          │    │
  │  │   • Check Alertmanager is running and reachable                      │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  STEP 5: SIMULATE TESTING                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Temporarily lower thresholds to force alert to fire:                 │    │
  │  │   expr: rate(http_errors[5m]) > 0  # Always true if any errors!     │    │
  │  │   for: 0s  # Fire immediately                                        │    │
  │  │ Then check Alertmanager → PagerDuty/Slack for notification.         │    │
  │  │ REMEMBER TO REVERT after testing!                                     │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🧪
Testing Without Affecting Production

Create a separate rules/test_alerts.yml file with severity: test label. Configure Alertmanager to route test alerts to a dedicated Slack channel or email. This way you can test freely without paging anyone.

📊
Monitoring Your Alerts

Prometheus exposes alert metrics: ALERTS{alertname="HighErrorRate"} = 1 when firing. alertmanager_alerts_total = count of alerts. Use these to build an "Alert Health" dashboard showing firing/pending counts over time.

🚨 Troubleshooting: "My alert isn't firing even though the condition is true!"

Checklist: (1) Is the alert rule file in prometheus.yml's rule_files:? If not, Prometheus silently skips it. (2) Is the PromQL expression returning data? Test at :9090/graph. (3) Did you forget for:? Check :9090/alerts for pending/firing state. (4) Are labels correct for routing? Severity in annotations instead of labels = Alertmanager can't route. (5) Is Alertmanager reachable? Check :9090/status. Most common: rule file not in rule_files: — no warning, just silently ignored.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 11 (Alerting Rules)

Q1

What are the 5 fields of a Prometheus alerting rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

alert (name), expr (PromQL condition), for (duration condition must persist), labels (for routing; severity, team), annotations (for humans; summary, description, runbook_url). alert and expr are required; the rest are optional.

💡 EXPLANATION

Think of alert rules as 5 questions: (1) alert: "What should we call this alarm?" (2) expr: "What PromQL query triggers it?" (3) for: "How long must it be broken before alerting?" (4) labels: "Where should Alertmanager route this?" (5) annotations: "What should the human see?" PCA will show you a YAML snippet and ask you to identify which field is wrong — memorize all 5 names and their purposes.

  ALERT RULE ANATOMY — 5 Fields (A.E.F.L.A.)
  ════════════════════════════════════════════
  
  groups:
    - name: anihpj_alerts
      rules:
        - alert: HighErrorRate          ← ① ALERT: unique name
          expr: |                        ← ② EXPR: PromQL condition
            rate(http_500s[5m])
            /
            rate(http_total[5m]) > 0.05
          for: 5m                        ← ③ FOR: must persist 5 min
          labels:                        ← ④ LABELS: routing
            severity: critical            (Alertmanager reads these)
            team: backend
          annotations:                   ← ⑤ ANNOTATIONS: human info
            summary: "Error rate > 5%"   (on-call engineer reads)
            runbook: https://wiki/err
  
  ① alert    → What's the alarm called?
  ② expr     → When should it trigger?
  ③ for      → How long to wait?
  ④ labels   → Where to send it?
  ⑤ annotations → What to tell the human?
Q2

What does the `for` field do in an alerting rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Prevents flapping. The alert only fires if the PromQL expression evaluates to true CONTINUOUSLY for the entire for duration. Without it, even a 1-second spike would fire the alert. Typical values: 2m for DB, 5m for latency, 10m for disk.

💡 EXPLANATION

Without for, a 0.1s CPU spike to 100% wakes up the on-call engineer at 3 AM — they check and everything is fine. This causes ALERT FATIGUE: after enough false alarms, people ignore all alerts, including real ones. The for duration is your trade-off: shorter = faster detection but more false alarms. Longer = fewer false alarms but slower response. Typical: 2m for availability (binary up/down), 5m for latency, 10m for slow-changing resources like disk.

Q3

What's the difference between labels and annotations in an alert rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

labels are for ROUTING — Alertmanager reads them to decide where to send the alert (severity, team). annotations are for HUMANS — summary, description, runbook_url. Labels are machine-actionable; annotations are human-readable.

💡 EXPLANATION

PCA LOVES this distinction. labels: {severity: critical, team: backend} → Alertmanager sees "severity=critical" and routes to PagerDuty. annotations: {summary: "PostgreSQL is DOWN", runbook_url: "https://wiki.anihpj.com/pg-down"} → the on-call engineer sees these in the notification. Never put routing info in annotations or human info in labels. Exam trick: a rule with severity in annotations instead of labels — that's wrong because Alertmanager can't see annotations for routing decisions.

Q4

What are the 3 standard alert severity levels?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

critical — page on-call immediately (PagerDuty). warning — notify during business hours (Slack). info — log for review, no notification needed.

💡 EXPLANATION

The severity taxonomy: critical = "wake someone up at 3 AM" (DB down, site offline, SSL cert expiring in 1h). warning = "deal with this tomorrow morning" (disk 80% full, CPU sustained at 85%, latency creeping up). info = "FYI for the dashboard" (deployment completed, autoscaling event, config change detected). The PCA exam expects you to classify scenarios: "The website returns 500 errors — what severity?" → critical. "Disk usage at 75% — what severity?" → warning.

Q5

How does `predict_linear()` work in the DiskFillingUp alert?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

predict_linear() takes a range vector and projects the trend forward by a specified number of seconds. predict_linear(disk_free[1h], 4*3600) < 0 means "based on the last hour's trend, will disk be full in 4 hours?" — predictive alerting.

💡 EXPLANATION

predict_linear is PromQL's crystal ball. It fits a linear regression line to the data points in the range and extends it forward. predict_linear(node_filesystem_free_bytes[1h], 4*3600): look at the last hour of disk free bytes, fit a line, project 4 hours ahead. If the projected value < 0 → disk will be full in < 4 hours → ALERT NOW, before it's too late. This is MUCH better than "disk > 90%" because it gives you time to react. PCA will ask about predict_linear specifically.

Q6

Why is `for: 2m` used for DatabaseDown but `for: 10m` for DiskFillingUp?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

DatabaseDown is binary and urgent — it's either up or down, and every second matters. 2 minutes accounts for brief network blips. Disk usage is slow-changing — a 2-minute spike in disk writes is normal. 10 minutes confirms the trend is real.

💡 EXPLANATION

The for duration should match the metric's natural behavior. Binary metrics (up/down): short for (1-2m) — if it's down for 2 minutes, it's REALLY down, not a network hiccup. Rate metrics (latency, error rate): medium for (5m) — gives rate() enough data points. Slow-changing metrics (disk, memory): long for (10-30m) — these change gradually, no need for urgency. PCA scenario: "You set for:0s on a CPU alert. What happens?" → Alert fatigue from every momentary spike.

Q7

Where do you configure which alert rule files Prometheus loads?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In prometheus.yml under rule_files:. Example: rule_files: ['rules/anihpj_alerts.yml', 'rules/node_alerts.yml']. Prometheus loads all specified files and evaluates them every evaluation_interval.

💡 EXPLANATION

rule_files is a top-level block in prometheus.yml (sibling to scrape_configs). It takes a list of glob patterns. Prometheus loads all matching YAML files at startup and continuously evaluates the rules inside. If you create an alert file but forget to add it to rule_files, Prometheus will NEVER evaluate it — silent failure, no error message. This is a common PCA gotcha: "You wrote the alert rule. Why isn't it firing?" → Because you didn't add it to rule_files in prometheus.yml.

Q8

What happens after an alert fires in Prometheus?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Prometheus sends the firing alert to Alertmanager (configured via alerting: block in prometheus.yml). Alertmanager then deduplicates (same alert from multiple Prometheus instances), groups (related alerts together), and routes (by severity/team labels to PagerDuty, Slack, Email).

💡 EXPLANATION

The complete flow: Prometheus rule fires → alert state = FIRING → Prometheus pushes to Alertmanager's /api/v2/alerts endpoint → Alertmanager receives, then in order: (1) Inhibit: check if any higher-priority alert suppresses this one, (2) Silence: check if there's an active silence matching this alert, (3) Dedup: same alert from multiple Prometheus instances → keep one, (4) Group: bundle related alerts by group_by labels, (5) Route: match against route tree, send to receiver. Know this sequence for PCA!

Q9

Can you have an alert rule without a `for` field?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes. If for is omitted, it defaults to 0s — the alert fires the instant the condition becomes true. This is NOT recommended for production because even a split-second spike will trigger the alert. Always set a reasonable for duration.

💡 EXPLANATION

Omitting for is technically valid YAML but operationally dangerous. Every scrape interval (~15s), Prometheus evaluates the rule. If for=0s (or omitted), the FIRST time the expression is true, the alert fires. Imagine: expr: rate(http_500s[1m]) > 0, for omitted. Even a single 500 error fires the alert — even if the next 1000 requests succeed. In production: ALWAYS set for ≥ 1m. Alertmanager also has group_wait (wait before sending first notification of a group) which adds another layer of spike protection.

Q10

What PromQL expression detects that PostgreSQL is down?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

pg_up{job="postgres-exporter"} == 0 — the PostgreSQL exporter sets pg_up to 1 when it can connect and 0 when it can't. This is the simplest and most reliable "is it alive?" check.

💡 EXPLANATION

Most exporters expose an _up metric: pg_up, redis_up, node_exporter_up. These are the simplest possible health checks — the exporter itself reports whether it can reach its target. pg_up == 1: PostgreSQL Exporter successfully connected and ran its queries. pg_up == 0: connection failed, or PostgreSQL is down, or exporter itself crashed. Combine with for: 2m for a reliable DatabaseDown alert. This pattern works for ANY exporter — memorize _up metric usage.

  • ❌ #1: Forgetting for — alert fires on millisecond spikes, causing alert fatigue and ignored pages.
  • ❌ #2: Confusing labels (for Alertmanager routing) with annotations (for human readers). The PCA exam WILL test this distinction.
  • ❌ #3: Using rate() on a gauge in an alert expr — rate() is only for Counters. Use direct gauge comparison: memory_usage > 90.
  • ❌ #4: Not specifying rule_files in prometheus.yml — Prometheus silently ignores your alert rules!
  • ✅ Can you write an alert rule from scratch with all 5 fields?
  • ✅ Do you know the difference between labels (routing) and annotations (human info)?
  • ✅ Can you explain why for: 5m prevents flapping and false alarms?

Chapter 12: Alertmanager — Routing, Grouping, and Silence 🟡 Intermediate

Alertmanager — The Air Traffic Controller of Alerts

Alertmanager is what happens AFTER Prometheus fires an alert. It deduplicates the same alert from multiple Prometheus instances, groups related alerts so the on-call engineer gets ONE notification (not 50), routes different alerts to different receivers (PagerDuty for critical, Slack for warnings), inhibits low-priority alerts when a higher-priority one is firing, and silences alerts during maintenance windows. Master Alertmanager and you master production alerting.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🚨 PCA 18%📬 Routing

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The 5 functions of Alertmanager: dedup, group, route, inhibit, silence
  • ✅ How to build a route tree that sends critical→PagerDuty, warning→Slack
  • ✅ Grouping: how 50 pod failures become ONE smart notification
  • ✅ All receiver types: PagerDuty, Slack, Email, Webhook, OpsGenie
  • ✅ Silences — muting alerts during planned maintenance
🎓 PCA Relevance
Alerting 18%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 12 Must-Knows
5 FunctionsDeduplication, Grouping, Routing, Inhibition, Silencing. Memorize these — guaranteed exam question.
Route TreeNested routes: with match: and match_re:. First match wins. Default receiver catches unmatched.
group_byAlerts with same label values are grouped. group_by: ['alertname'] = one notif per alert type.
InhibitionIf alert A fires, suppress alert B. Example: datacenter down → suppress all app-level alerts.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Alertmanager Made Simple

Alertmanager is the post office for your alerts. Prometheus writes alert letters. Alertmanager sorts them, bundles related ones together, and delivers them to the right person.

The 5 functions in plain English:

1. Dedup: "Oh, Prometheus A and Prometheus B both sent the same alert? I'll keep just one copy."

2. Group: "50 pods just crashed? Instead of 50 individual pages, I'll send ONE message: '50 pods in CrashLoopBackOff.'"

3. Route: "This alert has severity: critical — send it to PagerDuty. This one has severity: warning — send it to Slack."

4. Inhibit: "The entire datacenter is down. Don't ALSO page about individual app errors — the DC alert explains everything."

5. Silence: "We're upgrading the database from 2-4 AM. Mute all database alerts during that window."

12.1 Alertmanager Architecture — The 5 Functions

🔧 ALERTMANAGER — 5 FUNCTIONS IN ORDER
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     ALERTMANAGER PROCESSING PIPELINE                       │
  │                                                                           │
  │  Alerts from                                                            │
  │  Prometheus ──▶  1. DEDUP    ──▶  2. GROUP    ──▶  3. ROUTE             │
  │                   ┌────────┐      ┌────────┐      ┌────────┐            │
  │                   │ Same   │      │ 50 pod │      │ severity│            │
  │                   │ alert  │      │ alerts │      │ =crit→ │            │
  │                   │ from 2 │      │ → ONE  │      │ PagerD  │            │
  │                   │ Proms? │      │ group  │      │ =warn→ │            │
  │                   │ → keep │      │ notif  │      │ Slack  │            │
  │                   │  one   │      │        │      │        │            │
  │                   └────────┘      └────────┘      └────────┘            │
  │                                                                           │
  │  4. INHIBIT                          5. SILENCE                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐              ┌────────────────────┐              │
  │  │ Datacenter is DOWN │              │ Maintenance window │              │
  │  │   → Suppress ALL   │              │ Jan 15, 2-4 AM     │              │
  │  │     app-level      │              │   → Mute all       │              │
  │  │     alerts!        │              │     anihpj alerts  │              │
  │  └────────────────────┘              └────────────────────┘              │
  │                                                                           │
  │  RESULT: On-call engineer gets ONE clean notification, not a firehose.   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔄
Deduplication

If you run 2 Prometheus instances for HA, both fire the same alert. Alertmanager keeps only ONE copy. Identified by alert labels (name + severity + instance).

📦
Grouping

Related alerts are bundled. 50 pods crash = ONE notification: "50 pods in CrashLoopBackOff" — not 50 individual pages. Uses group_by labels.

12.2 Routing Alerts to the Right Team — The Route Tree

🌲 ROUTE TREE — How Alerts Find Their Destination
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                           ROUTE TREE ANATOMY                               │
  │                                                                           │
  │                        ┌──────────────────┐                               │
  │                        │  DEFAULT RECEIVER │  ← Catch-all                 │
  │                        │  email: platform  │    (no match found)          │
  │                        │  @anihpj.com      │                              │
  │                        └────────┬─────────┘                               │
  │                                 │                                         │
  │            ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐                    │
  │            │                    │                    │                    │
  │            ▼                    ▼                    ▼                    │
  │     ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐              │
  │     │ match:     │      │ match:     │      │ match:     │              │
  │     │ severity=  │      │ severity=  │      │ severity=  │              │
  │     │ critical   │      │ warning    │      │ info       │              │
  │     └─────┬──────┘      └─────┬──────┘      └─────┬──────┘              │
  │           │                   │                    │                      │
  │           ▼                   ▼                    ▼                      │
  │     ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐                  │
  │     │PAGERDUTY │       │  SLACK   │        │  EMAIL   │                  │
  │     │📟 WAKE UP│       │💬 NOTIFY │        │📧 LOG IT │                  │
  │     └──────────┘       └────┬─────┘        └──────────┘                  │
  │                             │                                             │
  │               ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐                              │
  │               │             │             │                              │
  │               ▼             ▼             ▼                              │
  │        ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                         │
  │        │ team=    │  │ team=    │  │ team=    │  ← Further routing      │
  │        │ platform │  │ dev      │  │ database │    by team label        │
  │        └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                         │
  │                                                                           │
  │  RULES: • First match wins (top-to-bottom evaluation)                     │
  │         • match_re: {severity: "critical|warning"} for regex matching    │
  │         • Default receiver catches everything that didn't match          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
alertmanager.yml — Route Configuration for anihpj
global:
  slack_api_url: 'https://hooks.slack.com/services/...'
  pagerduty_url: 'https://events.pagerduty.com/v2/enqueue'

route:
  group_by: ['alertname', 'namespace']
  group_wait: 30s
  group_interval: 5m
  repeat_interval: 4h
  receiver: 'platform-email'           # ← Default (catch-all)

  routes:
    # Critical → PagerDuty (wake someone up)
    - match:
        severity: critical
      receiver: 'pagerduty-oncall'
      continue: true                    # ← Continue to next route

    # Warning → Slack (check when free)
    - match:
        severity: warning
      receiver: 'slack-platform'

    # Database team gets all DB-related alerts
    - match_re:
        team: 'database'
      receiver: 'slack-database-team'

    # Info → just email (no one needs to wake up)
    - match:
        severity: info
      receiver: 'platform-email'

receivers:
  - name: 'pagerduty-oncall'
    pagerduty_configs:
      - routing_key: 'abc123def456'
        severity: '{{ .CommonLabels.severity }}'
        description: '{{ .CommonAnnotations.description }}'

  - name: 'slack-platform'
    slack_configs:
      - channel: '#anihpj-alerts'
        title: '{{ .GroupLabels.alertname }}'
        text: '{{ .CommonAnnotations.description }}'

  - name: 'slack-database-team'
    slack_configs:
      - channel: '#db-alerts'
        title: '{{ .GroupLabels.alertname }}'

  - name: 'platform-email'
    email_configs:
      - to: 'platform@anihpj.com'
        headers:
          Subject: '[{{ .Status }}] {{ .GroupLabels.alertname }}'

12.3 Grouping — Don't Flood the On-Call Engineer

📊 GROUPING — 50 Alerts → 1 Smart Notification
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                           │
  │   ❌ WITHOUT GROUPING:                                                     │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │   │  📟 "Pod web-1 crashed!"                                          │   │
  │   │  📟 "Pod web-2 crashed!"                                          │   │
  │   │  📟 "Pod web-3 crashed!"      50 SEPARATE ALERTS                  │   │
  │   │  📟 "Pod web-4 crashed!"      → Engineer's phone explodes         │   │
  │   │  ... (×46 more!)               → Can't see the big picture        │   │
  │   │  📟 "Pod web-50 crashed!"                                          │   │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                           │
  │   ✅ WITH GROUPING (group_by: ['alertname', 'namespace']):                 │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │   │                                                                    │   │
  │   │   group_wait: 30s  ← Wait 30s for sibling alerts                   │   │
  │   │        │                                                           │   │
  │   │        ▼                                                           │   │
  │   │   📟 ONE SMART NOTIFICATION:                                        │   │
  │   │      "PodCrashLooping in namespace anihpj"                          │   │
  │   │      "50 pods affected: web-1, web-2, ..., web-50"                 │   │
  │   │                                                                    │   │
  │   │   group_interval: 5m  ← Updates every 5 min (no spam)              │   │
  │   │   repeat_interval: 4h ← Re-send if still firing after 4h           │   │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                           │
  │   KEY PARAMETERS:                                                         │
  │   ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │   │ group_wait      │ Wait time for MORE alerts in the same group   │     │
  │   │ (default: 30s)  │ before sending the first notification         │     │
  │   ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤     │
  │   │ group_interval  │ Minimum time between notifications for the    │     │
  │   │ (default: 5m)   │ SAME group (prevents spam)                    │     │
  │   ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤     │
  │   │ repeat_interval │ How often to re-send if still firing          │     │
  │   │ (default: 4h)   │ (reminder: "this is STILL broken")            │     │
  │   └─────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

12.4 Receivers — Where Alerts Go

ReceiverUse CaseConfig Keyanihpj Setup
📟 PagerDutyOn-call rotation, wake someone uppagerduty_configscritical alerts → PagerDuty
💬 SlackChat notifications during business hoursslack_configswarning alerts → #anihpj-alerts

💡 Receiver routing is label-driven: The severity and team labels you set in alerting rules (Chapter 11) determine which receiver gets the alert. This is why labeling your alerts correctly is so important!

12.5 Silences & Maintenance Windows

✅ When to Use Silences
  • 🛠️ Planned maintenance: "Upgrading PostgreSQL tonight 2-4 AM — silence all DB alerts."
  • 🐛 Known issues being fixed: "We know the search is slow, the fix is deploying now. Silence for 30 min."
  • 🧪 Load testing: "Running a stress test — latency WILL spike. Silence during test window."
  • 📅 Always set an EXPIRY — never create a silence without an end time!
❌ When NOT to Silence
  • ❌ Alerts you find annoying — fix the root cause or adjust the threshold, don't silence indefinitely.
  • ❌ "Temporary" silences that never expire — you WILL forget. Always set an expiry.
  • ❌ Security-related alerts — certificate expiry, unusual access patterns. Never silence security.
Silence via Alertmanager API (or UI at :9093)
# Create a silence via amtool (CLI) or Alertmanager UI at :9093/#/silences
# Silence all anihpj alerts during PostgreSQL upgrade
amtool silence add \
  --alertmanager.url=http://localhost:9093 \
  --comment="PostgreSQL upgrade to v16" \
  --duration=2h \
  --author="platform-team" \
  instance=~"anihpj-db.*"
PCA Exam: Alertmanager

The exam will test: (1) Can you name the 5 Alertmanager functions in order (dedup → group → route → inhibit → silence)? (2) Do you understand the route tree — match: uses labels to direct alerts? (3) What does group_by do? (4) How does inhibition work (if alert A fires, suppress B)? (5) What are silences used for and why should they always have an expiry?

  • 5 Functions mnemonic: D.G.R.I.S. — Dedup, Group, Route, Inhibit, Silence. In that exact order. Exam WILL ask you to name them.
  • Route tree = if/else: First match wins (unless continue:true). Always set a default receiver as safety net. Unmatched alerts go there.
  • Silence expiry rule: ALWAYS set an end time. Permanent silence = missed critical alerts = production outage. Check active silences after every maintenance window.
  • group_wait vs group_interval: group_wait = wait for MORE alerts (30s). group_interval = wait BETWEEN updates (5m). repeat_interval = re-send reminder (4h). Don't confuse them!

12.6 Receiver Configuration — Complete Examples for Every Channel

alertmanager.yml — All 6 Receiver Types Configured
global:
  slack_api_url: 'https://hooks.slack.com/services/T.../B.../xxx'

route:
  receiver: 'platform-email'          # Default catch-all
  group_by: ['alertname', 'severity']
  group_wait: 30s
  group_interval: 5m
  repeat_interval: 4h
  routes:
    - match:
        severity: critical
      receiver: 'pagerduty-critical'
      continue: true                   # Also send to Slack!
    - match:
        severity: critical
      receiver: 'slack-critical'
    - match:
        severity: warning
      receiver: 'slack-warning'
    - match_re:
        team: 'database|platform'
      receiver: 'slack-engineering'

receivers:
  - name: 'pagerduty-critical'
    pagerduty_configs:
      - routing_key: 'abc123xyz'
        severity: 'critical'
        description: '{{ .CommonAnnotations.description }}'

  - name: 'slack-critical'
    slack_configs:
      - channel: '#alerts-critical'
        title: '🚨 {{ .GroupLabels.alertname }}'
        text: '{{ .CommonAnnotations.description }}'
        send_resolved: true

  - name: 'slack-warning'
    slack_configs:
      - channel: '#alerts-warning'
        title: '⚠️ {{ .GroupLabels.alertname }}'

  - name: 'slack-engineering'
    slack_configs:
      - channel: '#engineering'
        title: '[{{ .GroupLabels.severity }}] {{ .GroupLabels.alertname }}'

  - name: 'platform-email'
    email_configs:
      - to: 'platform-team@anihpj.com'
        from: 'alertmanager@anihpj.com'
        smarthost: 'smtp.anihpj.com:587'
        require_tls: true
