📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 12 (Alertmanager)

Q1

What are the 5 functions of Alertmanager, in order?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1. Deduplication (remove duplicate alerts from multiple Prometheus instances), 2. Grouping (bundle related alerts), 3. Routing (match labels → receiver), 4. Inhibition (suppress B when A fires), 5. Silencing (mute during maintenance).

💡 EXPLANATION

PCA GUARANTEES a question about the 5 functions. Memorize them in order: D-G-R-I-S. Dedup: two Prometheus instances each fire "DatabaseDown" → Alertmanager keeps one. Group: 100 pod crash alerts → one message "100 pods crashing." Route: severity=critical → PagerDuty, severity=warning → Slack. Inhibit: datacenter down → suppress all app-level alerts (they're caused by the DC outage). Silence: maintenance window 2-4 AM → mute all alerts with env=prod.

  ALERTMANAGER — 5 Functions (D-G-R-I-S)
  ═══════════════════════════════════
  
  Alerts In ──▶ ① DEDUP ──▶ ② GROUP ──▶ ③ ROUTE ──▶ PagerDuty
                    │            │            │           Slack
                    │            │            │           Email
              ④ INHIBIT    ⑤ SILENCE
              (suppress    (mute during
               if parent    maintenance)
               alert fires)
  
  ① DEDUP:  2 Proms → same alert → keep one
     "DatabaseDown" from A + "DatabaseDown" from B → 1 notif
  
  ② GROUP:  50 pods crash → 1 message
     "50 pods in CrashLoopBackOff" not 50 individual pages
  
  ③ ROUTE:  severity=critical → PagerDuty
             severity=warning  → Slack
             severity=info     → Email
  
  ④ INHIBIT: Datacenter down → suppress all app alerts
     "Don't page about pods when the whole DC is dead"
  
  ⑤ SILENCE: "Mute everything from 2-4 AM for DB upgrade"
Q2

What does `group_by` do in Alertmanager?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Alerts with the same label values for the specified labels are grouped into one notification. group_by: ['alertname', 'namespace'] means all "PodCrashLooping" alerts in "anihpj" namespace → ONE Slack message.

💡 EXPLANATION

Without grouping: 50 pods crash → 50 PagerDuty calls → on-call engineer rage quits. With group_by: ['alertname']: all 50 become ONE message: "[FIRING] PodCrashLooping (50 instances)" with details in the body. Common group_by patterns: ['alertname'] (one per alert type), ['alertname', 'severity'] (one per alert type+severity combo), ['...'] (empty = all alerts in one big notification — rarely used).

Q3

What's the difference between group_wait, group_interval, and repeat_interval?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

group_wait (30s): wait for more alerts before sending first notification. group_interval (5m): minimum time between updates for the same group. repeat_interval (4h): how often to re-send if the alert is still firing (reminder).

💡 EXPLANATION

These 3 timers control notification frequency. group_wait: "Alert came in, but wait 30s — maybe 50 more are coming. THEN send." group_interval: "Already sent a notification for this group. Wait 5 minutes before sending updates about new/add'l alerts in this group." repeat_interval: "Alert is still firing after 4 hours — remind the on-call engineer it's still broken." PCA exam will ask you to match each definition to its name.

Q4

How does the route tree work in Alertmanager?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Routes are evaluated top to bottom. Each route has a match: or match_re: that checks alert labels. First match wins (unless continue: true is set). The default receiver catches unmatched alerts.

💡 EXPLANATION

The route tree is like if-else chains with a default fallback. Example: route 1: match severity=critical → PagerDuty. route 2 (nested under 1): match team=database → PagerDuty-DB. route 3: match severity=warning → Slack. If none match: default receiver (email-platform). Order matters! Put more specific matches FIRST. The alert walks the tree top-down; first match wins and stops (unless continue:true). PCA scenario: "Where does alert{severity:warning, team:backend} go?" → traces the tree.

Q5

What is inhibition in Alertmanager?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

When a higher-priority alert fires, suppress lower-priority ones. Example: if the datacenter is down, don't ALSO page about individual app errors — the DC alert explains everything. Configured via inhibit_rules: in alertmanager.yml.

💡 EXPLANATION

Inhibition prevents alert storms from masking the root cause. Syntax: inhibit_rules: - source_match: {severity: critical} target_match: {severity: warning} equal: ['namespace']. This means: "If a critical alert is firing in namespace X, suppress all warning alerts in that same namespace." The on-call engineer sees "DatacenterUnreachable" instead of 200 "Pod not ready" alerts. Inhibition requires matching labels (equal field) between source and target.

Q6

What happens if no route matches an alert?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

The alert goes to the default receiver defined in the top-level route: block. This is your safety net — every alert must go somewhere.

💡 EXPLANATION

The top-level route block MUST have a receiver. This is the catch-all. route: receiver: 'platform-email' routes: [...]. If you forget the default receiver and no child route matches, Alertmanager has nowhere to send the alert — it disappears silently. This is a critical production gotcha. Always test with: amtool check-config alertmanager.yml and simulate with: curl -X POST alertmanager:9093/api/v2/alerts.

Q7

Why should silences always have an expiry?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Because people forget. A silence without an expiry could mute critical alerts forever — you'll only discover it after an outage. Always set endsAt or a duration.

💡 EXPLANATION

Silences are created via Alertmanager UI or API. If you set no expiry, the silence lives forever. Scenario: "DB upgrade from 2-4 AM" → create silence. Upgrade finishes at 3:30 AM. 4:01 AM — silence auto-expires, alerts resume. But if you forgot the expiry: the silence is STILL active at 4:01 AM, DB crashes at 5 AM, NO ALERT IS SENT. Production outage with no notification. ALWAYS set an explicit end time, and check active silences after maintenance windows.

Q8

How does Alertmanager deduplicate alerts?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Alerts with the same labels (excluding volatile ones like instance) are considered duplicates. The first one received is kept; subsequent identical alerts are discarded. This is critical for HA Prometheus setups.

💡 EXPLANATION

In HA Prometheus (2+ Prometheus instances scraping the same targets), both evaluate the same rules and both fire "DatabaseDown." Without dedup, the on-call gets 2 PagerDuty calls for the same problem. Alertmanager compares: alertname + all non-volatile labels. If identical → merge into one, tracking which Prometheus instances reported it. Dedup is automatic — you don't configure it. It just works. This is why Prometheus can be run in HA without alert storms.

Q9

How do you configure Alertmanager to receive alerts from Prometheus?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In prometheus.yml under alerting:: alertmanagers: - static_configs: - targets: ['alertmanager:9093']. This tells Prometheus where to send firing alerts.

💡 EXPLANATION

The alerting block in prometheus.yml is separate from scrape_configs. It tells Prometheus where Alertmanager lives. Default Alertmanager port: 9093. In Docker: alertmanager:9093 (service name). In K8s: alertmanager-operated.monitoring.svc:9093. Prometheus pushes alerts to Alertmanager (push model), unlike metrics which Prometheus scrapes (pull model). Also configure alert_relabel_configs under alerting to modify alert labels before sending.

Q10

Can you route alerts to different Slack channels based on team?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes. Create nested routes with match: {team: "database"} → receiver: slack-database-team, match: {team: "platform"} → receiver: slack-platform. Each receiver has its own Slack channel config.

💡 EXPLANATION

Team-based routing is the most common production pattern. Your alert rules add labels: {team: database, severity: critical}. Alertmanager route tree: child route 1: match {team: database} → receiver: pagerduty-db (critical) / slack-db (warning). child route 2: match {team: platform} → receiver: pagerduty-platform / slack-platform. Each receiver is configured separately in receivers section with its own Slack API URL, channel name, and mention settings. The PCA exam may show you a route tree and ask you to predict where a specific alert goes.

  • ❌ #1: Confusing group_wait (wait for MORE alerts) with group_interval (wait BETWEEN notifications). Different purposes!
  • ❌ #2: Not setting a default receiver — alerts with no matching route disappear silently.
  • ❌ #3: Creating silences without expiry — you WILL forget and critical alerts WILL be missed.
  • ❌ #4: Forgetting that match: requires EXACT match; use match_re: for regex patterns like severity: "critical|warning".
  • ✅ Can you name and explain all 5 Alertmanager functions?
  • ✅ Do you understand the route tree — how labels determine where alerts go?
  • ✅ Can you explain why grouping prevents alert fatigue?

Chapter 13: Recording Rules — Pre-Compute for Speed 🟡 Intermediate

Why Wait 15 Seconds for a Dashboard When You Can Wait 0?

Recording rules pre-compute expensive PromQL queries at evaluation_interval and store the results as new time series. When a dashboard queries the pre-computed metric, it returns instantly — no heavy computation needed. Think of them as materialized views for time-series data. Essential for production dashboards that query weeks of data.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🚨 PCA 18%⚡ Performance

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why recording rules are essential for production dashboards
  • ✅ How to write recording rules: `record`, `expr`, `labels`
  • ✅ Naming convention: `level:metric:operation`
  • ✅ 8 essential recording rules every anihpj deployment should have
  • ✅ Recording rules vs alerting rules — when to use each
🎓 PCA Relevance
Alerting 18%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 13 Must-Knows
Recording = CachePre-compute expensive queries. Result stored as new metric. Dashboard reads new metric (instant).
Naming Conventionlevel:metric:operation. Example: job:http_errors:rate5m (job-level, http errors, rate over 5m).
Recording vs AlertingRecording: record: field → stores result. Alerting: alert: field → fires notification. Different purposes.

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Recording Rules in Plain English

Recording rules = pre-calculated answers. Imagine you're a math teacher. Every day, 30 students ask: "What's the average test score from the last 30 days?" Instead of recalculating from 900 data points each time, you calculate it ONCE in the morning, write it on the board, and point to it all day. That's a recording rule.

Without recording rules (painful): VP opens Grafana dashboard → Grafana asks Prometheus: "histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_bucket[30d]))" → Prometheus scans 30 days of raw histogram data, millions of samples → 15 seconds later → dashboard loads. VP closes dashboard. 5 minutes later, VP opens it again → same 15-second wait.

With recording rules (fast): Prometheus evaluates record: job:http_latency:p99_30d every 60 seconds → stores result as a regular metric → VP opens dashboard → Grafana queries job:http_latency:p99_30d → under 100ms — instant! The pre-computed answer is already stored.

The trade-off is disk, not CPU. Each recording rule adds a new time series to TSDB. 10 rules × 50 label combos = 500 series ≈ 50MB/year. That's trivial. The benefit (15-second dashboard → instant) is enormous. Rule of thumb: always use recording rules for queries scanning >1 hour of data or using histogram_quantile().

13.1 Why Recording Rules? — Performance & Consistency

⚡ RECORDING RULES — Before vs After
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                           │
  │   ❌ WITHOUT RECORDING RULES:                                              │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │   │  User opens dashboard → Grafana queries Prometheus               │   │
  │   │  PromQL: histogram_quantile(0.99,                                │   │
  │   │    rate(http_duration_bucket[30d]))  ← 30 DAYS of raw data!      │   │
  │   │  ⏱️ 10-15 seconds of computation...                               │   │
  │   │  Dashboard loads 😤                                              │   │
  │   │  EVERY user repeats this wait!  EVERY dashboard refresh!         │   │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                           │
  │   ✅ WITH RECORDING RULES:                                                 │
  │   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │   │  Recording Rule (evaluated every 60s):                            │   │
  │   │    record: job:http_latency:p99_30d                               │   │
  │   │    expr: histogram_quantile(0.99,                                 │   │
  │   │      rate(http_duration_bucket[30d]))                             │   │
  │   │                          │                                        │   │
  │   │                          ▼                                        │   │
  │   │  Pre-computes ONCE → stores as new time series                    │   │
  │   │                          │                                        │   │
  │   │                          ▼                                        │   │
  │   │  Dashboard: job:http_latency:p99_30d  ← Just reads one metric!   │   │
  │   │  ⚡ Instant! Under 100ms!                                         │   │
  │   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                           │
  │   💡 Recording rules trade DISK SPACE for QUERY SPEED.                     │
  │      The pre-computed result is stored as a regular time series.          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚡
Speed

Computing p99 latency over 30 days of raw histogram data takes 10-15 seconds. Reading a pre-computed recording rule takes <100ms. Dashboards go from frustrating to instant.

🔄
Consistency

Without recording rules, the same query run 5 seconds apart might return slightly different results (new data arrived). Recording rules freeze the result at evaluation time — everyone sees the same value.

13.2 Writing Recording Rules

Recording rules use the same YAML structure as alerting rules but with record: instead of alert::

rules/anihpj_recording.yml — Recording Rules for anihpj
groups:
  - name: anihpj_recording_rules
    interval: 60s              # Evaluate every 60 seconds
    rules:

      # ── Pre-computed error rate by endpoint ──
      - record: job:http_errors:rate5m
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{
            job="anihpj-django",
            status=~"5.."}[5m]))
          by (endpoint)
        labels:
          team: platform

      # ── Pre-computed p99 latency ──
      - record: job:http_latency:p99_5m
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{
              job="anihpj-django"}[5m]))
            by (le, endpoint))

      # ── Pre-computed request rate per endpoint ──
      - record: job:http_requests:rate5m_by_endpoint
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{
            job="anihpj-django"}[5m]))
          by (endpoint, method)

      # ── Pre-computed CPU per node ──
      - record: instance:node_cpu:avg5m
        expr: |
          100 - (
            avg by (instance) (
              rate(node_cpu_seconds_total{
                mode="idle"}[5m])
            ) * 100
          )
FieldRecording RuleAlerting Rule
What it doesStores pre-computed result as new metricFires a notification when condition is true
Key fieldrecord: — name of output metricalert: — name of the alert
Has for?❌ No — evaluates immediately✅ Yes — prevents flapping
Has annotations?❌ No — it's just a metric✅ Yes — summary, runbook
OutputNew time series in TSDBNotification to Alertmanager

13.3 Essential Recording Rules for anihpj — 8 Rules for Any Project

#Recording Rule NameWhat It Pre-ComputesWhy It's Essential
1job:http_errors:rate5m5xx error rate per endpointUsed in EVERY dashboard panel and alert
2job:http_latency:p99_5mp99 latency per endpointMost expensive query — huge benefit from pre-computation
3job:http_requests:rate5m_by_endpointRequest rate per endpoint+methodFeed for RED dashboards
4instance:node_cpu:avg5mCPU usage per nodeSystem health dashboards
5instance:node_memory:usageMemory usage per nodeCapacity planning
6job:db_queries:rate5mDatabase queries per secondDatabase health monitoring
7job:cache_hit_ratio:5mRedis cache hit ratioCache efficiency tracking
8job:business:jobs_created_rate1hJobs created per hourBusiness KPI dashboard
PCA Exam: Recording Rules

The exam will test: (1) Can you distinguish recording rules (record:) from alerting rules (alert:)? (2) Do you understand the naming convention level:metric:operation? (3) Why use recording rules? (Speed + consistency). (4) Where are they configured? (Same rule_files as alerting rules, just different YAML structure).

  • Naming mnemonic: who:what:how — who (job/instance level), what (metric name), how (operation). Example: job:http_errors:rate5m = job-level + http errors + rate over 5m.
  • When to use: If a dashboard query takes >3 seconds, make it a recording rule. If the same PromQL appears in 3+ dashboards, make it a recording rule. If it queries >1 day of data, make it a recording rule.
  • Recording rules can feed alerts: Pre-compute job:http_errors:rate5m as a recording rule, then alert on job:http_errors:rate5m > 0.05. Cheaper evaluation, cleaner alert expressions.
🏆 Production Pattern: Recording Rules Pipeline

The complete chain: (1) Raw metric scraped every 15s → TSDB. (2) Recording rule every 60s: record: job:http_latency:p99_5m, expr: histogram_quantile(0.99, sum(rate(bucket[5m])) by (le)). (3) Pre-computed result stored as new time series. (4) Grafana reads job:http_latency:p99_5m in <100ms. (5) Alert reads same metric: job:http_latency:p99_5m > 1. Key insight: One recording rule feeds BOTH dashboards and alerts. Change the calculation once → everything updates. This is the production monitoring architecture.

13.4 Recording Rules — Production Best Practices & Pitfalls

📋 RECORDING RULES — Do's and Don'ts Checklist
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     RECORDING RULES BEST PRACTICES                             │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ✅ DO:                                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ • Pre-compute histogram_quantile() queries — biggest performance win  │    │
  │  │ • Pre-compute queries scanning >1 day of data                          │    │
  │  │ • Follow level:metric:operation naming convention                      │    │
  │  │ • Use recording rules to feed BOTH dashboards and alerting rules       │    │
  │  │ • Set shorter evaluation_interval for critical rules (30s vs 60s)     │    │
  │  │ • Keep recording and alerting rules in SEPARATE files for clarity     │    │
  │  │ • Monitor recording rule evaluation duration (rule_evaluation_*)      │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                               │
  │  ❌ DON'T:                                                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ • Pre-compute simple queries that run in <100ms — waste of disk       │    │
  │  │ • Create recording rules with HIGH cardinality output                  │    │
  │  │    (sum by(user_id) — user_id has unbounded unique values)            │    │
  │  │ • Forget to add new recording rules to rule_files: in prometheus.yml │    │
  │  │ • Put for:, labels:, or annotations: in recording rules (alert-only)  │    │
  │  │ • Create recording rules for metrics you don't actually query          │    │
  │  │ • Neglect to monitor TSDB size growth from recording rules            │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Recording Rules — Complete YAML Example with Groups
# rules/anihpj_recording.yml
groups:
  # ── Fast rules (evaluate every 30s) ──
  - name: anihpj_fast_recording
    interval: 30s
    rules:
      - record: job:http_errors:rate5m
        expr: sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])) by (endpoint)
      
      - record: job:http_requests:rate5m_by_endpoint
        expr: sum(rate(jobpost_http_requests_total[5m])) by (endpoint, method)
  
  # ── Medium rules (evaluate every 60s) ──
  - name: anihpj_medium_recording
    interval: 60s
    rules:
      - record: job:http_latency:p99_5m
        expr: histogram_quantile(0.99, sum(rate(jobpost_http_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) by (le, endpoint))
      
      - record: job:cache_hit_ratio:5m
        expr: |
          rate(redis_keyspace_hits_total[5m])
          /
          (rate(redis_keyspace_hits_total[5m]) + rate(redis_keyspace_misses_total[5m]))
  
  # ── Slow rules (evaluate every 5m) ──
  - name: anihpj_slow_recording
    interval: 5m
    rules:
      - record: job:http_latency:p99_30d
        expr: histogram_quantile(0.99, sum(rate(jobpost_http_duration_seconds_bucket[30d])) by (le))
      
      - record: job:business:jobs_created_rate1h
        expr: rate(jobpost_jobs_created_total[1h]) * 3600
⚡
Performance Impact

Recording rules ADD to CPU usage (evaluating the PromQL) but SUBTRACT from it when dashboards read pre-computed values. Net effect is usually positive — one CPU-intensive pre-computation saves hundreds of dashboard-load computations.

💾
Storage Impact

Each recording rule creates new time series. Estimate: samples = num_label_combos × (86400 / interval). 50 combos × 1440 samples/day = 72K samples/day = ~150KB/day per rule. Negligible for most deployments.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 13 (Recording Rules)

Q1

What is the primary purpose of recording rules?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

To pre-compute expensive PromQL queries and store the results as new time series. Dashboards then read the pre-computed metric instead of re-running the expensive query every time — giving instant load times.

💡 EXPLANATION

Recording rules are like materialized views in SQL databases. Instead of running histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_duration_bucket[30d])) every time a dashboard loads (taking 10-15 seconds), you pre-compute it once every 60 seconds and store the result as job:http_latency:p99_30d. The dashboard reads this pre-computed metric in <100ms. The trade: more disk space (storing the pre-computed series) for dramatically faster dashboards.

Q2

What's the difference between `record:` and `alert:` in rule files?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

record: stores the PromQL result as a new time series (for dashboards/queries). alert: fires a notification when the PromQL condition is true (for alerting). Recording rules have no for, labels, or annotations like alerting rules do.

💡 EXPLANATION

Same YAML file, same groups structure, different keyword. Recording: record: job:http_errors:rate5m + expr: rate(http_errors_total[5m]). Alerting: alert: HighErrorRate + expr: rate(http_errors_total[5m]) > 0.05 + for: 5m. Recording rules should NOT have for/labels/annotations — those are alerting-only fields. Recording rules produce a metric; alerting rules produce a notification. PCA WILL show you a YAML snippet and ask "is this recording or alerting?"

Q3

What is the naming convention for recording rule output metrics?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

level:metric:operation — colon-separated. level = aggregation level (job, instance), metric = what's measured, operation = what was done. Example: job:http_errors:rate5m means "job-level HTTP error rate over 5 minutes."

💡 EXPLANATION

Prometheus convention: level tells you aggregation scope — job: (per-service), instance: (per-host), cluster: (per-K8s-cluster), global: (everything). metric is what you're measuring — http_errors, cpu_usage, disk_free. operation is what you did — rate5m (rate over 5m), sum, avg, p99. This naming convention makes dashboards self-documenting. You see job:http_latency:p99_30d and instantly know: "service-level 99th percentile HTTP latency over 30 days." No guesswork.

Q4

Why would you use a recording rule instead of running the query directly?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

For performance (pre-computed vs real-time computation) and consistency (all dashboard users see the same value). Especially important for queries spanning large time ranges (30 days) or heavy aggregations (histogram_quantile over thousands of buckets).

💡 EXPLANATION

Two reasons: (1) Performance — a query scanning 30 days of histogram data processes millions of samples. Computing it at dashboard load time = 10-15s wait. Pre-computed = instant. (2) Consistency — if VP opens dashboard at 10:00:05 and you open it at 10:00:09, without recording rules you might see slightly different p99 values (new data arrived). Recording rules "freeze" the value at evaluation time — everyone sees the same number. Critical for business dashboards.

Q5

Do recording rules have a `for` field?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No. Recording rules evaluate immediately at each evaluation_interval and store the result. The for field is only for alerting rules (to prevent flapping).

💡 EXPLANATION

Recording rules don't need a for because they're not checking a condition — they're computing a value. Every evaluation_interval (default 60s), the rule runs: "compute rate(http_errors_total[5m]), store as job:http_errors:rate5m." There's no "wait before storing" concept — you want the latest value now. Alerting rules need for to say "wait 5 minutes before telling someone." Different purposes → different fields. PCA trick: showing a recording rule with a for field and asking "what's wrong?"

Q6

Where are recording rules configured?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

In the same YAML files as alerting rules, referenced by rule_files: in prometheus.yml. Both recording and alerting rules can coexist in the same file — they're distinguished by record: vs alert:.

💡 EXPLANATION

Both rule types live under rule_files in prometheus.yml. Example groups block: one group for recording (interval: 60s, rules with record:), another group for alerting (interval: 30s, rules with alert:). They can be in the same file or separate files: rule_files: ['rules/recording.yml', 'rules/alerts.yml']. Prometheus loads both at startup and evaluates each group at its configured interval. The key distinction in the YAML is the record: vs alert: field.

Q7

What's the trade-off of using recording rules?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

You trade disk space for query speed. Each recording rule creates a new time series that consumes storage. However, the speed improvement (15s → 100ms) is almost always worth the disk cost in production.

💡 EXPLANATION

Every recording rule output is a new time series stored in TSDB. Cost: ~2 bytes per sample + overhead. If your rule evaluates every 60 seconds, that's 1,440 samples/day per label combination. With 10 recording rules × 50 label combos each = 500 time series = ~50MB/year. The benefit: dashboards go from 15s to 100ms. Users get frustrated with slow dashboards; they don't notice 50MB of extra disk. Rule of thumb: always use recording rules for queries scanning >1 hour of data or using histogram_quantile().

Q8

What does `job:http_latency:p99_5m` tell you from its name?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

job-level aggregation of HTTP latency at the 99th percentile over a 5-minute window. The naming convention level:metric:operation makes the metric self-documenting.

💡 EXPLANATION

Breaking it down: job = aggregated across all instances of a service (not per-pod). http_latency = what's being measured (HTTP request duration). p99 = the statistical operation (99th percentile). 5m = the time window (5 minutes). This naming convention is a Prometheus community standard documented at prometheus.io/docs/practices/naming/. PCA expects you to recognize this format and understand each part. It also helps prevent metric name collisions between teams.

Q9

Can a recording rule be used as input to an alerting rule?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes! This is a common pattern. Pre-compute a complex query as a recording rule, then use the simpler recorded metric in an alert expression. Example: record job:http_errors:rate5m, then alert on job:http_errors:rate5m > 0.05.

💡 EXPLANATION

Chaining recording → alerting is a production best practice. (1) Recording rule pre-computes: job:http_errors:rate5m = rate(http_errors_total[5m]). (2) Alerting rule reads the simple metric: alert: HighErrorRate, expr: job:http_errors:rate5m > 0.05. Benefits: the alert expression is simpler (easier to debug), the recording rule can be reused by multiple alerts AND dashboards, and if you change the calculation (e.g., from 5m to 10m window), you update ONE recording rule, not 5 alerts.

Q10

How often do recording rules evaluate?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

At the evaluation_interval configured in the rule group (or the global evaluation_interval in prometheus.yml). Default is 1 minute. You can set a per-group interval: for different evaluation frequencies.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each rule group has its own interval. Example: groups: - name: fast_rules interval: 30s rules: [recording rules needing fresh data] and - name: slow_rules interval: 5m rules: [recording rules over 30d windows]. Why different intervals? Fast-changing metrics (error rate over 5m) benefit from 30s evaluation. Slow metrics (monthly trend) are fine at 5m. The global evaluation_interval is the default. Per-group interval overrides it. This gives you fine-grained control over freshness vs. CPU cost.

  • ❌ #1: Confusing recording rules (record:) with alerting rules (alert:). They look similar in YAML but serve completely different purposes!
  • ❌ #2: Using recording rules for simple queries that run instantly anyway — you're just wasting disk space.
  • ❌ #3: Forgetting that recording rules consume TSDB storage. Too many rules with high cardinality can fill your disk.
  • ❌ #4: Not following the level:metric:operation naming convention — makes dashboards confusing and unmaintainable.
  • ✅ Can you explain the difference between recording rules and alerting rules?
  • ✅ Do you know the naming convention: level:metric:operation?
  • ✅ Can you explain why pre-computation makes dashboards dramatically faster?

Chapter 14: Grafana — Turning Data into Dashboards 🟡 Intermediate

Prometheus Stores the Data — Grafana Makes It Beautiful

Prometheus is a time-series database with a basic graph UI. Grafana is the industry-standard visualization platform that connects to Prometheus (and 100+ other data sources) to create rich, interactive dashboards. Together they form the most popular monitoring stack in the world. This chapter covers building dashboards for anihpj, using variables for dynamic filtering, and designing the 5 essential dashboards every application needs.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🚨 PCA 18%📊 Viz

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why Grafana + Prometheus is the industry standard monitoring stack
  • ✅ How to build a dashboard — panels, PromQL queries, visualization types
  • ✅ Dashboard variables ($namespace, $pod, $endpoint) for dynamic filtering
  • ✅ 5 essential dashboards for anihpj: RED, Database, Cache, System, Business
  • ✅ Grafana Alerting vs Prometheus Alertmanager — when to use each
🎓 PCA Relevance
Alerting 18%
🎓 PCA Exam Cheat Sheet — Chapter 14 Must-Knows
Grafana + PrometheusGrafana = visualization layer. Prometheus = storage + query. Separate tools, perfect together.
VariablesDynamic dashboard filters: $namespace, $pod, $endpoint. Populated from Prometheus label values.
Panel TypesTime series (line graph), Stat (single number), Gauge (percentage), Heatmap (distribution), Table (list).

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Grafana in Plain English

Think of Prometheus as a giant spreadsheet of numbers and Grafana as the charting tool. Prometheus stores "at 10:00:00, CPU was 45%, at 10:00:15, CPU was 47%..." — millions of data points. Grafana turns those numbers into beautiful, interactive charts that humans can understand at a glance.

The magic of variables: Without variables, you'd need to create a separate dashboard for every environment (dev, staging, prod) and every service. With variables like $namespace, you create ONE dashboard with a dropdown at the top. Switch from "dev" to "prod" and ALL panels update instantly. This is why Grafana is so powerful — one dashboard rules them all.

Grafana is NOT a replacement for Prometheus: Grafana doesn't store metrics. It doesn't scrape targets. It doesn't evaluate alerting rules. It just queries Prometheus (and other data sources) and displays the results beautifully. You ALWAYS need Prometheus running behind Grafana.

14.2 Building Your First Dashboard — anihpj API Overview

1
Add Data Source

In Grafana: Configuration → Data Sources → Add Prometheus. URL: http://prometheus:9090. Click "Save & Test".

2
Create Dashboard

Click "+" → Dashboard → Add Panel. Choose visualization type.

3
Write PromQL

Enter your PromQL query in the query editor. Grafana auto-completes metric names!

4
Configure Display

Set title, legend, units (seconds, bytes, percent), thresholds, and time range.

Grafana Dashboard JSON Snippet — RED Panel for anihpj
{
  "dashboard": {
    "title": "anihpj API Overview",
    "panels": [
      {
        "title": "Request Rate (RPS)",
        "type": "timeseries",
        "targets": [{
          "expr": "sum(rate(http_requests_total{job=\"anihpj-django\"}[5m])) by (endpoint)",
          "legendFormat": "{{endpoint}}"
        }]
      },
      {
        "title": "Error Rate",
        "type": "timeseries",
        "targets": [{
          "expr": "sum(rate(http_requests_total{job=\"anihpj-django\",status=~\"5..\"}[5m])) by (endpoint)",
          "legendFormat": "{{endpoint}}"
        }]
      },
      {
        "title": "p99 Latency",
        "type": "timeseries",
        "targets": [{
          "expr": "histogram_quantile(0.99, sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{job=\"anihpj-django\"}[5m])) by (le, endpoint))",
          "legendFormat": "{{endpoint}}"
        }]
      }
    ]
  }
}

14.3 Dashboard Variables — Dynamic Filtering

Variables make dashboards reusable. Instead of creating 3 separate dashboards for dev/staging/prod, you create ONE dashboard with a $namespace variable:

VariableTypeQuery (Populates Dropdown)Usage in PromQL
$namespaceQuerylabel_values(kube_namespace_created, namespace){namespace="$namespace"}
$podQuerylabel_values(kube_pod_info{namespace="$namespace"}, pod){pod="$pod"}
$endpointQuerylabel_values(http_requests_total, endpoint){endpoint="$endpoint"}
$environmentCustomdev, staging, prod (comma-separated){env="$environment"}
💡 Variables are resolved at query time: When you change the dropdown from "dev" to "prod", Grafana substitutes $namespace in ALL panel queries simultaneously. One dashboard, infinite flexibility.

14.5 Grafana Alerting — Alternative to Alertmanager

AspectPrometheus AlertmanagerGrafana Alerting
Where alerts are definedYAML rule files in prometheus.ymlGrafana UI (visual builder)
Routing & groupingFull route tree, inhibition, silencingBasic routing to contact points
Best forInfrastructure + application alertsDashboard-level conditions, data from multiple sources
Setup complexityYAML-based, more flexibleUI-based, simpler to get started
PCA Exam?✅ Yes — heavily tested⚠️ Mentioned but not primary focus
🏆 Recommendation for anihpj

Use BOTH. Prometheus Alertmanager for infrastructure + application alerts (Chapter 11-12). Grafana Alerting for dashboard-level conditions (e.g., "no data for 10 minutes" or cross-data-source alerts). Alertmanager is more powerful; Grafana is easier to set up. For PCA exam: focus on Alertmanager — it's what's tested.

  • Variables magic: Create $namespace, $pod, $endpoint ONCE → one dashboard for ALL environments. Use Query type: label_values(metric, label) to auto-populate from Prometheus.
  • Panel type cheat sheet: Line graph = trends, Stat = current number, Gauge = % within range, Heatmap = distribution, Table = ranked list.
  • Grafana ≠ Prometheus replacement: Grafana doesn't store, scrape, or alert (natively). It ONLY visualizes. You ALWAYS need Prometheus behind it.
  • $__rate_interval: Use instead of hardcoding [5m] in Grafana panels. Auto-adjusts to dashboard time range for correct rate calculations.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 14 (Grafana)

Q1

What is Grafana's role in the Prometheus ecosystem?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Grafana is the visualization layer. It connects to Prometheus as a data source, sends PromQL queries, and renders the results as graphs, stats, heatmaps, and tables. Prometheus stores and queries; Grafana displays.

💡 EXPLANATION

Grafana ≠ Prometheus. Grafana has no time-series database, no scraper, no rules engine. It's PURELY a visualization tool. It sends HTTP requests to Prometheus API (like /api/v1/query_range), gets JSON back, and renders charts. This separation of concerns is why the stack is so popular: Prometheus handles data, Grafana handles display. You can also connect Grafana to MySQL, Elasticsearch, InfluxDB, CloudWatch — 100+ data sources — but Prometheus is the most common pairing.

  PROMETHEUS + GRAFANA — Division of Labor
  ════════════════════════════════════════
  
  PROMETHEUS (:9090)               GRAFANA (:3000)
  ──────────────────               ──────────────
  ✅ Scrapes /metrics              ❌ Does NOT scrape
  ✅ Stores time-series (TSDB)     ❌ No storage
  ✅ Evaluates alert rules          ❌ No rule evaluation
  ✅ PromQL query engine            ✅ SENDS PromQL to Prom
  ✅ /api/v1/query API              ✅ Renders JSON as charts
  ❌ No dashboards                  ✅ Beautiful dashboards
  ❌ No variables                   ✅ Dropdown variables
  
  Data Flow:
  ┌──────┐  PromQL query   ┌───────────┐  JSON result  ┌─────────┐
  │Grafana│──────────────▶│Prometheus │──────────────▶│ Grafana │
  │:3000  │               │:9090      │               │ renders │
  └───────┘               └───────────┘               │ chart   │
                                                      └─────────┘
  
  KEY: Prometheus = DATABASE. Grafana = UI. Both needed.
Q2

How does Grafana connect to Prometheus?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Via Data Sources (Configuration → Data Sources → Add Prometheus). Enter the Prometheus URL (http://prometheus:9090). Grafana sends HTTP API requests to Prometheus and renders the JSON response.

💡 EXPLANATION

Grafana uses Prometheus's HTTP API (port 9090). The most common endpoint: /api/v1/query_range for time-series graphs. Grafana manages the connection pool, authentication (basic auth, API key), and TLS. In Docker Compose, the URL is the service name: http://prometheus:9090. In K8s: http://prometheus-operated.monitoring.svc:9090. Grafana also supports Prometheus alerting data source to display alert states alongside metrics.

Q3

What are Grafana dashboard variables used for?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

To make dashboards dynamic and reusable. Variables like $namespace, $pod, or $endpoint create dropdowns at the top of the dashboard. Selecting a different value changes ALL panel queries — one dashboard for all environments.

💡 EXPLANATION

Variables are Grafana's superpower. Without variables: you need separate dashboards for dev, staging, prod, and every service. With $namespace variable: ONE dashboard, dropdown at top shows "dev | staging | prod". Select "prod" → every panel's PromQL automatically filters to {namespace="prod"}. Variable types: Query (populated from Prometheus label values), Custom (hardcoded list), Constant (hidden, always same value), Text box (free text). PCA loves the Query variable type.

Q4

Name 4 Grafana panel visualization types.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Time series (line graph), Stat (single large number), Gauge (percentage/range), Heatmap (distribution over time), Bar gauge, Table.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each panel type serves a purpose: Time series = trends over time (CPU usage over 24h). Stat = single current value ("Currently: 247 active jobs"). Gauge = value within a range (disk: 73% with thresholds at 80% yellow, 90% red). Heatmap = distribution patterns (request latency histogram — are most requests fast? where are the slow outliers?). Table = detailed list (slowest endpoints ranked by p99). PCA may ask which panel type fits a given scenario.

Q5

How does a Query variable populate its dropdown values?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

It runs a PromQL query against Prometheus to get label values. Example: label_values(http_requests_total, endpoint) returns all unique endpoint labels. These populate the dropdown.

💡 EXPLANATION

Query variables use Prometheus label values API. label_values(metric, label) is a special Grafana function that calls /api/v1/label/endpoint/values. Results: [/jobs, /api/skills, /admin]. These become dropdown options. Two variable types: label_values(metric, label) — get all values of that label from that metric. query_result(promql) — run any PromQL and use the result as options. Variables can be chained: $endpoint depends on $namespace (select namespace first, then endpoint options update).

Q6

What are the 5 essential dashboards for anihpj?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1. RED Dashboard (Rate, Errors, Duration per endpoint). 2. Database Dashboard (connections, queries, slow queries). 3. Cache Dashboard (hit rate, memory, evictions). 4. System Dashboard (CPU, memory, disk per node). 5. Business Dashboard (active listings, applications, skills).

💡 EXPLANATION

These 5 dashboards cover every layer of the anihpj stack. RED = application health (is the API working?). Database = PostgreSQL performance (slow queries kill everything). Cache = Redis effectiveness (are we actually caching?). System = infrastructure (is the server dying?). Business = KPIs (is the product succeeding?). Each dashboard has different audiences: RED → developers, DB/Cache → DBAs, System → DevOps/SRE, Business → PMs/leadership. PCA expects you to know what each dashboard type covers.

Q7

What's the difference between Grafana Alerting and Prometheus Alertmanager?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Prometheus Alertmanager is purpose-built for Prometheus alerts with full routing, grouping, inhibition, and silencing. Grafana Alerting is UI-based, simpler to configure, and works with multiple data sources. Use Alertmanager for production; Grafana for simple dashboard-level alerts.

💡 EXPLANATION

Grafana Alerting (introduced in Grafana 8) competes with Alertmanager but serves different use cases. Alertmanager advantages: inhibition (suppress app alerts when DC is down), complex route trees, HA-native, silences API. Grafana Alerting advantages: visual alert creation (no YAML), multi-data-source (alert on Prometheus + PostgreSQL + CloudWatch in one rule), built-in notification testing. For PCA: know that Alertmanager is the standard choice for Prometheus-native alerting. Grafana Alerting is for simpler, UI-based alerting across multiple data sources.

Q8

Can you display data from multiple Prometheus instances in one Grafana dashboard?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Yes. Add multiple Prometheus data sources. In each panel, select which data source to query. This is useful for comparing metrics across clusters or regions, and for viewing data from a Thanos/Cortex setup.

💡 EXPLANATION

Multi-source dashboards are common in multi-cluster setups. Add data sources: Prometheus-us-east, Prometheus-eu-west, Prometheus-ap-south. In a panel, use the "-- Mixed --" data source to query from multiple Prometheus instances and overlay the results. Example: CPU usage comparison across 3 regions — 3 lines, one graph. Also useful with Thanos: one data source for recent data (Prometheus), another for historical (Thanos querier). Grafana handles merging and display seamlessly.

Q9

What does the RED acronym stand for in RED Dashboard?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Rate (requests per second), Errors (failed requests), Duration (latency distribution). These 3 metrics give you a complete picture of any service endpoint's health.

💡 EXPLANATION

RED is the minimum viable monitoring for any service. Rate panel: rate(http_requests_total[$__rate_interval]) — is traffic normal? Errors panel: rate(http_requests_total{status_code=~"5.."}[$__rate_interval]) — are users seeing errors? Duration panel: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[$__rate_interval])) — is the service fast enough? These 3 panels on ONE dashboard tell you everything about service health at a glance. Tom Wilkie (Grafana Labs) created this pattern.

Q10

How do you use a variable in a Grafana panel's PromQL query?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Reference the variable with $variable_name. Example: if you define $endpoint, use it in PromQL as rate(http_requests_total{endpoint="$endpoint"}[5m]). Grafana substitutes the selected value at query time.

💡 EXPLANATION

Variable syntax in PromQL: $varname for single values, ${varname:text} for display override. Multi-value variables use =~: rate(http_requests_total{endpoint=~"$endpoint"}[5m]) — when user selects multiple endpoints, Grafana sends regex like "endpoint1|endpoint2|endpoint3". Grafana also provides built-in variables: $__interval (adjusted by dashboard time range), $__rate_interval (properly handles counter resets), $__from/$__to (time range bounds). Always use $__rate_interval instead of hardcoding [5m] — Grafana automatically picks the right window.

  • ❌ #1: Thinking Grafana REPLACES Prometheus. Grafana only visualizes — it doesn't store or scrape metrics.
  • ❌ #2: Hardcoding environment names in dashboards instead of using $namespace variables.
  • ❌ #3: Using Grafana Alerting for complex routing — Alertmanager is more powerful for production use.
  • ❌ #4: Forgetting to set units on panels — raw numbers like "0.0234" mean nothing without "seconds" or "percent".
  • ✅ Can you explain the relationship between Prometheus (storage) and Grafana (visualization)?
  • ✅ Do you know how variables make dashboards dynamic and reusable?
  • ✅ Can you name the 5 essential dashboards for any web application?

Chapter 15: Prometheus at Scale — HA, Federation, and Long-Term Storage 🔴 Advanced

From Single Server to Planet-Scale Monitoring

A single Prometheus server can handle ~10 million active time series comfortably. But what happens when you have multiple regions, need years of history, or want zero-downtime upgrades? This chapter covers High Availability (running multiple identical Prometheus servers), Federation (hierarchical global + regional topology), Long-Term Storage (Remote Write to Thanos/Cortex/VictoriaMetrics), and Agent Mode (lightweight scrape-only Prometheus for edge clusters). These are production essentials — not needed for PCA basics, but critical for real-world deployment.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2 hours🏢 Enterprise🌍 Multi-Region

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ How to run multiple Prometheus servers for High Availability
  • ✅ Federation — aggregating metrics across regions with /federate endpoint
  • ✅ Long-term storage with Remote Write (Thanos, Cortex, VictoriaMetrics)
  • ✅ Agent Mode — lightweight Prometheus for edge/IoT clusters

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Scaling Prometheus in Plain English

Think of Prometheus like a security camera system:

HA (High Availability): You install TWO identical cameras watching the same door. If one fails, the other still records. Both send footage to the same monitoring room (Alertmanager), which knows to only review one copy. Simple, no coordination needed.

Federation: You have buildings in New York, London, and Tokyo. Each building has its own camera system (regional Prometheus) recording EVERYTHING in full detail. The global security office (Global Prometheus) only watches a SUMMARY — "how many people entered each building?" — not every individual person. That's what /federate with match[] does.

Long-Term Storage: Camera footage fills up the hard drive in 15 days. You set up an automatic upload (Remote Write) to cloud storage (Thanos/S3) that keeps 2 years of history. When you need old footage, you query the cloud — not the local hard drive.

Agent Mode: You have tiny cameras in elevator shafts (edge/IoT). They don't have screens or hard drives — they just send footage to the central system. Agent Mode = scrape-only, no local storage, no queries.

15.1 High Availability — Running Multiple Prometheus Servers

🔄 HIGH AVAILABILITY — Two Prometheus, One Alertmanager
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     PROMETHEUS HIGH AVAILABILITY                           │
  │                                                                           │
  │  ┌───────────────────────┐          ┌───────────────────────┐            │
  │  │  Prometheus A         │          │  Prometheus B         │            │
  │  │  (primary)            │          │  (secondary)          │            │
  │  │                       │          │                       │            │
  │  │  Scrapes: anihpj:8000 │          │  Scrapes: anihpj:8000 │            │
  │  │           pg:9187     │          │           pg:9187     │            │
  │  │           redis:9121  │          │           redis:9121  │            │
  │  │           node:9100   │          │           node:9100   │            │
  │  │                       │          │                       │            │
  │  │  IDENTICAL config!    │          │  IDENTICAL config!    │            │
  │  └───────────┬───────────┘          └───────────┬───────────┘            │
  │              │                                   │                        │
  │              │         BOTH fire alerts          │                        │
  │              └───────────────┬───────────────────┘                        │
  │                              │                                            │
  │                              ▼                                            │
  │                    ┌──────────────────┐                                   │
  │                    │   ALERTMANAGER   │                                   │
  │                    │                  │                                   │
  │                    │  Deduplicates!   │  ← Same alert from A and B       │
  │                    │  "I already got  │     → keeps only ONE copy        │
  │                    │   this alert     │                                   │
  │                    │   from A"        │                                   │
  │                    └────────┬─────────┘                                   │
  │                             │                                             │
  │                             ▼                                             │
  │                    ┌──────────────────┐                                   │
  │                    │   PagerDuty      │  ← ONE notification, not two     │
  │                    └──────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                           │
  │  KEY PRINCIPLES:                                                          │
  │  • No coordination between A and B — they're completely independent      │
  │  • Both scrape the SAME targets with the SAME scrape_interval            │
  │  • external_labels differentiate: {prometheus="A"} vs {prometheus="B"}   │
  │  • Alertmanager deduplication is label-based (identical alerts → one)    │
  │  • Grafana can query BOTH (use one as primary, other as fallback)         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
prometheus.yml — external_labels for HA Deduplication
global:
  external_labels:
    prometheus: 'prometheus-a'    # ← Identifies this instance
    replica: 'a'                  # ← Used by Alertmanager for dedup
    region: 'us-east-1'

# Alertmanager deduplicates alerts where ALL labels match.
# external_labels make alerts from A and B distinguishable.
# Alertmanager is smart enough to group them as duplicates.

15.2 Federation — Hierarchical Prometheus

🌍 FEDERATION TOPOLOGY — Global + Regional Prometheus
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    PROMETHEUS FEDERATION TOPOLOGY                          │
  │                                                                           │
  │                    ┌─────────────────────────┐                            │
  │                    │     GLOBAL PROMETHEUS    │                            │
  │                    │  (aggregates regional)   │                            │
  │                    │                          │                            │
  │                    │  Scrapes /federate from  │                            │
  │                    │  each regional instance  │                            │
  │                    │  every 30s (less often)  │                            │
  │                    └────────────┬────────────┘                            │
  │                                 │                                         │
  │            ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐                    │
  │            │                    │                    │                    │
  │            ▼                    ▼                    ▼                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐          │
  │  │  us-east-1       │ │  eu-west-1       │ │  ap-southeast-1  │          │
  │  │  PROMETHEUS      │ │  PROMETHEUS      │ │  PROMETHEUS      │          │
  │  │  (Regional)      │ │  (Regional)      │ │  (Regional)      │          │
  │  │                  │ │                  │ │                  │          │
  │  │  Full detail:    │ │  Full detail:    │ │  Full detail:    │          │
  │  │  • anihpj-us pod │ │  • anihpj-eu pod │ │  • anihpj-apac   │          │
  │  │  • node exporter │ │  • node exporter │ │  • node exporter │          │
  │  │  • pg exporter   │ │  • pg exporter   │ │  • pg exporter   │          │
  │  │  • redis exporter│ │  • redis exporter│ │  • redis exporter│          │
  │  └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘          │
  │                                                                           │
  │  FEDERATION RULES:                                                        │
  │  • Regional: scrape ALL targets (15s interval) — full detail locally     │
  │  • Global: scrape /federate (30s interval) — aggregated metrics only     │
  │  • match[]: filter which metrics to pull up to global                     │
  │    match[]: '{job="anihpj-django"}' — only anihpj metrics, not node      │
  │  • Why? Global doesn't need per-pod CPU of every region. Just aggregates. │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Global Prometheus — Scraping Regional /federate Endpoints
# Global Prometheus prometheus.yml
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'federate-us-east'
    honor_labels: true              # ← Keep original labels
    metrics_path: '/federate'       # ← NOT /metrics!
    params:
      'match[]':                     # ← Only pull these metrics
        - '{job="anihpj-django"}'
        - '{__name__=~"job:.*"}'    # ← Recording rules (aggregated)
    static_configs:
      - targets:
          - 'prometheus-us-east.anihpj.com:9090'
          - 'prometheus-eu-west.anihpj.com:9090'

15.3 Long-Term Storage — Beyond 15 Days

prometheus.yml — Remote Write Configuration for Thanos
remote_write:
  - url: "http://thanos-receive:19291/api/v1/receive"
    remote_timeout: 30s
    queue_config:
      capacity: 2500          # ← Samples per shard buffer
      max_shards: 200         # ← Max parallel senders
      min_shards: 1           # ← Min parallel senders
      max_samples_per_send: 500  # ← Batch size per request
      batch_send_deadline: 5s
      min_backoff: 30ms       # ← Retry backoff (min)
      max_backoff: 100ms      # ← Retry backoff (max)
    write_relabel_configs:    # ← Filter what to send
      - source_labels: [__name__]
        regex: 'up|scrape_.*' # ← DON'T send these
        action: drop

# PCA NOTE: remote_write can overwhelm receivers if misconfigured.
# Key tuning: max_shards (parallelism) + capacity (buffer size).
SolutionArchitectureBest ForKey Feature
ThanosSidecar + Store + Querier + CompactorGlobal query view + S3 storageDownsampling (raw → 5m → 1h)
CortexMicroservices (distributor, ingester, querier)Multi-tenant SaaS platformsHorizontal scaling, tenant isolation
VictoriaMetricsSingle binary (or cluster mode)Simple setup, low resourceDrop-in Prometheus replacement
Grafana MimirMicroservices (Cortex fork)Large-scale Grafana Cloud1 billion+ active series

15.4 Agent Mode — Prometheus Without Storage or Querying

🤖 AGENT MODE — Lightweight Scrape-Only Prometheus
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                           │
  │   STANDARD PROMETHEUS                    AGENT MODE PROMETHEUS             │
  │   ┌───────────────────────┐            ┌───────────────────────┐          │
  │   │  Scrape → TSDB → Query│            │  Scrape → Remote Write│          │
  │   │  (full server)        │            │  (forward only)       │          │
  │   │                       │            │                       │          │
  │   │  • Stores data locally│            │  • NO local storage   │          │
  │   │  • Answers queries    │            │  • NO query answering │          │
  │   │  • Evaluates rules    │            │  • NO rule evaluation │          │
  │   │  • Disk: 10-100+ GB  │            │  • Disk: minimal      │          │
  │   │  • RAM: 2-8 GB       │            │  • RAM: ~500 MB       │          │
  │   └───────────────────────┘            └───────────┬───────────┘          │
  │                                                    │                      │
  │   USE CASE — anihpj multi-region edge:              │                      │
  │   ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌────┴─────────────┐       │
  │   │ Edge Cluster US  │  │ Edge Cluster EU  │  │ Edge Cluster     │       │
  │   │ Agent Prometheus │  │ Agent Prometheus │  │ APAC             │       │
  │   │ (scrape only)    │  │ (scrape only)    │  │ Agent Prometheus │       │
  │   └────────┬─────────┘  └────────┬─────────┘  └────────┬─────────┘       │
  │            │                     │                     │                  │
  │            └─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┘                  │
  │                                  │                                        │
  │                                  ▼                                        │
  │                     ┌────────────────────────┐                            │
  │                     │ Central Prometheus/     │                            │
  │                     │ Thanos (full-featured)  │                            │
  │                     │ Stores + Queries + Rules│                            │
  │                     └────────────────────────┘                            │
  │                                                                           │
  │   START AGENT MODE: prometheus --agent --config.file=prometheus.yml       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 Agent Mode (v2.32+, stable v2.40+): Add --agent flag. Prometheus becomes a forward-only scraper — scrapes targets → remote writes to central instance. No local TSDB means drastically lower disk I/O and RAM. Perfect for edge clusters, IoT devices, and environments where you don't need local query capability.
PCA Exam: Scaling Prometheus

The PCA exam may touch on these advanced concepts: (1) What does external_labels do? (distinguish Prometheus instances in HA/federation). (2) What's the difference between /metrics and /federate? (/metrics = all data, /federate = filtered by match[]). (3) What is Remote Write? (send data to external storage for long-term retention). (4) What's Agent Mode? (scrape-only, no local TSDB). These are "beyond basic" but increasingly common on the exam.

🏆 Scaling Decision Guide for anihpj

Single region, single cluster: One Prometheus with 15-day retention is enough. Multi-region: Regional Prometheus in each region + Global Prometheus via /federate for cross-region views. Need >15 days of history: Add Thanos (or VictoriaMetrics) with Remote Write for S3-backed long-term storage. Edge/IoT clusters: Agent Mode Prometheus → remote write to central Thanos. HA everywhere: Always run 2+ Prometheus replicas per region — the cost is minimal compared to missing alerts during an outage.

15.5 Capacity Planning — How Big Should My Prometheus Be?

Prometheus Sizing — The Key Formulas
# PROMETHEUS CAPACITY PLANNING CHEAT SHEET
# ═══════════════════════════════════════

# 1. ACTIVE TIME SERIES (the primary capacity metric)
#    Each unique label combination = 1 active time series
#    Formula: series = scrape_targets × metrics_per_target × unique_label_combos
#    Example: 20 pods × 500 metrics × avg 3 label combos = 30,000 series

# 2. MEMORY REQUIREMENT
#    ~2-3 KB per active time series (including overhead)
#    1M series ≈ 2-3 GB RAM
#    10M series ≈ 20-30 GB RAM (practical limit for single Prometheus)

# 3. DISK REQUIREMENT
#    ~1-2 bytes per sample after compression
#    Daily: samples = active_series × (86400 / scrape_interval)
#    Example: 100K series × 86400/15 = 576M samples/day ≈ 1 GB/day
#    15-day retention: ~15 GB + 20% overhead = ~18 GB

# 4. CPU REQUIREMENT
#    Scraping: ~1 core per 100K targets at 15s interval
#    Rule evaluation: depends on rule complexity
#    Query load: ~1 core per 10 concurrent heavy queries

# 5. FLAGS TO TUNE
#    --storage.tsdb.retention.time=15d    (or .size=50GB)
#    --storage.tsdb.max-block-duration=2h
#    --query.max-samples=50000000         (increase for large queries)
#    --query.timeout=2m                   (increase for heavy dashboards)
EnvironmentActive SeriesRAMDisk (15d)CPU
Dev laptop5K-20K256MB-512MB500MB-2GB0.5 core
Small prod (10 nodes)100K-500K1-2GB10-20GB1-2 cores
Medium prod (50 nodes)500K-2M2-8GB50-100GB2-4 cores
Large prod (200 nodes)2M-10M8-30GB100-500GB4-8 cores
Enterprise (federated)10M+30GB+500GB+8+ cores
  • HA = simple duplication: Two identical Prometheus servers, zero coordination. Alertmanager handles dedup. No consensus, no leader election — refreshingly simple.
  • /federate vs /metrics: /metrics = EVERYTHING. /federate?match[] = SELECTIVE. Use match[] to avoid crushing the global Prometheus with raw per-pod data.
  • Remote Write tuning: capacity=2500 (buffer), max_shards=200 (parallel), max_samples_per_send=500 (batch). Increase shards if samples_pending is high.
  • Agent Mode = scrape-only: No TSDB, no query, no rules. ~200MB RAM vs ~2GB full Prometheus. Just scrape + forward via remote_write.

📝 PCA Practice Questions — Chapter 15 (At Scale)

Q1

How does Prometheus achieve High Availability?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Run two (or more) identical Prometheus servers scraping the same targets. They are independent — no coordination. Alertmanager deduplicates identical alerts. Use external_labels to distinguish instances.

💡 EXPLANATION

Prometheus HA is refreshingly simple: just run two copies. No leader election, no consensus, no shared storage. Each Prometheus independently scrapes the same targets and stores its own TSDB. Both evaluate the same rules — both fire the same alerts. Alertmanager deduplicates (keeps one). Pros: dead simple, no SPOF. Cons: double the scrape load on targets, no guarantee both have identical data (slight timing differences). This is different from traditional DB replication — Prometheus chose simplicity over perfect consistency.

  PROMETHEUS HIGH AVAILABILITY (HA)
  ════════════════════════════════
  
  ┌─────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────┐
  │ Prometheus A     │     │ Prometheus B     │
  │ (identical)      │     │ (identical)      │
  │                  │     │                  │
  │ Scrapes:         │     │ Scrapes:         │
  │  • anihpj:8000   │     │  • anihpj:8000   │
  │  • node:9100     │     │  • node:9100     │
  │  • pg:9187       │     │  • pg:9187       │
  │                  │     │                  │
  │ TSDB: 15d        │     │ TSDB: 15d        │
  │ external_labels: │     │ external_labels: │
  │  replica: A      │     │  replica: B      │
  └────────┬─────────┘     └────────┬─────────┘
           │                        │
           │  Both fire alerts      │
           └───────────┬────────────┘
                       ▼
              ┌─────────────────┐
              │  Alertmanager   │
              │                 │
              │  "DatabaseDown"  │
              │  from A + B      │
              │  → DEDUP → 1    │
              │    notification  │
              └────────┬────────┘
                       ▼
                  PagerDuty
              (ONE call, not two)
  
  KEY: No coordination. No leader. Just 2 copies + dedup.
Q2

What is the /federate endpoint?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A special Prometheus endpoint that selectively exposes time series for a parent/global Prometheus to scrape. Use match[] parameters to filter which metrics are exposed (e.g., only aggregated recording rules, not raw node metrics).

💡 EXPLANATION

Federation is the hierarchical model: regional Prometheus scrapes everything (raw CPU, HTTP metrics). Global Prometheus scrapes only AGGREGATED metrics from regionals via /federate?match[]={__name__=~"job:.*"}. This means: regional stores 30 days of raw data; global stores 90 days of aggregated data. match[] uses the same label matching syntax as PromQL selectors. The global Prometheus sees each regional as a scrape target with honor_labels: true to preserve original labels. PCA may ask which endpoint to use for federation.

Q3

What is Remote Write?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A Prometheus feature that sends scraped metrics to an external storage system (Thanos, Cortex, VictoriaMetrics) in real-time. Configures via remote_write: in prometheus.yml. Enables long-term retention beyond the local 15-day TSDB.

💡 EXPLANATION

Remote Write solves Prometheus's single-biggest limitation: local storage is capped (15 days typical, ~10M active series). With Remote Write, every sample scraped is also written to: Thanos (stores in S3/GCS, global query view), Cortex (multi-tenant SaaS-scale), VictoriaMetrics (single binary, very efficient), or Mimir (Grafana Labs' massive-scale solution). Configure: remote_write: - url: "http://thanos-receive:19291/api/v1/write". Use write_relabel_configs to filter what gets sent (save bandwidth).

Q4

What is Agent Mode?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A lightweight Prometheus mode (v2.32+) that only scrapes targets and remote-writes data — no local TSDB, no querying, no rule evaluation. Launched with --agent flag. Much lower resource usage. Ideal for edge clusters.

💡 EXPLANATION

Agent Mode is Prometheus stripped to its essentials. Removes: TSDB (no disk I/O), query engine (no Grafana queries against it), rule evaluation (no CPU for alerts/recording). Keeps: service discovery, scraping, remote_write. Memory drops from ~2GB to ~200MB. CPU drops ~80%. Perfect for: edge/IoT clusters with limited hardware, environments where you just need to ship metrics to a central Prometheus, and reducing costs in large-scale deployments. Requires remote_write to be configured — that's the only place data goes.

Q5

How does Alertmanager handle duplicate alerts in HA?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Alertmanager deduplicates alerts with identical labels. When Prometheus A and B both fire the same alert, Alertmanager keeps only one copy. external_labels are stripped during dedup comparison.

💡 EXPLANATION

The dedup logic: Alertmanager compares alerts by their label set EXCLUDING external_labels (like prometheus="prod-a" vs prometheus="prod-b"). If alertname, severity, instance, etc. all match → it's a duplicate → keep one, note both sources. This is why external_labels must NOT be the same between HA instances. If prometheus="prod-a" and prometheus="prod-b" are stripped during comparison, the remaining labels match → dedup works. Without external_labels, you can't tell which instance scraped what when debugging.

Q6

What do external_labels do in prometheus.yml?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

They add identifying labels to all metrics scraped by this Prometheus instance. Used in HA to distinguish which instance scraped what, and in federation to track data origin across regions.

💡 EXPLANATION

external_labels are set globally in prometheus.yml: global: external_labels: {region: us-east, cluster: prod, replica: A}. Every metric scraped gets these labels appended. Uses: (1) HA — distinguish which Prometheus scraped a metric (replica=A or B). (2) Federation — global Prometheus knows data came from "us-east" regional. (3) Multi-tenancy — add tenant_id for isolation. external_labels are NOT scraped from targets — they're added by Prometheus itself. Alertmanager strips them during dedup to avoid treating identical alerts as different.

Q7

Name three long-term storage solutions compatible with Prometheus.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Thanos (S3 storage, global query view, downsampling), Cortex (microservices, multi-tenant), VictoriaMetrics (single binary, low resource), Grafana Mimir (Cortex fork, massive scale). All integrate via Remote Write.

💡 EXPLANATION

Each solution has its niche: Thanos — S3/GCS for cheap infinite storage, global query view across clusters, downsampling (5m/1h resolution for old data). Cortex — microservices architecture, multi-tenant (SaaS providers love this), horizontal scaling per component. VictoriaMetrics — single Go binary, incredibly efficient, drop-in Prometheus replacement for smaller deployments. Mimir — Grafana Labs' Cortex fork, battle-tested at 1B+ active series scale. PCA will likely ask to NAME a few, not deeply compare them.

Q8

What's the difference between /metrics and /federate?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

/metrics exposes ALL metrics scraped by this Prometheus. /federate exposes only metrics matching match[] parameters — used to selectively expose aggregated metrics to a parent Prometheus, filtering out raw per-pod metrics.

💡 EXPLANATION

The difference is filtering. /metrics returns EVERYTHING in the TSDB — if the regional Prometheus has 5M time series, /metrics dumps all 5M (this would crush a global Prometheus). /federate?match[]={__name__=~"job:.*"} returns only metrics whose names match — maybe 500 time series. This is the key architectural pattern: regional stores raw data (short retention), global stores aggregated data (long retention). match[] syntax: {job="anihpj"} (exact), {__name__=~"node_.*"} (regex), {severity="critical"} (label filter).

Q9

What does `queue_config` control in remote_write?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Controls the write buffer and parallelism. capacity = samples per shard buffer, max_shards = max parallel senders, max_samples_per_send = batch size. Tune these to avoid overwhelming the receiver or losing data during bursts.

💡 EXPLANATION

The queue_config is the safety valve for Remote Write. capacity (default 2500): how many samples can be queued per shard before dropping. max_shards (default 200): how many parallel HTTP connections to the remote storage. max_samples_per_send (default 500): samples per HTTP batch. Tuning: if prometheus_remote_storage_samples_pending is high → increase max_shards. If remote storage is slow → increase capacity (buffer more). If network is unreliable → increase max_samples_per_send (fewer, bigger batches). PCA may ask about these parameters.

Q10

Can you query data from Agent Mode Prometheus?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

No. Agent Mode has no local TSDB and no query API. It only scrapes targets and remote-writes data. You must query the central Prometheus/Thanos instance that receives the remote-written data.

💡 EXPLANATION

Agent Mode is scrape-then-forward, nothing more. No :9090 query UI, no PromQL API, no /api/v1/query endpoint. If you try: "connection refused" or the agent didn't even bind that port. All data flows through remote_write to a central system. This central system (could be another full Prometheus, Thanos Receive, Cortex, or VictoriaMetrics) handles ALL querying. Agent Mode Prometheus is a pure data collector. This is similar to Telegraf or fluent-bit — collect and forward, don't query locally.

  • ❌ Expecting HA Prometheus instances to coordinate — they're completely independent, only Alertmanager dedups.
  • ❌ Using /federate without match[] — exposes ALL metrics, overwhelming the global Prometheus.
  • ❌ Forgetting to configure write_relabel_configs in remote_write — sends unnecessary metrics to long-term storage.
  • ❌ Running Agent Mode without remote_write — the scraped data has nowhere to go!
  • ✅ Can you explain how HA Prometheus works with Alertmanager deduplication?
  • ✅ Do you know the difference between /metrics and /federate endpoints?
  • ✅ Can you name the key Remote Write tuning parameters (capacity, max_shards)?

Chapter 16: Prometheus on Kubernetes — Cloud-Native Monitoring 🔴 Advanced

Prometheus Was Born for Kubernetes

Prometheus and Kubernetes share DNA — both were created around the same time with the same philosophy: labels, dynamic discovery, and declarative configuration. The Prometheus Operator makes Prometheus truly Kubernetes-native with Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs) that let you define monitoring as code. The kube-prometheus-stack gives you a complete monitoring platform in one Helm install: Prometheus + Grafana + Alertmanager + Node Exporter + Kube State Metrics. This chapter covers the Operator model, the complete stack, and how to monitor Kubernetes itself — the control plane, etcd, CoreDNS, and every pod.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2 hours☸️ K8s⚡ Operator

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ How the Prometheus Operator uses CRDs (Prometheus, ServiceMonitor, PodMonitor)
  • ✅ Installing kube-prometheus-stack with one Helm command
  • ✅ Monitoring K8s itself — kube-state-metrics, kubelet, cAdvisor, API server, etcd

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — Prometheus on Kubernetes in Plain English

Kubernetes is a city. Prometheus is the city-wide surveillance system. In a city, you don't manually visit every building to check if it's on fire — you have fire alarms, security cameras, and emergency dispatch. Prometheus on K8s works the same way:

1. The Operator = City Hall. You file a permit (a YAML file) saying "I want to monitor all buildings with the 'restaurant' sign." City Hall (the Operator) reads your permit, dispatches inspectors (configures Prometheus), and they start visiting those buildings automatically. New restaurant opens? Inspector is assigned automatically. Restaurant closes? Inspector stops visiting. No manual paperwork needed.

2. ServiceMonitor = The permit form. "Monitor pods with label app: anihpj on port metrics:8000." That's all you write. The Operator does everything else: finds the pods, gets their IPs, configures Prometheus, and reloads it. You never touch prometheus.yml.

3. kube-state-metrics = Census data. "How many deployments are in the 'anihpj' namespace? How many pods are Running vs Pending? How many nodes are Ready?" This is Kubernetes object state, not hardware metrics.

4. cAdvisor = Utility meters. Every building (node) has utility meters measuring electricity (CPU), water (memory), and gas (network). cAdvisor is built into every kubelet — you don't install it separately. It reports per-container resource usage.

The big picture: Operator manages Prometheus → ServiceMonitors define WHAT to scrape → kube-state-metrics tells you ABOUT K8s objects → cAdvisor tells you HOW MUCH resources they use → together, complete K8s visibility.

🧠 Decision: What K8s Component Do I Need?
Which component gives me the data I need?
"How many pods are running in namespace X?" or "What's the desired vs available replicas?"
kube-state-metrics — Object state. Deployments, pods, nodes, PVs, jobs. Not resource usage!
"How much CPU/memory/network is pod X using?"
cAdvisor (via kubelet) — Container resource metrics. Built into every kubelet at :10250/metrics/cadvisor.
"Is the API server slow? Are etcd operations timing out?"
K8s Control Plane — API server (:6443/metrics), etcd (:2379/metrics), scheduler, controller-manager. Critical for cluster health.
"How do I auto-discover pods without manual config?"
Prometheus Operator + ServiceMonitor — CRDs that auto-generate scrape configs. kubectl apply → auto-discovered.

16.1 Prometheus Operator — Kubernetes-Native Prometheus

☸️ PROMETHEUS OPERATOR — CRD Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              PROMETHEUS OPERATOR — KUBERNETES NATIVE                       │
  │                                                                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                    PROMETHEUS OPERATOR                              │   │
  │  │  (Controller — watches CRDs, reconciles desired vs actual state)   │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                               │                                          │
  │            ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐                       │
  │            │                  │                  │                       │
  │            ▼                  ▼                  ▼                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐             │
  │  │  Prometheus CRD  │ │ServiceMonitor│ │   PodMonitor     │             │
  │  │                  │ │    CRD       │ │     CRD          │             │
  │  │ "I want a        │ │              │ │                  │             │
